# Problem research: 48 problems across 16 domains

Generated from the research run that preceded product selection. Each entry answers the seven investigation questions from the brief. Figures were gathered by research agents using web search; several agents could not reach some primary hosts (WHO, CDC, FTC, ILO) and say so in the entry. These entries were **not** independently fact-checked; treat figures as leads to verify, not citations.

## 1. Recalled, substandard and falsified medicine batches keep being dispensed because regulator alerts never reach the pharmacy counter

*Domain:* healthcare-access / medication safety / substandard and falsified medicines · *Researcher confidence:* 0.65

**Who experiences it.** Patients buying medicines from retail pharmacies, drug shops and patent-medicine vendors in low- and middle-income countries, above all children (the 2022-2025 diethylene-glycol cough-syrup deaths were almost all under-fives in The Gambia, Indonesia, Uzbekistan and Madhya Pradesh, India). Dispensers and procurement staff in India (CDSCO publishes 50-170 'Not of Standard Quality' batches every month as PDFs; state regulators publish more), Nigeria (NAFDAC numbered public alerts), Kenya (171 recalls 2016-2025 announced via website and social media), Rwanda, Ghana, Tanzania, Morocco (2025: recalled antidepressant Ludiomil still on shelves because the alert was only posted on the agency website). Cross-border importers: Rwanda recalled a Kenyan-made product; a COMESA 2026 notice warned recalled batches had likely reached other member states; India-made syrups killed children in three other countries. Also hospital pharmacies and faith-based drug supply organisations (MEDS Kenya, JMS Uganda, CHAG Ghana) that currently do their own manual recall checks.

**Scale and severity.** WHO (2017, still its current fact-sheet figure) estimates 1 in 10 medical products in LMICs is substandard or falsified (10.5% of ~48,000 samples from 88 countries) and that LMICs spend US$30.5 billion a year on them. The most recent pooled meta-analysis (Ozawa et al., Am J Trop Med Hyg 2022: 130 studies, 95,520 samples, literature to Feb 2020) finds 12.4% (95% CI 10.2-14.6%) of essential-medicine samples in LMICs fail at least one quality test, ~18.9% in Africa. Modelled mortality (2017, high uncertainty): 72,000-169,000 child pneumonia deaths/yr from substandard/falsified antibiotics (Univ. of Edinburgh) and ~116,000 (64,000-158,000) excess malaria deaths/yr in sub-Saharan Africa (LSHTM). Confirmed recent events: >300 children died of acute kidney injury in 2022 from DEG/EG-contaminated syrups in The Gambia, Indonesia and Uzbekistan (WHO, Jan 2023); at least 20 children under 5 died in Madhya Pradesh in Sept-Oct 2025 from Coldrif syrup containing DEG reported at ~500x the permissible limit; WHO has issued 9 cough-syrup warnings in 3 years, 5 for Indian-made products. WHO's Global Surveillance and Monitoring System has logged >10,000 suspect products since 2013 but issued only 5 public Medical Product Alerts in 2024 and 7 in 2025. India: CDSCO flagged 48 NSQ batches in Aug 2024, 167 in Dec 2025 and 111 in May 2026, yet India still has no binding recall law (2012 draft recall/rapid-alert guideline never became law). Kenya PPB: 171 product recalls 2016-2025. The scale of SF medicines is well evidenced; the share of recalled units actually recovered from LMIC retail is not measured anywhere I could find, which is itself part of the problem.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Regulators publish alerts as PDFs or web posts on their own sites, one jurisdiction at a time, with no structured batch-level data, no push channel to dispensers and no cross-border propagation; a pharmacist has to visit each site to know (Morocco 2025 case; CDSCO's monthly PDF lists; Kenya PPB website/social media). (2) Manufacturer-driven authentication (mPedigree, Sproxil scratch codes, GS1 serialisation) only covers brands that opt in, verifies the pack not the contents, and says nothing about a genuine product later declared NSQ or recalled. (3) USP's Medicines Quality Database is public but its analysed data cover 2003-2013 in 17 countries. (4) Existing recall aggregators (openFDA enforcement API, DeepRecall, Apify scrapers) are US/HIC-centric; none covers NAFDAC, CDSCO, Ghana FDA or Kenya PPB. (5) WHO GSMS reports are not public and alerts are a tiny subset. (6) India has no mandatory recall law, so NSQ publication is the only lever and it depends on dispensers reading it. (7) Most LMIC pharmacies lack batch-level inventory records, so even an informed pharmacist cannot easily check shelves. (8) Recall-effectiveness data (what fraction of a distributed batch is recovered) is not collected, so nobody can see the failure.

**What people do today.** Pharmacists rely on wholesaler phone calls, WhatsApp groups, newspaper reports and social media; hospital and faith-based supply organisations run their own quality testing and manual batch checks; procurement agencies screen suppliers rather than batches; patients rely on brand trust, price and the appearance of the pack; regulators use press releases and ask the public to call a hotline; after mass-casualty events, governments ban products country-wide. Many dispensers simply do nothing because they never learn of the alert.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, cross-regulator, batch-level recall and NSQ alert registry with a closed loop to dispensers. Ingest and normalise every public alert source into one schema (INN, brand, strength, form, manufacturer, batch/lot, manufacture/expiry dates, defect class, regulator, action, verbatim source link): CDSCO national and state NSQ lists (PDF), NAFDAC, Ghana FDA, Kenya PPB, Rwanda FDA, Tanzania TMDA, SAHPRA, WHO Medical Product Alerts, openFDA enforcement API, MHRA alerts (gov.uk), EMA, Health Canada, TGA. On top: (a) instant batch lookup by typing or by scanning the pack barcode with a phone browser camera; (b) watchlists and push subscriptions (email/SMS/WhatsApp) by molecule, manufacturer, jurisdiction or a pharmacy's uploaded stock list; (c) automatic cross-border matching: the same manufacturer+batch recalled in one country is surfaced to subscribers in countries that import it; (d) a 'stock check' workflow where each pharmacy, hospital or warehouse acknowledges an alert and records units found/quarantined, producing the first real recall-effectiveness data for regulators; (e) a public API so pharmacy POS/inventory vendors (Maisha Meds, Field/Shelf Life, mPharma, e-pharmacies) can screen stock automatically; (f) registration-status cross-check against public registers (NAFDAC Greenbook, CDSCO SUGAM, Kenya PPB retention register). What is novel: batch-level, multi-jurisdiction, structured and subscribable, with a measured closed loop, independent of manufacturers. It is fully useful without AI; an LLM is optional only for first-pass PDF extraction behind a human verification queue.

**Works across countries?.** Yes. Every regulator already publishes some alert stream, so the schema and workflow transfer; only the ingestion adapters differ. It is most valuable where recall law is weak or absent (India, much of Africa), but recall effectiveness is poor in high-income countries too, and the cross-border function specifically targets the India-to-Africa/Asia export channel where recent deaths occurred. Language localisation and SMS/WhatsApp delivery are needed for low-connectivity settings.

**Sustainable business model.** Core data stays open (CC-BY) and public lookup plus basic alerts are free for individual pharmacies and the public. Paid tiers: multi-site hospital groups and chains (watchlists, audit logs for accreditation), wholesalers and distributors (bulk batch screening via API), procurement agencies and NGOs (Global Fund principal recipients, MEDS, JMS, CHAG, MSF, UNICEF SD) for supplier/batch screening, pharmacy POS and e-pharmacy vendors (API licensing), and regulators (hosted alert distribution and effectiveness dashboards as SaaS). Manufacturers may buy recall-effectiveness reporting but must have no influence on content. Early funding from USP, Gates Foundation, WHO Member State Mechanism, Fleming Fund-type donors. Social value is protected because the alert data itself is never paywalled.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, database and auth by a small team. Real public data exists now: openFDA enforcement API (structured JSON, no key), FDA/MHRA/Health Canada/TGA web lists, WHO Medical Product Alerts list, CDSCO monthly NSQ PDFs and state alerts, NAFDAC numbered public alerts, Kenya PPB and Rwanda FDA safety-alert pages, Ghana FDA recall page, NAFDAC Greenbook and CDSCO SUGAM registers. Hard parts: brittle PDF/HTML scraping with per-regulator formats; batch-number normalisation; no APIs from LMIC regulators; keeping verbatim source citations to manage liability; most LMIC packs lack batch-encoding 2D barcodes so batch entry is often manual; cold-start adoption by pharmacies; SMS/WhatsApp delivery cost. No custom hardware, no regulatory approval and no logistics network required; a 3-regulator MVP (openFDA + CDSCO + NAFDAC) with lookup, subscriptions and stock-check acknowledgement is realistic in weeks.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median hours from regulator publication to notification of subscribed dispensers; share of subscribed pharmacies acknowledging and completing a stock check within 72 hours; units quarantined or returned per alert; recall-effectiveness rate (fraction of distributed batch recovered) reported back to regulators; audited post-recall dispensing rate in sample pharmacies before vs after enrolment; number of cross-border matches surfaced that the importing regulator had not yet published; number of jurisdictions and alerts normalised per month; time-to-detection of repeat offenders (manufacturers with multiple NSQ batches).

## 2. When a medicine is out of stock, patients and clinicians have no way to learn where it is available or what an acceptable substitute is

*Domain:* healthcare-access / drug stockouts and shortages · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** In LMICs: patients at public primary-care facilities (lowest-priced generics available in only ~38% of public facilities in WHO Africa region, pooled 2025 analysis), people on chronic therapy for HIV, TB, hypertension and diabetes (Kenya, Apr-May 2025: over 40% of facilities reported stockouts of critical medicines, cotrimoxazole missing in about a third, nearly two-thirds of Kenyans paid out of pocket for essential medicines), women needing SRH commodities in Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda and Zambia (3-12 stockout days per month per facility), and the poorest households who exit the private market when prices rise during public stockouts (Uganda). In high-income countries: people with ADHD (US survey of 10,936: 38% had trouble filling prescriptions, 21% still disrupted; patients report calling 3-16 and up to 20 pharmacies a month), diabetes/obesity (GLP-1s), cancer and ICU patients, and community pharmacists in 27 EU/EFTA countries spending ~11 hours a week hunting stock and substitutes.

**Scale and severity.** LMIC availability: a Lancet Global Health 2025 secondary analysis of 71 WHO/HAI surveys in 54 countries found public-sector availability of lowest-priced generics ranging from 37.6% (Africa) to 68.4% (Europe), and patients paying 3.0-11.5x international reference prices for generics. Kenya 2025 (NSDCC survey via Eastleigh Voice; single news source, methodology unverified): >40% of facilities with stockouts, ~2/3 of citizens paying OOP, KEMSA fill rate 55% and 24-day delivery times. Uganda (2017 working paper): private antimalarial prices rise 36% during public stockouts and low-income buyers drop out. USA: ASHP recorded 323 active shortages in Q1 2024, the most since tracking began in 2001; 270 in March 2025; 41% of active shortages have lasted >2 years; a secondary source reports 227 in Q2 2026 after three quarterly rises. Europe: PGEU 2025 report, 96% of responding countries report shortages, 89% report treatment interruptions, 70% say the situation has stagnated at an unacceptably high level; pharmacists spent ~10 hours/week in 2023 and ~11 in 2024 managing shortages (up from 6.3 in 2020). ADHD stimulant shortage (ADDitude, late 2023): 38% of 10,936 respondents could not fill, 21% ongoing disruption; clinicians report calling 20 pharmacies to place one prescription. Treatment interruptions from stockouts drive resistance (HIV, TB), uncontrolled hypertension (only 21% of 1.28 billion hypertensives are controlled, WHO 2023) and catastrophic spending, but a global death toll attributable to stockouts is not quantified; I did not inflate it.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Official shortage lists (FDA/openFDA, ASHP, EMA catalogue, BfArM, national EU registers) say what is short nationally, never where stock physically is, and most depend on voluntary manufacturer reports. Pharmacy inventory is proprietary: chains do not share it, so patients and prescribers phone around. The few locators either require pharmacy opt-in inventory feeds (ApoApp, Austria, ~1,000 of 1,400 pharmacies after years), charge patients for staff to phone pharmacies (Medfinder, US), or are narrow/local (InStockRx New Jersey, ScriptChecker UK). African pharmacy-tech players (Field/Shelf Life, Maisha Meds, mPharma) sell inventory and supply tools to pharmacies, not availability to patients; Medsaf shut in March 2024. Public-sector SMS reporting (Uganda mTrac: ~5,000 facilities, ACT stockouts fell 25.2% to 13.8% by 2012) feeds the ministry, not the patient at the counter, and such data are not public. Substitution knowledge (alternate strength, salt, formulation, therapeutic equivalent) sits in pharmacists' heads and in scattered references, so each switch needs a prescriber to rewrite and resend the script, repeatedly.

**What people do today.** Patients phone or visit many pharmacies, swap tips on Reddit, WhatsApp and community spreadsheets, split tablets, stretch doses or go without; caregivers travel to other towns; LMIC patients buy from private drug shops at markups or abandon treatment; pharmacists phone wholesalers daily and improvise substitutions; prescribers rewrite prescriptions for alternate strengths or drugs several times per patient; hospitals maintain internal shortage boards and compounding workarounds.

**Proposed intervention.** A shortage-aware stock-sighting and substitution network. (1) Ingest official shortage feeds (openFDA drug-shortages endpoint, EMA public catalogue, EMA-listed national registers, BfArM list, UK Serious Shortage Protocols) and map each to RxNorm/ATC concepts. (2) An equivalence engine built from public data (RxNorm, FDA Orange Book TE codes, NDC directory, WHO EML/ATC-DDD, national formularies) that enumerates clinically equivalent presentations: 2x10 mg for 20 mg, other salts, liquid vs tablet, same-class alternatives flagged for prescriber decision. (3) Time-decayed, de-duplicated 'filled here today' sightings from patients and verified pharmacists, keyed to pharmacy locations from NPPES/OpenStreetMap, with trust scoring and no personal health data; controlled substances shown only as 'recently filled' without quantities, with rate limits and optional suppression to limit diversion risk. (4) A prescriber 'substitution packet': one page with the chosen equivalent, dose conversion, and a pre-filled transfer note, cutting the rewrite loop. (5) LMIC mode: facility-level stockout reports from community health workers and patients on a public map with SMS/USSD entry, plus an opt-in inventory feed for pharmacies that already use POS software. Novel versus existing tools: it combines official feeds, equivalence data, crowd sightings and a clinician workflow in one open system and does not require pharmacy integration to be useful. It works without AI; AI is not needed for any core function.

**Works across countries?.** High. Structured shortage feeds exist in the US, EU, UK, Canada and Australia; the equivalence layer uses international (ATC, WHO EML) plus national coding; the sighting layer only needs pharmacy locations and users. In LMICs, where no official feed exists, the crowd and CHW reporting modes work standalone and can later ingest ministry LMIS exports. Controlled-substance rules and substitution laws differ by country and must be configured per jurisdiction.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for patients and caregivers. Revenue from clinics and health systems (prescriber substitution tools, EHR/e-prescribing integration), pharmacies (verified-status updates and listing tools, with a strict no-pay-to-rank rule and sighting-based verification), payers/PBMs and manufacturers (aggregated, anonymised shortage-impact and abandonment data), and public-health agencies (dashboards and API). In LMICs, contracts with ministries, Global Fund/PEPFAR implementing partners and NGOs for facility-level availability monitoring. No sale of individual patient data; crowd data remains open under a share-alike licence to protect the public good.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web app with backend, database and auth. Real public data available today: openFDA drug shortages (public, keyless) and NDC directory, NLM RxNorm API, FDA Orange Book, DailyMed, NPPES NPI registry (pharmacy taxonomy codes and addresses), OpenStreetMap, EMA shortage catalogue and BfArM list (web, scraping needed), WHO EML/ATC. Hard parts: cold start for sightings (seed with pharmacist partners and ADHD/diabetes communities), staleness and false reports, pharmacy gaming, diversion risk for Schedule II drugs (mitigations above, possibly excluding them publicly at launch), liability framing (equivalence shown as reference only, prescriber decides), and LMIC coverage depending on partner CHW programs. No hardware or regulatory approval required; physical logistics are explicitly out of scope.

**Measurable outcomes.** Days from prescription to fill for drugs on the shortage list (users vs matched baseline); number of pharmacies contacted per successful fill; prescription abandonment rate for shortage drugs (via pharmacy partners); clinician minutes per shortage episode; self-reported treatment-interruption days; sighting precision (confirmed-in-stock rate when a user acts on a sighting within 24 h); in LMICs, facility stockout-days reported and median time to resolution, and share of patients redirected to an in-stock facility within a set distance.

## 3. Patients pay several times the regulated or reference price for the same molecule because retail medicine prices and cheaper equivalents are invisible at the point of sale

*Domain:* healthcare-access / affordability and medicine prices · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Households paying out of pocket for medicines in LMICs, where medicines are the largest household health cost after food (up to 90% of people in developing countries buy medicines out of pocket; medicines are >60% of out-of-pocket health spending in WHO South-East Asia). Concretely: Indian patients prescribed branded generics (studies find 100-1,177% price variation between brands of the same molecule and strength; ~34% of 1,575 antihypertensive brands priced above the DPCO 2021 ceiling; only 12% of 200 patients in a 2024 study knew the difference between branded and generic), chronic-disease patients in Africa (lowest-priced generics at 3.0-11.5x international reference price, originators >25x), patients in Afghanistan and other markets with documented pharmacy-to-pharmacy price dispersion, and the poorest quintile globally (3 in 4 of the poorest face financial hardship from health costs versus fewer than 1 in 25 of the richest, WHO/World Bank 2025).

**Scale and severity.** WHO/World Bank 2025 Global Monitoring Report: 2.1 billion people (26% of the world) faced financial hardship from out-of-pocket health spending in 2022, including 1.6 billion living in poverty or pushed deeper into it; progress has slowed and 24% are projected to still face hardship in 2030. The 2023 report (data to 2019) counted >1 billion people with catastrophic spending (>10% of household budget) and 344 million pushed into extreme poverty. Medicines account for 20-60% of health spending in LMICs (WHO pricing guideline). Lancet Global Health 2025 (71 WHO/HAI surveys, 54 countries): patients pay 3.0-11.5x international reference prices for lowest-priced generics. India: NPPA had demanded about Rs 10,013 crore (roughly US$1.2 billion) from companies for selling above ceiling prices, of which Rs 8,526 crore (85%) was unrecovered as of 30 Sept 2025, ~70% tied up in litigation; the government's Jan Aushadhi scheme (17,990 outlets by Dec 2025, prices 50-80% below branded) exists precisely because retail prices are far above cost, yet awareness surveys are small and show large knowledge gaps. Caveat: the share of financial hardship attributable to price opacity rather than to absolute poverty or absent coverage is not quantified; this is a large contributor within a very large problem, not the whole of it.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Regulated price data exist but are unusable at the counter: South Africa's Medicine Price Registry is public but 'difficult to find, requires technical skills' (a civic-tech wrapper was last updated years ago); India's NPPA Pharma Sahi Daam covers only scheduled medicines and manufacturer-reported prices and has no overcharge feedback loop that works (85% of demanded dues unrecovered); the Philippines' Drug Price Reference Index is used for government procurement and has no public download; Brazil's public price list found was dated 2019. E-pharmacies (Tata 1mg, PharmEasy, TrueMeds) show substitutes but are urban, online, sales-driven and absent from the physical pharmacy where most LMIC purchases happen; GoodRx is US-only and coupon-based. WHO/HAI price surveys are periodic research exercises, not consumer tools. Prescribing by brand persists, pharmacists earn more on high-margin brands, and patients fear that cheaper equivalents are lower quality, a fear reinforced by real NSQ incidents.

**What people do today.** Asking the pharmacist for a cheaper brand; walking to a Jan Aushadhi or public-sector outlet when one exists; buying partial strips or half a course; skipping doses or stopping chronic therapy; comparing prices on e-pharmacy apps for later offline purchase; borrowing money; NGOs run periodic price surveys and advocacy; regulators issue overcharging notices that take years to enforce.

**Proposed intervention.** An open point-of-sale reference-price and cheapest-equivalent engine anchored on regulated prices, not on an e-commerce catalogue. Build a multi-country database mapping molecule+strength+form to (a) the regulated ceiling or reference price (India NPPA ceiling prices and Jan Aushadhi MRPs; South Africa Single Exit Price; Philippines DPRI; Brazil CMED; Kenya KEMSA/MEDS price lists; HAI/MSH international reference prices), (b) the list of registered equivalents (CDSCO SUGAM, SAHPRA, PPB registers) with their MRPs, and (c) receipt-verified retail prices crowdsourced from patients (photo of bill, moderated, pharmacy-level, no personal data). A patient or community health worker searches by brand name and sees: the molecule, the legal ceiling or reference price, the cheapest registered equivalent and nearest public/Jan Aushadhi outlet, and a one-tap, pre-filled overcharge complaint to the regulator (NPPA complaint channel exists but is barely used). A clinician view supports generic prescribing with ceiling prices per molecule. Offline-first progressive web app plus SMS/USSD lookup for feature phones. Optional link to the NSQ/recall registry so quality fears about a cheaper equivalent are answered with data rather than brand reputation. Novel: regulator-price anchoring, multi-country equivalence, receipt-based verification and a complaint loop; e-pharmacies have no incentive to build this. Works without AI.

**Works across countries?.** Works anywhere a regulated or reference price list exists (India, South Africa, Brazil, Philippines, Egypt, Iran, Bangladesh, Indonesia, many EU states), and degrades gracefully to international reference price plus crowd prices where none exists (most of sub-Saharan Africa). Country adapters are needed for each price list and register; the comparison, complaint and prescribing layers are common.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for patients and community health workers. Revenue from health insurers and government schemes (claims price-audit and benchmark API), employers and microinsurers, hospital and clinic software vendors (generic-prescribing and formulary-pricing API), pharmacies that opt into a receipt-verified 'fair price' badge (no pay-to-rank), and NGOs/regulators licensing price-monitoring dashboards in place of ad hoc surveys. No pharmaceutical sponsorship and no sale of individual purchase data. Grant funding plausible from HAI, WHO, Gates and national health-system strengthening programs.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web app with backend, database and auth, India-first: NPPA publishes ceiling prices and monthly notifications, PMBJP publishes its product list with MRPs, CDSCO SUGAM lists registered products, and South Africa's SEP list is downloadable (XLS) from open.africa for a second country. Hard parts: there is no open, maintained Indian brand-to-molecule database, so the equivalence map must be built from NPPA, PMBJP and CDSCO data and curated; ceiling lists change monthly; receipt verification quality and fraud; defamation risk (publish only verified, sourced prices); low-literacy UX and vernacular languages; the Philippines DPRI and Brazil CMED lists require scraping; and the incentive problem (pharmacists and prescribers) that software alone cannot remove, so impact depends on CHW and insurer distribution. No hardware or regulatory approval required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Receipt-verified per-prescription out-of-pocket cost before vs after use for enrolled users; share of searches where a cheaper registered equivalent was purchased; number and resolution rate of overcharge complaints filed through the tool; measured price dispersion for tracked molecules in monitored cities over time; refill-continuity for chronic patients (hypertension, diabetes) among users versus controls; number of molecules and countries with regulated-price coverage; insurer claim savings on audited prescriptions.

## 4. Medication harm in older adults from polypharmacy and fall-risk drugs because no one owns the complete medication list across prescribers

*Domain:* elderly-chronic · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Adults 65+ on five or more medicines and the family members who manage their pills. A 2024 meta-analysis (122 studies, 57.3 million people) puts polypharmacy at nearly 40% of older adults worldwide, highest in Europe, North America, age 70+, and nursing homes; it is 62% among older people with dementia (2023 pooled estimate). In the US more than 4 in 10 older adults take 5+ prescriptions (Lown Institute); in England over 1 million people take 10+ medicines, almost half of them aged 75+ (Feb 2026 policy brief). The person doing the work is usually a family caregiver: 82% of the ~20 million US caregivers performing medical/nursing tasks manage medications (AARP Home Alone Revisited). Hardest hit: people 75+, people with dementia, those seeing several specialists plus a GP, and anyone recently discharged from hospital.

**Scale and severity.** (Method note: direct fetches of who.int, cdc.gov, cms.gov, aarp.org, jamanetwork.com and NCBI were blocked by this session's network policy; figures are from search-engine excerpts of those primary pages, cross-checked.) US: an estimated 10 million older Americans (1 in 5) had an adverse drug event in 2018 and ~280,000 were hospitalized for one (Lown Institute, Medication Overload, 2019). Emergency visits for harms from correctly used medicines rise with age to 16 per 1,000 people aged 75+ per year, led by anticoagulant bleeding and hypoglycemia from diabetes drugs (Budnitz et al., JAMA 2021, NEISS data 2017-2019). A nationwide Korean cohort of adults 65-84 (2016-2018) found continuous polypharmacy associated with adjusted hazard ratios of 1.85 for hospitalization, 1.92 for ED visits and 2.57 for death (Chae et al., Frontiers in Pharmacology 2024). WHO (World Patient Safety Day 2022) states that half of all preventable harm in medical care is medication-related, a quarter of it severe or life-threatening, with a global cost of medication errors of about US$42 billion a year (a figure carried forward from 2017). Falls are the main injury pathway: CDC reports 41,400 unintentional fall deaths among Americans 65+ in 2023 (69.9 per 100,000, rising in every age band since 2003) and over 14 million older adults falling each year; a 2022 single-site ED study found 45.8% of older adults presenting after a fall were on fall-risk-increasing drugs and 25.5% on two or more; 94% of adults 65+ were taking at least one high-risk medication in 2017 versus 57% in 1999 (cited in ACP Internist 2025). After discharge, medication-related harm affects 17-51% of older patients within 30 days, 35-59% of it judged preventable (Parekh et al. systematic review, 2018). The Lown Institute projected 4.6 million hospitalizations, 150,000 premature deaths and US$62 billion in costs over the 2020s if nothing changes (a projection, not a measurement).

**Why existing solutions fail.** The tools exist on paper but are applied by the wrong people at the wrong moment. Explicit criteria (AGS Beers; STOPP/START v3, 2023, 190 criteria, CC-BY licensed) are implemented as EHR alerts to prescribers who suffer alert fatigue, and each prescriber sees only their own slice of the list. Reviews of deprescribing barriers (BJGP 2020; Reeve-type mixed-studies review 2018) find fragmented care, GP-specialist communication gaps, time pressure, prescriber uncertainty and a pattern of deprescribing only after harm has occurred. Formal review programs reach few people: in the 2022 data year only ~3.2 million of ~50 million Medicare Part D enrollees (6.4%) received a Comprehensive Medication Review, and the majority of plans score 2 stars or less on that measure; England's Structured Medication Reviews are restricted to 10+ medicines and average 0.9 medication changes per review. Fall-related care encounters do not reduce FRID use afterwards (review evidence). Consumer medication apps (Medisafe and similar) are adherence tools: they help people take more pills on time, not ask whether a pill should still be there. Developers lost the free NLM drug-drug interaction API in January 2024, so even the public infrastructure for consumer-side checks went backwards.

**What people do today.** Handwritten or spreadsheet lists kept by a daughter or spouse; weekly pill organizers; a 'brown bag' review only if a pharmacist or clinic offers it; pharmacy printouts; reading leaflets and searching side effects online; carrying the bottle bag to the ED; relying on each prescriber's partial list; and after a fall, usually nothing changes. Fewer than half of older adults even tell a clinician about a fall (CDC, via secondary sources).

**Proposed intervention.** A caregiver-owned medication ledger with a transparent deprescribing screen and event-driven review triggers. The family caregiver and older adult build one canonical list across all prescribers and pharmacies (manual entry with RxNorm autocomplete; optional pill-label photo capture; optional import of Medicare Part D claims via CMS Blue Button 2.0). The backend normalizes every item to RxCUI/ATC and runs explicit, citable rules rather than opaque advice: STOPP v3 criteria (open license), anticholinergic burden scoring using published scales, fall-risk-increasing drug classes, therapeutic duplication via RxClass, drug-disease flags against the person's entered conditions, and renal-dose flags if an eGFR is entered. Output is (1) a one-page 'questions for your prescriber or pharmacist' sheet that names the criterion and its evidence, (2) a fall/hospitalization/new-prescriber trigger that automatically assembles a review request ('a fall on 3 March; these three medicines are FRIDs'), and (3) a versioned shareable list for ED and admission. What is novel: it is deprescribing-oriented and owned by the person who actually sees all the bottles, it converts the criteria clinicians ignore into a patient-side request that prescribers must answer, and it fires on the events (falls, discharges, new specialists) that the health system misses. It is useful with zero AI: it is a rule engine over open criteria. OCR of labels or free-text parsing can be added later but are not required.

**Works across countries?.** High. The WHO ATC classification is universal; STOPP/START was developed and validated in Europe; anticholinergic and FRID lists are international. National drug dictionaries exist as open data in several countries (UK dm+d, Canada DPD, Australia AMT/PBS, US RxNorm), so the normalization layer is per-country mapping rather than new science. Care fragmentation across prescribers is universal, and the burden is rising fastest in Asia and LMICs. Regulatory caution: an informational tool that prompts the user to ask a clinician is generally not a medical device, but automated interaction or dosing warnings edge toward software-as-medical-device under EU MDR and FDA CDS guidance, so wording must stay 'review prompts' with the clinician as decision-maker.

**Sustainable business model.** B2B2C with the family tier free. Payers whose money is at stake for ADE admissions and whose quality scores reward reviews: Medicare Advantage and Part D plans (CMR completion is a Star Ratings measure; the tool prepares and documents reviews), PACE programs, GUIDE/chronic-care-management providers, home care and home health agencies, UK Primary Care Networks that must deliver SMRs, long-term care insurers, and employer caregiver benefits. Revenue is per enrolled member per month or per completed, documented review. Social value holds because the paying party only benefits when fewer high-risk medicines and fewer ADE admissions occur; no pharma funding and no data sales.

**MVP feasibility.** Fully buildable as a web app by a small team. Stack: auth with household roles (older adult, caregivers, read-only clinician link), Postgres tables for persons, medications (RxCUI), conditions, labs, events (falls, admissions, new prescriber) and review sheets. Real public data/APIs: NLM RxNorm REST API (free, no key) for normalization, RxClass for ATC/therapeutic classes, openFDA drug label API and DailyMed SPL for boxed warnings and contraindication text, STOPP/START v3 (CC-BY) and published anticholinergic scales encoded as rules, CMS Blue Button 2.0 sandbox with synthetic beneficiaries for claims import. Hard parts: the NLM drug-drug interaction API was discontinued in January 2024, so interaction checking needs either a licensed dataset (DrugBank) or heuristic use of openFDA label text; encoding ~190 STOPP criteria needs clinical review and testing; HIPAA/GDPR handling; Blue Button covers only Medicare fee-for-service Part D claims and requires app registration; label OCR is optional. None of this needs hardware, regulatory clearance before use, or logistics.

**Measurable outcomes.** Verified deprescribing: number and share of flagged medicines discontinued or dose-reduced within 90 days (caregiver-confirmed, validated against claims in a pilot); change in anticholinergic burden score per person; FRID count 90 days after a logged fall; share of fall events followed by a documented medication review; completed CMRs/SMRs per 1,000 members; falls, ED visits and ADE hospitalizations per 100 person-years versus matched controls. Not counted as success: medications listed, logins, or reminders sent.

**Sources consulted:**

## 5. Family caregivers managing dementia behavioral symptoms at home with no individualized support, leading to caregiver depression, sedating drugs and early institutionalization

*Domain:* elderly-chronic · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** People living with dementia at home and the relatives (about two-thirds women) who care for them. WHO's Executive Board paper (EB156, 2025) counts 57 million people with dementia in 2021, over 60% in low- and middle-income countries, with nearly 10 million new cases a year; roughly two-thirds of people with dementia live in the community rather than institutions. Globally 75% are undiagnosed (about 41 million; up to 90% in LMICs) per ADI's World Alzheimer Report 2021, so most families manage behaviors without any diagnosis-triggered support. In the US, 7.2 million people 65+ have Alzheimer's dementia and nearly 12 million unpaid caregivers support them; 66% of those caregivers live with the person and 60% were employed in the past year (Alzheimer's Association 2025 Facts and Figures).

**Scale and severity.** US unpaid dementia care: 19.2 billion hours in 2024 valued at US$413.5 billion, with fewer caregivers each doing more (about 31 hours a week); 59% rate their emotional stress as high or very high (Alzheimer's Association 2025). Pooled prevalence among informal dementia caregivers is 31% for depression (43 studies, 16,911 participants) and 32% for anxiety (UEA meta-analyses, 2020). Behavioral and psychological symptoms (agitation, aggression, sleep disturbance, wandering, refusal of care, apathy, delusions) affect 80-90% of people with dementia at some point; in a community sample, 88.5% had clinically significant symptoms and the caregivers of 79.6% reported moderate-to-severe distress from at least one, versus 26.8% of professional caregivers in nursing homes (PMC9150772). A matched case-control study found a 10% rise in Neuropsychiatric Inventory score raised the odds of nursing-home admission by 30% (OR 1.30, 95% CI 1.14-1.50), though not every cohort confirms BPSD as an independent predictor. About 10% of community-dwelling people with dementia in Texas Medicare (2015-2020) were prescribed antipsychotics, with other estimates of 11-29%, despite the FDA boxed warning for increased mortality; the CMS nursing-home reduction campaign produced no spillover to the community. Only 8.3% of US dementia caregivers received any caregiver training across three national survey waves (2015-2021); in a UsAgainstAlzheimer's survey 68.7% had received no training or information at all. Global societal cost of dementia was US$1.3 trillion in 2019, projected to exceed US$2.8 trillion by 2030 (WHO). The broader context from the 2025 AARP/NAC survey: 63 million US caregivers, 44% providing high-intensity care, 1 in 5 in fair or poor health because of caregiving.

**Why existing solutions fail.** The effective interventions are known and almost never delivered. Psychosocial and caregiver-delivered non-drug interventions reduce BPSD with moderate effect sizes and reduce caregiver distress with small but significant effects (Brodaty & Arasaratnam; Gilhooly meta-review; Meng et al. 2021), and guidelines say they are first line, but programs such as REACH II, Savvy Caregiver, the Tailored Activity Program and DICE are multi-session, staff-delivered and reach a tiny fraction of families. WHO's iSupport is a generic online course, not an individualized plan. One in three clinicians believes nothing can be done about dementia (ADI 2021), and most families have no diagnosis and therefore no entry point. Helplines are reactive and lose the pattern between calls. The US GUIDE model (launched July 2024; 330 participating organizations as of the July 2025 CMS fact sheet) pays for care navigation, 24/7 access and respite up to US$2,563 per patient-year, but navigators lack a tool that turns a family's day-to-day observations into a tested behavior plan at scale. Existing apps are reminder tools, content libraries, peer forums or chatbots, none of which run the describe-investigate-create-evaluate loop that the evidence base says works.

**What people do today.** Trial and error; Facebook groups and forums; sedating with over-the-counter antihistamine sleep aids (which are strongly anticholinergic and worsen cognition) or 'as needed' antipsychotics; locking doors and hiding car keys; quitting or cutting work; emergency department visits for agitation; and eventually placement in long-term care, often earlier than the family wanted.

**Proposed intervention.** A structured behavior-episode log and N-of-1 experiment engine built on the DICE framework (Describe, Investigate, Create, Evaluate). The caregiver logs each episode in under 30 seconds on a phone-sized web page (what happened, time, what preceded it, what helped, severity, how distressed they were). The system aggregates episodes into time-of-day and trigger patterns (e.g., sundowning clusters, episodes after bathing, after a new medicine). The Investigate step is a decision tree that first forces a check of physical and medical causes (pain, infection, constipation, dehydration, sensory loss, and new or anticholinergic/sedative medicines pulled from the medication list via RxNorm/RxClass), then environment and unmet needs. It proposes a small matched set of evidence-based non-drug strategies as a two-week experiment, then measures whether episode frequency, severity and caregiver distress fell, and either keeps, swaps or escalates. It produces a clinician-facing summary (symptom and distress scores over time, what was tried) for GUIDE navigators, GPs, memory clinics and Admiral-nurse-type services, which is exactly what those programs need and lack. Novelty: it is an experiment engine for behavior, not a content library or chat; it closes the loop between family observation and clinical decision; it links behavior to medication changes. It works without AI: aggregation and a rule-based decision tree. Free-text summarization can be added later.

**Works across countries?.** High. BPSD, caregiver distress and the DICE/NPI framework are international; the UK (NHS dementia care-plan reviews, Admiral Nurses), Australia (Dementia Support Australia's behaviour support program), Canada, Japan and the Nordics all have services that would consume the clinician summary. More than 60% of people with dementia live in LMICs where nearly all care is family care and diagnosis is rare, so a mobile-web, low-bandwidth, multilingual version without a diagnosis prerequisite is the global path. No hardware. Regulatory exposure is low because it does not diagnose or treat, but it must be positioned as caregiver support and not as a medical device, and the NPI-Q instrument is copyrighted (license or a non-proprietary scale needed).

**Sustainable business model.** US: GUIDE participants (monthly care-management payment per beneficiary plus respite billing) and Medicare chronic care and principal-illness navigation codes have a direct reason to pay per enrolled dyad; Medicare Advantage plans and ACOs save on ED visits and placement; memory clinics and home care agencies; employer caregiver benefits (60% of dementia caregivers work). UK Integrated Care Boards and Australia's behaviour support program are public-sector buyers. Family tier free; institutional tier priced per enrolled dyad per month. No data sales and no pharma funding, so the incentive stays aligned with fewer episodes and fewer sedating prescriptions.

**MVP feasibility.** Fully buildable as a web app. Stack: auth with a family-circle model (multiple caregivers, one person with dementia, optional clinician link), Postgres for episodes, patterns, strategies, experiments and periodic scores, scheduled email/SMS nudges (Twilio) for logging and two-week evaluations, PDF export of the clinician summary. Content: the DICE framework, published non-drug strategy taxonomies and the Alzheimer's Association Dementia Care Practice Recommendations are public literature that must be encoded and clinically reviewed; medication linkage uses the free RxNorm/RxClass APIs to flag anticholinergic and sedative additions. No large external dataset is required. Hard parts: validating the strategy library with dementia-care clinicians; sustaining logging during crises; multi-caregiver consistency; licensing or replacing NPI-Q; localization. Demonstrating effect on placement needs a pilot with a GUIDE or memory-clinic partner.

**Measurable outcomes.** Episode frequency and severity per week per dyad over 8-12 weeks versus baseline; caregiver distress score (NPI-Q distress or Zarit short form) change; share of logged episodes with a documented physical-cause check; new antipsychotic, benzodiazepine or anticholinergic sleep-aid starts; ED visits for behavioral crises; time to long-term-care placement in a matched cohort; share of GUIDE or clinic enrolled dyads with a current behavior plan. Not counted: downloads, articles read, chat messages.

- https://www.dementiaresearcher.nihr.ac.uk/who-dementia-statistics/

## 6. The hospital-to-home transition for older adults: family caregivers sent home with unreadable instructions, no notice and no training, with no owner of the first 30 days

*Domain:* elderly-chronic · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** Older adults discharged from hospital and the relatives who take over their care that same afternoon. In the US this is the Medicare population (tens of millions of discharges a year) and roughly 20 million family caregivers who perform medical and nursing tasks such as wound care, injections, catheter and drain care and complex medication regimens, most with little or no training (AARP Home Alone Revisited 2019); only about 20% received at least 24 hours' notice of discharge. The 2025 AARP/NAC survey counts 63 million US caregivers, 44% providing high-intensity care and 38% reporting no established planning in place. Patients with multiple chronic conditions, cognitive impairment, heart failure, COPD and hip fracture, and caregivers with low health literacy or limited English are hit hardest. The same transition failure is documented in the NHS, Canada and Australia.

**Scale and severity.** Medicare fee-for-service 30-day readmissions were 15.0% in 2023, up 0.4 points from the prior year (MedPAC January 2025 meeting summary); 2,273 of 5,236 hospitals (42%) were penalized under the Hospital Readmissions Reduction Program in FY2023 (Advisory Board). Harm in the first month is largely medication-related and preventable: a systematic review found medication-related harm in 17-51% of older patients within 30 days of discharge, 35-59% of it preventable (Parekh et al. 2018); at 30 days nearly half of patients were nonadherent to at least one medication change made in hospital, 26% had visited an ED and 6% had been readmitted (Weir et al. 2020). On the hospital side, in the average hospital fewer than half of nurses were confident the patient or caregiver could manage care after discharge, and higher confidence predicted lower 30-day readmission, especially for multimorbid patients (Pogue et al., Medical Care 2024). Written instructions are not written for families: 81% of standard English discharge documents in a major EHR vendor's templates exceeded the AMA sixth-grade threshold (American Journal of Medicine, 2025), and in a safety-net hospital only 11.3% of instructions were below seventh grade (JGIM letter, 2021). The fix is known: pooled randomized trials show that integrating caregivers into discharge planning cuts readmission by 25% at 90 days (RR 0.75, 95% CI 0.62-0.91) and 24% at 180 days (Rodakowski et al., JAGS 2017), yet Transitional Care Management, the Medicare service meant to deliver this, is underused and reaches high-risk, minority and frail beneficiaries least (2015-2019 claims analysis).

**Why existing solutions fail.** Everything that exists is hospital-facing or clinician-facing. Discharge-planning and placement platforms (CarePort/WellSky, EHR discharge modules) optimize bed flow and documentation, not what the family does at 8 pm on day one. Transitional Care Management pays for a clinician contact within two business days and a visit within 7-14 days, but uptake is low and skewed away from the highest-risk patients, and the clinician has no view of what happened at home in between. The CARE Act (enacted in 44 states and territories per AARP) requires hospitals to record a caregiver, notify them of discharge and instruct them, but compliance is weakly measured and 'instruction' is usually a verbal walkthrough at the moment of discharge. Home health often starts days later. Discharge summaries are written for the next clinician, not the family, and are above recommended reading level in most cases. Caregiver proxy access to patient portals is patchy and varies by state law. The result is that the first 72 hours, when medication changes, new tasks and red-flag symptoms all land at once, have no owner.

**What people do today.** Three-ring binders and sticky notes on the fridge; photographing the discharge papers; calling the hospital unit back; asking the community pharmacist to explain the medication changes; searching symptoms online; using the emergency department as the plan when unsure; paying for private aides where affordable; and in the best cases a visiting nurse who arrives several days later and reconstructs what happened.

**Proposed intervention.** A discharge-to-home operating plan builder for the family caregiver, generated from structured discharge data before or at discharge. Data sources: the hospital's 21st Century Cures Act Patient Access API (FHIR R4: MedicationRequest, Condition, Procedure, Appointment, DocumentReference), CMS Blue Button 2.0 for Medicare claims, or fallback manual entry from the printed summary. Output: (1) a day-by-day schedule for the first 30 days built around the three medication change types that cause 30-day harm (new, stopped, changed dose), each with a one-line plain-language reason; (2) tasks that require a skill (wound care, injections, drains, oxygen) mapped to the free AARP Home Alone Alliance instructional videos; (3) follow-up appointments with 'what to bring and ask'; (4) condition-specific green/yellow/red symptom zones (heart failure, COPD, pneumonia, hip fracture, stroke, surgery) with an explicit who-to-call ladder so the ED is the last step, not the first; (5) a CARE Act rights checklist and an 'ask for a TCM visit' prompt; (6) a daily status log (weight, symptoms, doses taken) that the home health nurse and TCM clinician can view through a share link, so the two-business-day contact has something to look at. Novelty: it is caregiver-facing and built from structured discharge data, it sequences actions rather than listing facts, and it operationalizes the one transition intervention with RCT evidence (caregiver integration) without adding hospital staff. No AI is required: structured FHIR mapping plus condition templates. Free-text summary parsing can be added later as an optional accelerator.

**Works across countries?.** High. The transition failure is universal (NHS 'discharge to assess', Canadian and Australian readmission programs). Patient-access FHIR APIs are mandated in the US, available via the NHS App and GP Connect in England, My Health Record in Australia, and the EU European Health Data Space from 2029, and manual entry works anywhere. Symptom-zone sheets already exist in many languages. Adaptations needed: per-country medication dictionaries, local who-to-call ladders, and proxy-access rules which vary by jurisdiction.

**Sustainable business model.** Buyers whose money is already at stake: hospitals and health systems (HRRP penalties, CARE Act compliance evidence, enabling billable TCM 99495/99496 visits), home health agencies (start-of-care efficiency), Medicare Advantage plans and ACOs carrying readmission risk, and employer caregiver benefits; in the UK, ICBs and discharge-to-assess programs. Pricing per discharge or per member per month. Family tier free. Incentives are aligned because the buyer only wins when readmissions and ED returns fall; no data sales and no device or pharma tie-ins.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app by a small team, with one honest dependency. Stack: auth with roles (caregiver, patient, clinician share link), Postgres for plans, medication changes, tasks, appointments, zone templates and daily observations, SMS/email reminders (Twilio), PDF export. Real APIs: SMART on FHIR patient-access OAuth against public sandboxes (SMART Health IT, HAPI FHIR, EHR vendor open sandboxes) to prove the structured ingestion path; CMS Blue Button 2.0 sandbox with synthetic beneficiaries; NLM RxNorm for medication normalization; condition zone templates from AHA/AHRQ public materials; AARP Home Alone Alliance videos are freely linkable. Hard parts: production hospital Patient Access API onboarding is per-vendor app registration and slow; many discharge summaries still arrive as free-text PDFs, so manual entry and later NLP are needed; caregiver proxy access laws vary; medication reconciliation accuracy must be clinically reviewed; proving a readmission effect requires a hospital or home-health pilot; HIPAA compliance. No hardware, no pre-use regulatory approval, no physical logistics.

**Measurable outcomes.** 30- and 90-day readmission and ED return rates in pilot discharges versus matched controls; medication-change discrepancies found at a day-7 reconciliation; share of discharges where the family had the plan at least 24 hours before leaving; TCM contact within 2 business days and visit within 14 days; caregiver Preparedness for Caregiving Scale at day 7; share of yellow-zone escalations resolved by phone rather than ED; CARE Act compliance documentation rate. Not counted: plans generated, pages viewed, reminders sent.

## 7. Severely wasted children go untreated because RUTF pipelines break and case-finding misses them

*Domain:* maternal-child (childhood malnutrition / wasting treatment) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** Children under 5 with severe wasting and their caregivers (mostly mothers) in food-insecure and conflict-affected districts: northern Nigeria, Somalia, South Sudan, Sudan, Ethiopia, Kenya's arid counties, Afghanistan, Yemen, the Sahel, plus the large caseloads in Southern Asia (India, Pakistan, Bangladesh), which holds over three-quarters of severely wasted children but where treatment coverage is lowest (Pakistan and Nepal under 5% in UNICEF-linked reporting). Also the frontline workers who run outpatient therapeutic programmes (OTPs): community health workers screening with MUAC tapes, nutrition nurses at primary health centres, and district nutrition officers and NGO logisticians who must decide where scarce Ready-to-Use Therapeutic Food (RUTF) goes.

**Scale and severity.** UNICEF/WHO/World Bank Joint Child Malnutrition Estimates 2025 (2024 data): 42.8 million children under 5 wasted (6.6%), of whom 12.2 million (1.9%) severely wasted; 150.2 million stunted. UNICEF has repeatedly stated that only about 1 in 3 children with severe wasting receives treatment (UNICEF 'No Time to Waste' 2022; Nutrition Cluster/UNICEF webinar brief Feb 2024). UNICEF RUTF Market and Supply Update (Dec 2025): UNICEF delivered 86,500 MT of RUTF in 2024, enough for about 6.2 million children, against 12.2 million needing immediate treatment; funded demand averages ~80,000 MT/year while offered supply capacity is 290,000 MT, i.e. the binding constraint is funding and planning, not manufacturing; funded demand expected lower in 2025 as large donors scaled back. 2025 pipeline crisis: Save the Children (Aug 2025) warned Nigeria, Kenya, Somalia and South Sudan could run out of RUTF within ~3 months; only 39% of required 2025 nutrition funding had been received; Nigeria secured 64% of the 629,000 cartons needed for the lean season; South Sudan treated only one third of children with severe acute malnutrition Jan–Jul 2025 after facility closures. UNICEF (2024) appealed for US$165M warning nearly 2 million severely malnourished children were at risk of death from therapeutic-food funding shortfalls. 2026 UNICEF situation reports: Sudan RUTF pipeline gap ~US$21.2M with facility stock-out rate 11% (early 2026); Afghanistan RUTF pipeline interruptions since April 2026 reduced admissions. Note: primary pages were read via domain-restricted search excerpts because direct fetches were blocked in this session.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Community management of acute malnutrition (CMAM) with RUTF is a proven, standardized treatment, so the failure is in reach and continuity, not efficacy. (1) Funding is volatile and short-cycle; UNICEF's own supplier consultation lists forecasting among the main supply challenges and its 2025 update says funding 'remains unpredictable and insufficient'. (2) Commodity planning is done with national annual consumption/morbidity methods (desktop tools such as Quantimed/PipeLine, NutriDash annual reporting) that cannot anticipate district-level surges driven by drought, floods, price spikes, conflict displacement or lean seasons; stock-outs then cause defaulting and deaths. (3) Early-warning systems (IPC, FEWS NET, CHIRPS, WFP price data) and treatment data (OTP admissions in DHIS2/paper registers, stock in LMIS) live in separate silos; the one serious attempt to model admissions from leading indicators (Action Against Hunger's MERIAM, since 2017, up to 6–8 months lead time) has remained a research/pilot effort with no published operational accuracy and no shared tool used by district teams. (4) Coverage assessments (SQUEAC reviews across 21+ countries) consistently find distance, opportunity cost, lack of awareness of the programme and of malnutrition itself as the dominant barriers; children arrive late or not at all. (5) Leakage and black-market diversion of RUTF (documented in Nigeria) worsen shortages. (6) When supply collapses, there is no principled way to allocate scarce cartons across districts by expected caseload and mortality risk; decisions are ad hoc.

**What people do today.** NGOs and UNICEF country offices shift buffer stocks between districts and borrow from neighbouring facilities; programmes switch to reduced-dosing protocols or restrict admissions to the most severe cases; OTP sites close or suspend admissions (15% of nutrition facilities shut in South Sudan in 2025); UNICEF relied on rollover inventory and 15,800 MT of donated US product to hold 2024 deliveries flat; caregivers share sachets between siblings, dilute rations, sell sachets, or walk to distant sites; district teams make monthly projections by hand from tally sheets; Family MUAC programmes shift screening to mothers to find cases earlier; emergency appeals and media warnings are used to trigger last-minute donor top-ups.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, district-level RUTF pipeline early-warning and allocation platform. It ingests (a) monthly OTP admissions, discharges and stock-on-hand from DHIS2 (Web API) or CSV/Excel uploads from NGO partners, and (b) public leading indicators: IPC acute food insecurity phases (IPC public API), FEWS NET outlooks, CHIRPS rainfall anomalies, WFP/FAO market food prices and ACLED conflict events via HDX, seasonal calendars and WorldPop under-5 population. It produces per-district 1–3 month admissions forecasts using transparent seasonal baselines adjusted by leading indicators (statistical models with back-tested error bands, no LLM required), converts forecasts into cartons required, computes weeks-of-stock and projected stock-out dates against in-country pipeline deliveries, and recommends reorders and cross-district reallocations under a stated priority rule (predicted caseload x severity). Views are shared across MoH, UNICEF, WFP and Nutrition Cluster NGOs so that one agreed picture replaces siloed spreadsheets. What is novel: today no operational tool links anticipatory early warning to commodity planning at district granularity, forecasting is national/annual, and allocation under scarcity, now the central problem after the 2025 funding collapse, is done ad hoc. The tool is useful without any AI: its value is data integration, seasonal forecasting, and visibility of stock-out risk weeks ahead.

**Works across countries?.** High across sub-Saharan Africa, the Middle East and South/Southeast Asia emergency contexts: CMAM protocols and RUTF cartons are standardized (WHO 2023 wasting guideline), IPC/CH classifications cover 30+ countries, FEWS NET covers ~30, CHIRPS and WorldPop are global, and DHIS2 is the HMIS in 70+ countries. Lower applicability where RUTF is not the national standard or programmes are domestically financed and run on other systems (e.g., India's POSHAN Abhiyaan/ICDS with locally produced products), where the forecasting layer is still useful but the supply-chain integration must be rebuilt.

**Sustainable business model.** Not a caregiver-paying product. Revenue comes from the organizations that already procure supply-chain and nutrition information systems: (1) service contracts with UNICEF country offices, WFP and Nutrition Cluster NGOs (Save the Children, Action Against Hunger, IRC) for hosting, integration and forecast calibration; (2) MoH procurement financed through World Bank, Global Financing Facility and nutrition supply-chain strengthening lines; (3) an open-core 'global good' model (open-source core, paid hosting/SLA/integration), consistent with Digital Square's 2025 roadmap that names government ownership and structured market pathways as the sustainability gaps. Honest caveats: this is donor-dependent and the sector's 2025 funding collapse shrinks budgets; a small team is viable on roughly 5–10 country contracts, and keeping the core and the forecast data open preserves the social value even if a customer lapses.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web application (e.g., Django/FastAPI or Next.js backend, PostgreSQL with district/time-series tables, role-based auth, multi-tenant by country) deployable to a cloud host. Real public data available now: IPC public API (token via docs.api.ipcinfo.org; R wrapper 'ripc' documents endpoints), FEWS NET Data Explorer exports, CHIRPS rainfall (public downloads), HDX CKAN API (WFP food prices, admin boundaries, ACLED aggregates), WorldPop under-5 rasters (CC-BY), JME country estimates, and the public DHIS2 'play' demo for testing the aggregate data API. What is hard and must be stated honestly: district-level admissions and stock data are not public in most countries and require an MoH/UNICEF/NGO partner to supply them (Nutrition Cluster bulletins publish admissions for some countries such as Nigeria and Somalia, which can seed a demo); forecast validation needs several years of admissions history and will be noisy where reporting is incomplete; data quality problems (missing months, negative stock) must be handled; the tool cannot create funding, it only makes shortages visible earlier and allocates better. No custom hardware, no regulatory approval, no physical logistics network is required; integration with existing LMIS is optional in the MVP.

**Measurable outcomes.** District-months with RUTF stock-outs (count and share) before vs after adoption, and versus matched districts; share of OTP sites reporting at least 8 weeks of stock; forecast accuracy (MAPE of monthly admissions vs actuals, by lead time); days between an early-warning signal and a confirmed reorder or reallocation; treated/needed ratio (coverage) in participating districts; defaulter and death rates in OTP registers (stock-outs are a known driver of defaulting); cartons expired or unaccounted for; admissions-to-stock ratio during lean seasons.

## 8. Under-vaccinated children are invisible to immunization programmes because denominators and defaulter tracking are broken

*Domain:* maternal-child (routine immunization: zero-dose and dropout children) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Infants and toddlers who never start or do not complete the routine schedule, concentrated in three settings Gavi identifies as holding roughly half of all zero-dose children: urban slums, remote rural communities, and conflict-affected areas. Largest absolute numbers are in Nigeria (WorldPop estimates ~2.1 million zero-dose in 2024), DR Congo, Ethiopia, India (679,000 in 2025, down from 909,000), Pakistan, Indonesia, Yemen, Sudan and Afghanistan; more than half of zero-dose children live in fragile and conflict-affected settings that hold only about a third of the world's children. Also the frontline: facility nurses keeping paper tally sheets and tickler files, community health workers asked to trace defaulters without lists, and district EPI managers planning outreach with target populations they know are wrong.

**Scale and severity.** WHO/UNICEF WUENIC estimates: 14.3 million zero-dose children in 2024 (released July 2025) and 13.5 million in 2025 (released July 2026), still above the 2019 baseline of 12.9 million and about 4 million above the Immunization Agenda 2030 trajectory; nearly 20 million infants missed at least one DTP dose in 2024; 7.3 million infants received DTP1 but dropped out before their first measles dose in 2025; DTP1 90%, DTP3 85%, MCV1 84%, MCV2 77% in 2025, all below the 95% needed for measles control; 57 countries reported large or disruptive measles outbreaks in 2025 and WHO estimated ~30 million children at risk for measles (2024 release). Zero-dose children in fragile/conflict settings rose from 3.6 million (2019) to 5.4 million (2024). Data-quality evidence: a PLoS ONE analysis of WHO-reported data 2000–2016 found administrative coverage above 100% in 11% of BCG and 6% of DTP3 reporting events; 13 South African districts logged >100% coverage in 2021; an Ethiopian study found DHIS2 underestimated pentavalent dropout versus DHS, with negative dropout in 3.3% of zone-years. Note: primary pages were read via domain-restricted search excerpts because direct fetches were blocked in this session.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Administrative coverage divides doses by projected target populations that are frequently wrong, so programmes cannot tell a well-covered facility from one whose denominator is too small; WHO's own denominator guidance recommends triangulation but this is rarely operationalized monthly. (2) Household surveys (DHS/MICS) come every 3–5 years and, as Gavi-linked reviewers note, systematically under-represent the very children most likely to be missed. (3) Electronic immunization registries are expensive, partial and often not maintained; most facilities still use paper registers and tickler boxes, so defaulter lists are built by hand. (4) Reminder and tracing interventions demonstrably work when individual data exist (Iraq DHIS2 registry + SMS: pentavalent dropout 5.7% vs 29.7% control; Kenya phone tracing cut dropout below 10% in 11 of 12 facilities; South Sudan tickler files 18% to 11%), but a Ugandan cluster RCT of community defaulter tracing with an app found only DPT3–MCV dropout improved and that the app required extra manpower, i.e. tools that add work without lists and denominators fail. (5) High-resolution zero-dose mapping (WorldPop/GRID3 Bayesian models at 1 km, ADM2 CSVs for Nigeria, DRC, Cameroon, Chad, Mali) exists but is published as research datasets, explicitly 'not official statistics', and is not wired into the monthly microplanning cycle. (6) The DHIS2 Immunization Analysis App computes dropout and classifies facilities but uses the official target population and does not reconcile denominators spatially or generate outreach or defaulter workflows. (7) Conflict, insecurity, supply disruption and misinformation compound all of the above.

**What people do today.** Facility staff keep paper tally sheets and hand-written defaulter lists; EPI practitioners use the 'BCG/DTP1 denominator' trick (infer the cohort from an early dose with high coverage) and focus on dropout because it does not depend on denominators; CHWs do door-to-door head counts before microplanning; periodic intensification of routine immunization and catch-up campaigns are used to sweep up missed children; phone calls to caregivers when a number was recorded; outreach sessions are scheduled by habit rather than by where unvaccinated children actually live; Gavi funds civil-society mapping of zero-dose communities and WorldPop produces maps on a project basis.

**Proposed intervention.** A facility catchment denominator-reconciliation and dropout-tracing web tool, DHIS2-native. (1) It builds facility catchments from open master facility lists (MoH lists, healthsites.io/OSM) and gridded under-1 population (WorldPop, GRID3 settlements) with travel-time surfaces, to produce an alternative expected birth cohort per facility with uncertainty, and flags facilities whose official target population is inconsistent with DTP1/BCG counts. (2) It pulls monthly antigen doses from the DHIS2 aggregate API to compute per-facility dropout (DTP1 to DTP3, DTP1 to MCV1, MCV1 to MCV2) and estimated zero-dose counts, ranking facilities and settlements for outreach and producing a printable monthly microplan. (3) Where a DHIS2 Tracker or other child registry exists, it generates weekly due/overdue lists per CHW, sends scheduled SMS reminders through local gateways, and records tracing outcomes so the loop closes. Novelty: it operationalizes geospatial denominators (currently research-only) into routine monthly planning and ties them to defaulter lists, which neither the DHIS2 immunization app nor standalone registries do. It is useful with no AI at all; the value is denominator triangulation, dropout arithmetic and worklists.

**Works across countries?.** High across low- and lower-middle-income countries: DHIS2 is the national HMIS in 70+ countries and the standard in most Gavi-supported programmes, WUENIC covers all countries, WorldPop/GRID3 provide gridded population for all of Africa and Asia, and the DTP/MCV schedule is universal. Applicability drops where child-level registries do not exist (the tracing module needs one, or at minimum caregiver phone numbers on paper registers) and in high-income countries that already have population registries. Conflict zones limit field use but the planning layer still works on remote data.

**Sustainable business model.** Buyers are ministries of health and their funders rather than families. (1) Gavi Health Systems Strengthening and zero-dose funding lines explicitly finance identifying and reaching zero-dose children, and UNICEF/WHO country offices procure immunization information tools; (2) open-core global-good model: open-source core with paid hosting, DHIS2 integration, training and SLA, which is how DHIS2 ecosystem apps are sustained; (3) contracts with civil-society organizations implementing Gavi's zero-dose programmes. Caveats: donor budgets tightened in 2025 and the 2025 Digital Square roadmap documents that global goods struggle with inconsistent funding and weak government ownership; pricing should be modest per country (tens of thousands of dollars per year) and the core must stay open so that a lapsed contract does not strand a ministry's data.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web application with backend, PostgreSQL/PostGIS, and role-based auth (district manager, facility nurse, CHW). Real public data available now: WUENIC country datasets (WHO immunization data portal and UNICEF data), WorldPop's CC-BY Nigeria DTP1/DTP3/MCV1 coverage and zero-dose estimates 2000–2024 (1 km GeoTIFFs plus ADM0–ADM2 CSVs at data.worldpop.org) and WorldPop under-1 population rasters, GRID3 settlement layers, healthsites.io facility data, Malaria Atlas Project travel-time friction surfaces, and the public DHIS2 'play' demo instance whose Web API and tracker can be used to build and test integration before any ministry access. Hard parts, stated honestly: real facility-level dose data require MoH DHIS2 credentials and a data-sharing agreement; child-level tracing handles personal data of minors and must comply with national data-protection laws (e.g., Nigeria NDPA, Kenya DPA) and ministry consent; denominators can be improved but never fully resolved, and showing coverage above 100% is politically sensitive; SMS costs and connectivity in remote areas; CHW workload must not increase (lists must be short and prioritized). No custom hardware, no regulatory approval (not a medical device), no logistics network is required.

**Measurable outcomes.** DTP1-to-DTP3 and MCV1-to-MCV2 dropout at participating facilities (target below 10%) versus matched facilities; number and share of overdue children traced and vaccinated within 30 days of due date; share of facilities whose reconciled denominator is consistent with DTP1 counts and whose reported coverage no longer exceeds 100%; zero-dose children found and started on the schedule through outreach to flagged settlements; cost per additional fully immunized child; measles cases in catchment areas over 2–3 years; CHW time per traced child.
