## 9. Mothers fall through the gap after birth: no structured follow-up in the days and weeks when most postpartum deaths occur

*Domain:* maternal-child (postnatal / postpartum care and maternal mortality) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** Women in the first six weeks to twelve months after birth, especially those discharged within 24 hours of a facility delivery with unrecognized risk (hypertensive disorders, haemorrhage, caesarean, anaemia, infection, depression). In low- and lower-middle-income countries, which account for 92% of the 260,000 maternal deaths (2023), roughly a third of women receive no postnatal check in the first days after birth; sub-Saharan Africa carries 70% of deaths and newborn postnatal-care coverage ranges from 97% (South Africa) to 43% (Ethiopia) in recent DHS analyses. In the United States, women in the 35.1% of counties that are maternity care deserts (2.3 million women of reproductive age, 38-minute average travel to a birthing hospital, more than 100 obstetric units closed since 2022), Medicaid-insured and Black women, and women with postpartum hypertension or perinatal mental illness. Also midwives, nurses and community health workers who have no list of who was discharged yesterday with which risks.

**Scale and severity.** WHO/UN estimates: 260,000 maternal deaths in 2023 (712 per day), MMR 197 per 100,000, progress stalled since 2016. WHO systematic analysis in The Lancet Global Health (2024/25, deaths 2009–2020): haemorrhage 27% and hypertensive disorders 16% of maternal deaths; most haemorrhage and sepsis deaths occur postpartum, and rising facility birth has not reduced these deaths; WHO (March 2025): about a third of women, mostly in lower-income countries, still miss essential postnatal checks in the first days after birth. UNICEF data (2023): only 69% of mothers and 68% of newborns received a postnatal check within the recommended period globally. A 2022 JBI systematic review of postpartum timing: of maternal deaths in the first 42 days, 48.9% occur on day 1, 24.5% on days 2–7 and 24.9% on days 8–42; 81% of eclampsia/hypertensive deaths occur within the first week and 61% of infection deaths on days 8–42, i.e. after discharge and before the traditional 6-week visit. Perinatal depression affects about 1 in 4 women in LMICs (JAMA Psychiatry 2023 meta-analysis, 616,708 women, 51 countries; 25.5% in lower-middle-income countries). United States (CDC Maternal Mortality Review Committees): 53% of pregnancy-related deaths occurred 7 days to 1 year after pregnancy (2017–2019 data), 47% in the 2020 38-state dataset; more than 80% judged preventable (84% in 2020); mental health conditions were the leading underlying cause (23%) and contributed to 26% of 2020 deaths; roughly 70% of US hypertension-related maternal deaths occur postpartum (secondary citation). Note: primary pages were read via domain-restricted search excerpts because direct fetches were blocked in this session.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) WHO's 2022 postnatal care recommendations specify contacts at 24 hours, day 3, days 7–14 and 6 weeks, but facilities do not keep risk-stratified lists of discharged women, so the schedule is not operationalized; community health worker programmes make routine visits without knowing who had a hypertensive disorder or haemorrhage. (2) The conventional 6-week visit is mis-timed: hypertensive deaths and readmissions cluster in the first week (a 2025 US series found all hypertension readmissions within 7 days of discharge), yet attendance at early postpartum clinic visits is poor (44% at Penn before its text programme). (3) The one well-evidenced model, Penn's Heart Safe Motherhood (home cuff plus twice-daily text-reported BP; readmissions for postpartum hypertension fell from 5% to 1%, 92% engagement), is single-condition, embedded in one health system's EHR and limited to a few US academic centres; home BP monitoring in LMICs is feasible (a 12-week programme in Nigeria showed high recruitment, fidelity and retention) but has not been scaled. (4) Mental health screening is rare and disconnected from physical follow-up; CDC committees found 100% of pregnancy-related mental-health deaths preventable and 63% in days 43–365. (5) Health information systems (EHRs, DHIS2) effectively stop at delivery; postpartum data are not captured, so no one can measure the gap. (6) Payment: US remote-monitoring reimbursement exists but is patchy and most Medicaid plans do not fund it; LMIC programmes depend on donors. (7) Obstetric unit closures lengthen distances, making the in-person 6-week visit even less likely in rural US areas.

**What people do today.** Women rely on family and traditional confinement practices and return to a facility only when symptoms are severe; nurses make ad hoc phone calls to women they remember as high-risk; paper discharge checklists and danger-sign counselling at discharge; CHW routine visits without prioritization; in the US, readmission through emergency departments, nurse home-visiting where it exists, and general pregnancy apps that carry no clinical follow-up; some hospitals run dedicated postpartum BP clinics that patients largely do not attend.

**Proposed intervention.** A risk-stratified postpartum follow-up registry and protocol engine, implemented from WHO's SMART Guidelines postnatal-care Digital Adaptation Kit (L2, published July 2025) and the WHO self-monitoring-of-blood-pressure SMART guideline, on FHIR. At discharge, a midwife or nurse enrols the mother and newborn with structured risk flags (hypertensive disorder, postpartum haemorrhage, caesarean, anaemia, preterm or low-birth-weight infant, HIV, adolescent, Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale score). The system generates a follow-up plan (24 h, day 3, days 7–14, 6 weeks, plus BP checks on days 3–10 for hypertensive women and an EPDS re-screen at 2 and 6 weeks), creates daily worklists for CHWs and nurses, collects BP readings and coded danger-sign checklists through SMS/USSD keypad responses or CHW data entry (structured fields, not free-text chat), applies explicit thresholds (e.g., BP 140/90 or 160/110, EPDS 13 or self-harm item, fever, heavy bleeding) to route cases to a clinician queue with time-to-response tracking, and closes each episode. The same core serves two markets: rural and Medicaid clinics in the US (postpartum hypertension and EPDS follow-up without needing a particular EHR) and MoH/CHW programmes in LMICs. What is novel: there is no open, multi-condition postpartum registry that implements the WHO schedule end to end; existing programmes are single-condition, proprietary and US-only, and mental health is never in the same workflow as hypertension and bleeding. It needs no AI; the value is enrolment at discharge, protocol timing, worklists and escalation. It depends on commodity BP cuffs (about USD 15–30, shareable at CHW level), which is a real but not custom-hardware dependency.

**Works across countries?.** High: the WHO postnatal-care recommendations and DAK are global, EPDS is validated in dozens of languages, and SMS/USSD channels and CHW programmes exist in most LMICs; the US version requires HIPAA compliance and optional EHR integration (FHIR) rather than a different product. Configuration differs by country (contact schedule, thresholds, referral pathways, language), and the tool is weakest where most births are at home with no discharge event, where enrolment must instead happen at the first CHW contact.

**Sustainable business model.** Two complementary payers, neither of them the mother. (1) United States: health systems and Medicaid managed-care organizations pay to reduce postpartum hypertension readmissions and to meet postpartum-care quality measures now that most states cover 12 months postpartum (CDC committees documented Medicaid extensions driven by their recommendations); remote physiologic monitoring CPT codes exist, so per-enrolled-patient or per-site subscriptions are realistic. (2) LMICs: ministry of health and donor procurement (UNFPA, Gates-funded MNCH programmes, World Bank/GFF maternal health projects, NGOs such as Jhpiego-type implementers) as an open-core global good aligned with WHO SMART Guidelines, which lowers adoption friction for ministries. Keeping the core open-source and patient-facing SMS free protects the social value; US margins can cross-subsidize LMIC deployments.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web application with backend, PostgreSQL, role-based auth (facility nurse, CHW, clinician, programme manager), scheduled jobs, and an SMS/USSD gateway (Twilio, Africa's Talking) in a cloud deployment. Real public inputs: the WHO PNC Digital Adaptation Kit (personas, workflows, decision logic, indicators) and the self-monitoring BP SMART guideline, the public-domain EPDS instrument, DHS Program API for baseline postnatal-care coverage by country/region, CDC MMRC data for US context, and the public DHIS2 demo for reporting integration tests. What is hard, honestly: this handles protected health information, so HIPAA (US) and national data-protection compliance, encryption and audit logging are required from day one; escalation thresholds must be reviewed and owned by a clinical partner; in the US the alerting logic should be designed to stay within FDA's non-device clinical decision support criteria (transparent rules, clinician makes the decision), and in LMICs ministry sign-off is needed; a facility or CHW programme partner is needed for any real use; BP cuffs must be supplied; outcome evidence on mortality requires large trials, so the MVP should measure process outcomes and readmissions. No custom hardware or logistics network is needed; the WHO PNC FHIR implementation guide (L3) was not yet published as of 2025, so FHIR profiles must be drafted from the DAK.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of enrolled women with a documented contact by day 3 and by day 14 (baseline: roughly a third miss any early check in LMICs); share of women with hypertensive disorders with at least one BP reading on days 3–10; median time from an abnormal BP or EPDS result to clinician action; postpartum hypertension readmissions within 42 days (US sites; benchmark 5% to 1% at Penn); EPDS-positive women referred and starting treatment; severe maternal morbidity, near-miss and death counts among enrolled women versus facility baseline; postnatal-care coverage in the catchment as reported to DHIS2 or DHS; CHW visits per high-risk woman versus low-risk woman (evidence of prioritization).

- https://www.gao.gov/assets/gao-26-108690.pdf
- https://www.marchofdimes.org/maternity-care-deserts-report

## 10. The first days after a psychiatric discharge or a self-harm emergency visit: the highest-risk window for suicide is also the least-owned part of the care pathway

*Domain:* mental-loneliness (suicide prevention / transitions of care) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.82

**Who experiences it.** People leaving psychiatric inpatient units or emergency departments after a suicide attempt or self-harm, and their families. In the US this is on the order of 600k-800k ED visits a year for self-harm or suspected attempts (CDC FastStats: ~616,000 ED visits for self-harm injury; CDC MMWR June 2026: 833,335 suspected-attempt ED visits identified in the NSSP sample 2021-2025, 15.8 per 10,000 ED visits, highest among girls 12-17 and rising among adults 26+ and 65+). In England it is roughly 70-75k eligible discharges a year from adult acute beds (RCPsych: 160,430 eligible discharges Apr 2020-May 2022). Globally, 73% of the 727,000 suicides in 2021 occurred in low- and middle-income countries (WHO), where structured aftercare barely exists. The population skews to young adults (suicide is the 3rd leading cause of death at 15-29, WHO) and to people with depression, substance use and recent crises.

**Scale and severity.** WHO: 727,000 suicide deaths in 2021 (likely an undercount because COVID disrupted registration), 3rd leading cause of death at ages 15-29; US: 48,824 suicide deaths in 2024 (CDC). Post-discharge risk is extreme and concentrated: pooled suicide rate in the first week after psychiatric discharge is ~2,950 per 100,000 person-years (95% CI 1,740-5,000; 24 studies; Chung et al., BMJ Open 2019) and ~1,132 per 100,000 in the first 3 months (Chung et al., JAMA Psychiatry 2017), versus a general-population rate around 9-14 per 100,000, i.e. roughly 100x. UK National Confidential Inquiry (NCISH, Feb 2025 report, data 2012-2022): ~198 patient suicides in 2022 occurred within 3 months of discharge (11% of all patient suicides), the number and rate have risen since 2017, and deaths peak on days 3-6 after discharge. Follow-up performance is poor on both sides of the Atlantic: NCQA HEDIS 7-day follow-up after mental-illness hospitalization averaged 38.5% nationally in measurement year 2023 (36.6% in 2022); NHS England's 72-hour follow-up standard (80% target) was met for only 73% of eligible adults as of April 2023 (Nuffield Trust) and 76% across Apr 2020-May 2022 (RCPsych).

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Nobody owns the gap: the inpatient team's responsibility ends at discharge and the outpatient appointment is often 2-4 weeks away, so the days of peak risk fall between two systems. (2) Follow-up is unfunded staff time: a Zero Suicide formative evaluation in EDs (2023) found 'limited resources needed to engage and re-engage at-risk patients ... and provide follow-up supportive contacts during transitions', and UCHealth's implementation cited leadership buy-in, resources and funding as barriers. (3) Evidence-based pieces exist but are rarely delivered together: Safety Planning Intervention plus structured phone follow-up within 72h roughly halved suicidal behaviour at 6 months in VA EDs (3.03% vs 5.29%, Stanley et al., JAMA Psychiatry 2018) and doubled treatment attendance; caring-contact messages have mixed evidence (Comtois 2019 RCT: null primary outcome, fewer attempts as a secondary outcome 9% vs 15%). Implementations are bespoke, home-grown (e.g., Nationwide Children's EHR-automated texts, VA Caring Letters) and not widely available to ordinary hospitals; searches surfaced no dominant specialised vendor. (4) Consumer safety-plan apps (Stay Alive, Beyond Now, MY3) are not connected to the discharging team and cannot tell anyone whether the 7-day appointment actually happened. (5) Non-clinical aftercare without strong linkage underperforms: Australia's Way Back Support Service found no reduction in repeat self-poisoning admissions in the Hunter historical-controlled trial (McGill 2022) and the national evaluation (Nous 2022) said referral effectiveness 'was constrained by the quality of those services'.

**What people do today.** Paper safety plans handed over at discharge (often lost); a single discharge phone call if staff have time; families improvising 24/7 watch and removing medicines or firearms; patients told to call a crisis line (988/111/Lifeline); clinics' spreadsheets to chase missed appointments; a few health systems hand-build caring-contact text programs inside their EHR; in LMICs, essentially nothing structured between discharge and the next crisis.

**Proposed intervention.** A hospital- and ED-deployable 'discharge-to-first-appointment safety bridge': a multi-tenant web platform that, at discharge, (a) digitises the Stanley-Brown safety plan and lets the patient share it with one or two chosen support people (who get their own simple view and a short role script), (b) schedules a protocolised, human-signed, non-demanding caring-contact series (SMS/email; day 1, 3, 7, 14, 30, then monthly to 12 months, mirroring the trial cadences), (c) tracks the two hard targets that payers and regulators already measure, contact within 72 hours and a mental-health appointment within 7/30 days, and raises a worklist alert to the discharging clinic when either has not happened, (d) routes any reply that matches an escalation rule to an on-call human and the national crisis line, and (e) emits the HEDIS FUH / NHS MHS80-style metrics. What is novel is the closed loop (did the follow-up actually occur, and who is accountable today) plus the support-person channel, packaged as a product any mid-sized hospital can adopt in weeks rather than a research project. It is not a chatbot: AI is optional (e.g., triaging free-text replies) and rules-based keyword escalation is sufficient for an MVP; the value is in scheduling, accountability and measurement.

**Works across countries?.** High. The same post-discharge risk curve is documented in Denmark, UK, US and Australia, and most high- and middle-income systems already have a named follow-up target (NHS 72-hour standard, HEDIS FUH in the US, Australia's federally funded aftercare programs, Joint Commission NPSG 15.01.01 follow-up expectation). The message protocol, safety plan and escalation logic are language-localisable; only the crisis-line numbers and the metric definitions change. In LMICs it would run through NGOs or district hospitals rather than payers, and SMS-first design matters.

**Sustainable business model.** Sell to institutions, never to patients. US: Medicaid managed-care plans and Medicare Advantage are scored and paid on HEDIS FUH, hospitals face Joint Commission suicide-prevention requirements, and 30-day readmissions are penalised, so a per-discharge or per-site SaaS fee is justifiable from existing budgets. UK: trusts are contractually measured on 72-hour follow-up; Australia: aftercare is federally commissioned. Pricing per enrolled discharge (tens of dollars) against a readmission that costs thousands keeps the social value intact; an open-source core with paid hosting/compliance would let LMIC NGOs use it free.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable by a small team as a web app with backend, database and auth: roles (clinician, patient, support person, on-call monitor), safety-plan editor, message scheduler on Twilio or equivalent, reply inbox with rule-based escalation, appointment-confirmation workflow (manual entry first; later SMART on FHIR / HL7 FHIR Appointment pull from EHRs, which have public sandboxes), and a metrics module reproducing the FUH / 72-hour definitions (NHS MHSDS spec is open; NCQA specs are licensed). Public data/APIs: FHIR sandboxes, 988/111 referral guidance, published trial message scripts. Hard parts (honest): HIPAA/GDPR and a BAA; 24/7 human coverage for replies (a governance problem, not a code problem); hospital procurement and EHR integration cycles; consent at a vulnerable moment; and proving outcomes needs registry linkage. It is a care-coordination/communication tool, so it is unlikely to be regulated as a medical device if it makes no diagnostic claims, but pilots will need institutional ethics/IRB sign-off. No custom hardware or physical logistics.

**Measurable outcomes.** % of discharges with a documented contact within 72 hours (baseline ~73% England, lower elsewhere); % with a mental-health appointment within 7 and 30 days (baseline 38.5% / ~60% US); repeat self-harm ED visits and psychiatric readmissions at 30, 90 and 180 days; suicidal behaviour at 6 months (trial benchmark: 3.0% vs 5.3%); safety-plan access events and support-person activation; time-to-escalation for flagged replies; and, with registry linkage, suicide deaths within 90 days of discharge.

- https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/suicide

## 11. Depression and anxiety go untreated in low- and middle-income countries because the only scalable workforce (lay counsellors) cannot be supervised, measured or kept to fidelity at scale

*Domain:* mental-loneliness (untreated depression/anxiety; global mental health) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.72

**Who experiences it.** Adults and adolescents with depression, anxiety and related distress in low- and lower-middle-income countries (sub-Saharan Africa, South Asia, humanitarian settings), where specialist care is effectively absent outside capital cities; and the non-specialist workforce that is asked to fill the gap: community health workers, 'grandmother' counsellors (Friendship Bench, Zimbabwe, Malawi, Kenya, Vietnam, Jordan), lay facilitators (StrongMinds Uganda/Zambia), PM+ helpers in refugee settings, plus the small number of psychologists expected to supervise them.

**Scale and severity.** WHO (World Mental Health Today, Sept 2025; data 2021): more than 1 billion people live with a mental disorder; 71% of people with psychosis receive no services; governments spend a median 2.1% of health budgets on mental health, unchanged since 2017 (Mental Health Atlas 2024). Workforce: global median 13.5 specialised mental-health workers per 100,000, but barely 1 per 100,000 in low-income countries versus 60+ in high-income countries. Treatment coverage for major depression: minimally adequate treatment ranged from 23% in high-income to 3% in low- and lower-middle-income countries (Moitra et al., PLOS Medicine 2022, 84 countries, 2000-2019). 73% of the world's 727,000 suicides (2021) occur in LMICs. Task-sharing works but scale is fragile: Friendship Bench reports 800k-1M clients since 2016 and 5,000+ trained counsellors in Zimbabwe; a 2026 systematic review of Friendship Bench implementation across Africa lists 'limited supervision' and 'inadequate incentives' as barriers and finds cost-effectiveness under enhanced supervision but not under basic internal-champion models. The 2025 US aid cuts removed services for ~750,000 people and training for ~50,000 prospective mental-health workers across 131 programs in 32 countries (GMHAN/MHIN survey), on top of a public financing gap estimated at US$200bn+.

**Why existing solutions fail.** The binding constraint on task-shared psychological care is not training but what happens after training: sustained supervision, fidelity monitoring, outcome measurement and safe escalation. Reviews of non-specialist-delivered interventions in LMICs repeatedly note that 'empirical evaluations of supervision are scarce' (perinatal review), that only 9 of the youth-intervention studies provided ongoing supervision, and that 'sustainable models of training and supervision' are the stated next step. WHO/UNICEF's EQUIP platform (2022) solved competency assessment at the point of training (794 training programmes, 36 countries, 3,760 trainees, 10,001 assessments by March 2024) but stops at training; it does not run the service. Harvard/Sangath's EMPOWER adds digital training and peer supervision but is still at pilot scale (e.g., 40+ CHWs in Gujarat) and India-focused. Most programs otherwise run on paper registers, Excel and generic data-collection tools (CommCare/ODK), which do not encode stepped-care rules, supervision workflows or validated outcome trajectories, so donors cannot see quality and ministries cannot justify budget lines. Funding shocks then hit the least-documented programs first.

**What people do today.** Paper session logs and symptom checklists photographed on phones; WhatsApp groups for ad hoc supervision; monthly in-person group supervision where fuel and time allow; counsellors deciding escalation (e.g., suicidal risk) by instinct; programs adapting CommCare forms per project; outcome data assembled retrospectively for donor reports; clients travelling to district hospitals or going untreated.

**Proposed intervention.** An offline-first, open-core 'practice and quality system' for task-shared mental health care: per-client measurement-based care (PHQ-9/GAD-7 and locally validated tools such as SSQ-14, scored and trended per session), a stepped-care rule engine (automatic flags for non-response, deterioration or suicide risk with a time-bound referral task), structured supervision workflows (caseload summaries for group peer supervision, random session sampling for fidelity checklists, specialist escalation queues) and program-level outcome reporting that ministries and funders can audit. Differentiation versus EQUIP (training-stage competencies) and EMPOWER (training plus peer supervision in pilot) is that this runs the ongoing service and makes quality legible to the people who fund it, and versus CommCare that the clinical logic, supervision ratios and outcome definitions are first-class rather than rebuilt per project. It is useful with no AI at all; any later AI use would be limited to summarising free-text case notes for supervisors and would not involve voice or speech.

**Works across countries?.** Designed for it: the same lay-counsellor model (Friendship Bench, PM+, Thinking Healthy, StrongMinds' group IPT) is used across Zimbabwe, Malawi, Kenya, Uganda, Zambia, India, Pakistan, Nepal, Vietnam, Jordan and refugee settings in Lebanon and Bangladesh. Instruments and workflows are localised per program; the WHO mhGAP pathways and EQUIP competency lists provide common scaffolding. Also applicable to high-income peer-support and community-health programs that have the same supervision problem.

**Sustainable business model.** Open-source core (so cash-poor programs and ministries are never locked out) with paid hosting, data-protection compliance, integrations and support sold to NGOs, ministries of health and donors on a per-site or per-counsellor basis; funders increasingly require outcome data, which is exactly what the product produces, so the buyer is the program budget rather than the client. Honest caveat: the paying market shrank in 2025 with the aid cuts, so revenue should be anchored in multi-year philanthropic and multilateral funders (Global Fund-style mechanisms, Grand Challenges Canada, Wellcome) and in middle-income government programs, not in short grants.

**MVP feasibility.** High for a small team: a multi-tenant web app (auth, roles for counsellor/supervisor/program manager, client records with consent, session forms with scored instruments, rule-based flags and tasks, supervision scheduling and fidelity checklists, exportable reports), built as a progressive web app with local-first sync for intermittent connectivity. Public resources: PHQ-9 and GAD-7 (public domain), WHO mhGAP and PM+ manuals, EQUIP/ENACT competency tools (free), published Friendship Bench and PM+ protocols. Hard parts: offline sync and conflict resolution; data protection for sensitive health data across jurisdictions; the incremental value over CommCare must be proven in a real program; adoption requires a launch partner NGO and field-tested forms in local languages. No hardware, no regulatory pre-approval, no physical logistics, but field deployment support is unavoidable.

**Measurable outcomes.** Supervision coverage (% of counsellors receiving scheduled supervision each month; sustained supervisor-to-counsellor ratio); % of sessions with a recorded outcome measure; PHQ-9 response/remission rates versus published trial benchmarks; dropout before session 3; median time from screening to first session; % of suicide-risk flags with a documented referral within 24-48 hours; fidelity checklist pass rate on sampled sessions; cost per client in remission; and, for the field, the share of programs that survive a funding shock with auditable outcome data.

- https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789240113817
- https://ruralindiaonline.org/en/library/resource/mental-health-atlas-2024/

## 12. Family caregivers burn out invisibly: they are rarely identified by the health system, rarely trained, and almost never connected to the respite and support programs that already exist

*Domain:* mental-loneliness (caregiver burnout; isolation of elderly and their carers) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.78

**Who experiences it.** Unpaid family caregivers of older, disabled or chronically ill adults, disproportionately women aged 45-64 and increasingly adults under 50 (one in three US caregivers; 47% of under-50 caregivers are 'sandwich generation'). US: 63 million caregivers, about 1 in 4 adults (AARP/NAC, July 2025); ~12 million of them care for someone with dementia (Alzheimer's Association 2025). Europe: informal carers provide ~80% of all long-term care (Eurocarers). UK: Carers UK's State of Caring 2024 found 57% of carers feel frequently overwhelmed and 35% report poor mental health, up from 27% a year earlier. The problem is global and growing with population ageing; WHO counts 55M+ people with dementia, 60% in LMICs, almost all cared for at home.

**Scale and severity.** US: 63M caregivers in 2025, up 45% (nearly 20M people) since 2015; 24% provide 40+ hours a week; 44% provide high-intensity care; 20% report fair or poor health that they attribute directly to caregiving; 39% say respite would help but only 13% use it (14% in 2020; 16.45% in 2015-2021 national data) (AARP/NAC Caregiving in the US 2025; AARP PPI 2024). Dementia: 59% of dementia caregivers report high or very high emotional stress; ~19 billion unpaid hours valued at $413-446bn in 2024-25 (Alzheimer's Association). Europe: carers' prevalence of mental-health problems is ~20% higher than non-carers and the value of informal care (~2.5% of EU GDP) exceeds public LTC spending (1.7%) (Eurocarers). Australia's 2024 National Carer Survey ranked planned and emergency respite among the highest unmet needs. Public programs are tiny relative to need: the US National Family Caregiver Support Program serves ~700,000 caregivers a year (~1% of caregivers) and Lifespan Respite is funded at ~$10-20M nationally. Health-system touchpoints are missed: under the CARE Act (adopted in 40+ US states) a UPMC study found only 22.2% of 1,801 identified caregivers received discharge education and training, and a 2026 Health Affairs piece reports only 22% of caregivers get training for complex medical tasks.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Caregivers do not self-identify and no one in the clinical pathway is tasked with finding them, so strain is never screened; the CARE Act created a legal duty but compliance is weak and untracked. (2) Evidence-based psychoeducation delivered as self-guided websites fails on adherence: WHO's iSupport, the flagship free online program for dementia carers, showed no benefit on depression or distress in the 352-carer UK RCT (Lancet Regional Health Europe, Dec 2024) because most participants barely used it; the process evaluation calls for blended delivery with 'regular contact with a health professional'. (3) Respite supply is scarce and rationed (waitlists, direct-care workforce shortages), and the little that exists is not matched to the people who need it; higher-income, better-educated caregivers are the ones who find it. (4) Existing software serves the wrong buyer or the wrong user: employer care-concierge services (Wellthy, Cariloop) reach only insured employees; consumer coordination apps (ianacare, Carely, Jointly) rely on the exhausted caregiver to find and set them up; SDOH referral platforms (Unite Us, findhelp) are built around the patient, not the carer. (5) Benefits and assessments that carers are entitled to (UK statutory Carer's Assessment, Carer's Allowance, US Medicaid waiver respite, VA caregiver support, Australia's Carer Gateway) are fragmented, jurisdiction-specific and hard to navigate while caring 40 hours a week.

**What people do today.** Reducing paid work or quitting; using personal savings (the AARP state analysis documents heavy out-of-pocket costs); relying on relatives or neighbours for ad hoc breaks; Facebook and WhatsApp caregiver groups; hotlines (Alzheimer's Association 24/7 line, Carers UK helpline); learning medical tasks from YouTube; crisis-driven ED visits for the care recipient when the carer collapses; in the UK and Australia, waiting months for a statutory carer's assessment.

**Proposed intervention.** A caregiver identification-to-support loop for health systems, memory clinics and carer organisations: at the clinical touchpoint (hospital discharge, dementia diagnosis, GP annual review) the care recipient's record spawns a caregiver record (CARE Act compliance built in), the caregiver completes a 2-minute strain screen (Zarit-4 / Caregiver Strain Index), the system matches them against a maintained, jurisdiction-specific catalogue of concrete supports with eligibility rules (respite grants and vouchers, carer allowances and assessments, training sessions, counselling, Medicaid waiver or VA programs), generates the applications or referrals, schedules brief human check-ins by a coordinator or trained peer (2, 6 and 12 weeks; the 'blended' element the iSupport evidence says is missing), and tracks whether each support was actually received, with escalation when strain rises. What is novel is treating the caregiver as a patient of record with a closed-loop referral and a measured outcome, rather than as a consumer who must find an app; nothing in it requires AI, though AI could later help maintain the benefits catalogue from public sources.

**Works across countries?.** The underlying pattern (large unpaid-care population, scarce respite, statutory entitlements with low uptake, no identification at clinical touchpoints) is documented in the US, UK, EU, Australia and Japan. The screening instruments are validated in many languages; the supports catalogue must be built per jurisdiction (US states, UK local authorities, Australian Carer Gateway regions), which is the main localisation cost. In LMICs, where formal respite is nearly absent, the product would reduce to identification, peer check-ins and community resources, with lower leverage.

**Sustainable business model.** Institutional buyers with an existing cost or duty: US hospitals (CARE Act compliance, 30-day readmission penalties; caregiver unmet needs and burden are associated with higher rehospitalisation and ED revisit odds, though the evidence base is small and imprecise), Medicare Advantage plans (caregiver supports are allowable supplemental benefits), UK local authorities and ICBs (statutory carer-assessment backlogs), Australian PHNs and Carer Gateway providers, and dementia charities. Per-site or per-enrolled-caregiver SaaS fees; never monetise caregiver data; a free tier for carer charities preserves access. Honest caveat: public-sector sales cycles are slow, and navigation cannot create respite supply where none exists, so the product must be sold on identification, training completion and strain reduction rather than promising respite hours.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, database and auth: caregiver registration (standalone or triggered from a discharge/diagnosis event), validated screens with scoring, a structured supports catalogue with eligibility rules (seeded from public sources: US Eldercare Locator and ACL program lists, state Medicaid waiver pages, 211 directories where they publish Open Referral HSDS data, VA Caregiver Support, GOV.UK carer pages and council directories, Australia's Carer Gateway), referral generation and status tracking, a coordinator check-in worklist with scripts, and outcome reporting. Hard parts: keeping the catalogue current (an editorial process, partly automatable); jurisdiction sprawl; HIPAA/GDPR for a record that links two people; EHR integration (SMART on FHIR) for automatic triggering; proving outcomes needs a pilot site. No hardware or regulatory approval; the respite-supply problem is a physical-logistics limitation the software cannot solve and should not claim to.

**Measurable outcomes.** % of discharges/diagnoses with a caregiver recorded and screened (baseline: unmeasured in most systems); % of identified caregivers who receive training before discharge (baseline ~22%); % of enrolled caregivers who receive at least one concrete support within 60 days and respite use (baseline 13-16%); change in Zarit burden and PHQ-9 at 3 and 6 months; caregiver-reported ability to take a break; care-recipient 30-day readmissions and ED revisits; delayed institutionalisation; caregiver employment retention; and statutory carer-assessment completion times where applicable.

- https://www.aarp.org/content/dam/aarp/ppi/topics/ltss/family-caregiving/caregiving-in-us-2025.doi.10.26419-2fppi.00373.001.pdf
- https://www.aarp.org/press/releases/2025-10-28-new-data-expose-financial-strain-of-family-caregiving.html

## 13. Skilled migrants' and refugees' qualifications are not recognised, so they work far below their training ('brain waste')

*Domain:* education — credential recognition for migrants and refugees / adult skills · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Tertiary-educated immigrants and refugees in high-income destination countries, above all those whose degree was earned abroad. Concretely: foreign-educated nurses, physicians, engineers, teachers and accountants from the Philippines, India, Nigeria, Syria, Ukraine, Afghanistan, Venezuela and Sudan living in the US, Canada and the EU (worst in Greece, Italy, Spain); refugees who fled without transcripts or whose universities no longer exist (Syria, Afghanistan, Sudan); and refugee-hosting countries in Africa (Uganda, Kenya, Zambia) where recognition capacity is a handful of part-time evaluators. Women and Black and Latino graduates face higher underutilisation (MPI).

**Scale and severity.** United States: about 2 million college-educated immigrants were in jobs requiring no more than a high-school diploma or unemployed as of 2019 (Migration Policy Institute); MPI's 2016 'Untapped Talent' study (2009-13 Census data) put forgone wages at $39.4 billion per year and lost taxes above $10 billion per year; 29% of foreign-educated immigrant graduates were underutilised vs 21% of US-educated ones. EU: 39.4% of employed non-EU citizens with tertiary education were over-qualified for their job in 2023, almost double the ~21% rate for nationals; Greece 69.6%, Italy 64.1%, Spain 56.0% (Eurostat, July 2024 release); 39.6% in 2024, down from 45.9% in 2014. Canada: 25.8% of immigrants aged 25-64 with a foreign degree worked in jobs needing at most high school in 2021, twice the Canadian-born/educated rate; only 36.5% of immigrants with a foreign nursing degree worked as RNs or closely related jobs (Statistics Canada, 2021 Census). Licensure for an internationally educated nurse in British Columbia can cost about $3,000 (C.D. Howe Institute, 2024). Refugee recognition capacity is tiny: Uganda's UNESCO Qualifications Passport programme (from May 2024) drew 220 applications, assessed 60 and issued 49 passports with only eight part-time evaluators; passports are issued in only Zambia, Kenya and Uganda (The Observer, Uganda). Note: the sponsor's network policy blocked direct page fetches, so these figures were taken from search-result excerpts of the named primary sources rather than the full reports.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Credential evaluation services (WES, ECE, CGFNS, ENIC-NARIC centres) are document-centric, paid per report, and answer only 'what is this degree equivalent to'; they do not tell a nurse from Lagos what the licensure sequence, cost, exam, bridging course and timeline are in Ohio versus Ontario, and a person often pays for several evaluations for different purposes. Licensing is fragmented across hundreds of state/provincial regulators and professional bodies with no shared machine-readable data, so settlement agencies (Upwardly Global, ACCES, IMPRINT) hand-maintain PDFs and web pages that go stale. Employers cannot interpret foreign credentials, so they default to 'local experience required'. Refugees without documents fall outside the whole system; the UNESCO Qualifications Passport exists but has been issued in only a few countries and in the dozens, constrained by evaluator capacity. Information failure compounds the real barriers (language, exam cost, bridging seats): MPI found English proficiency to be the strongest predictor of underemployment, so credential pathway guidance must be integrated with language-level planning rather than treated in isolation.

**What people do today.** Taking survival jobs (driving, care work, warehouse) for years while saving for exams; re-doing an entire degree locally; paying $100-$300 per evaluation multiple times; relying on diaspora WhatsApp groups and settlement caseworkers for licensing lore; switching to a less regulated adjacent occupation (nurse to personal support worker, physician to medical assistant); volunteering or unpaid internships to get 'local experience'; for refugees, sworn statements and interview-based assessment where a Qualifications Passport pilot happens to exist.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, pathway-centric credential navigator: a structured, versioned, machine-readable graph that maps (origin country, institution, qualification) to destination-country comparability, and (target occupation, jurisdiction) to the exact licensure sequence — prerequisites, documents, exams, bridging programmes, fees, statutory timelines, appeal routes — with a cost/time estimator, a personal checklist with document-request generation to the issuing institution, and an employer-facing 'credential explainer' page a candidate can attach to applications. What is novel: (1) it models the occupation-by-jurisdiction pathway, not the paper, so a user sees total cost and months-to-licence before paying anyone; (2) the pathway data is open and crowd-verified by settlement navigators and licensing bodies with change-tracking, instead of locked in evaluators' proprietary files; (3) it is explicitly built for the no-documents case, structuring the UNESCO Qualifications Passport style interview evidence into a portable record. It needs no AI to be useful; LLM extraction can merely speed up ingesting regulator pages, with human review.

**Works across countries?.** The schema (qualification → comparability; occupation × jurisdiction → steps) is universal; the content is jurisdiction-specific, so launch should be corridor-first (e.g., Philippines/India/Nigeria nurses to the US and Canada; Syrian/Ukrainian engineers to Germany), then expand. Supporting public data exists in most OECD countries and the EU (ENIC-NARIC country profiles, EQF/NQF referencing reports, Europass, ESCO, O*NET, CareerOneStop licence data) and the UNESCO regional recognition conventions give a legal frame in refugee-hosting countries.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for individuals (never sell applicant data). Revenue from employers and staffing firms (hospital systems recruiting internationally educated nurses pay for verified pathway status and credential explainers), settlement agencies and immigrant-serving nonprofits (SaaS caseload tools), and licensing bodies and governments (pre-screening that reduces incomplete applications; Canada's Foreign Credential Recognition Program and EU integration funds already pay for this kind of work). Open pathway data as a public good; paid tier is workflow, verification and employer integrations.

**MVP feasibility.** High. A web app with backend, Postgres and auth can ship with: a pathway knowledge base for two or three corridors (hand-built from regulator sites, ENIC-NARIC pages, NQF referencing reports), accreditation lookups against open datasets (US DAPIP accreditation database, ENIC-NARIC country pages, WHED for institution lists — WHED bulk access needs an IAU licence), occupation mapping via the ESCO API and O*NET Web Services, US state licence requirements via CareerOneStop's License Finder API, and a document-request generator. Hard parts: keeping hundreds of regulator pages current (needs change-monitoring plus human curation), staying clearly on the 'information, not legal advice' side with disclaimers and sourcing on every step, winning cooperation from issuing institutions for verification, and the refugee no-documents flow which requires partner evaluators. No hardware, no regulatory approval and no physical logistics are required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of registered users employed in an occupation matching their qualification level within 12 months (versus ~25-40% over-qualification baselines); months from arrival to licence application and to licence for tracked cohorts; wage change six and twelve months after a pathway is completed; number of evaluation fees avoided or pathway abandonments prevented (dollars saved per user); regulator-side reduction in incomplete or rejected applications; number of verified pathway records maintained and their staleness (median days since last regulator check).

## 14. Children in low- and middle-income countries cannot read by age 10, in large part because instruction and reading material are not in a language they understand

*Domain:* education — learning poverty, foundational literacy, language of instruction · *Researcher confidence:* 0.65

**Who experiences it.** Primary-school children in low- and middle-income countries, overwhelmingly in Sub-Saharan Africa (where learning poverty is highest) and South Asia; most acutely children whose home language differs from the official language of instruction — e.g., Francophone West Africa where French is used from grade 1, multilingual Kenya, Uganda, Tanzania, Nigeria, Ethiopia, Mozambique, DR Congo, the Sahel, India's tribal-language belts, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea. Rural, poor and minority-language children are hit hardest, and their teachers, who often lack any graded text in the local language, are the second affected group.

**Scale and severity.** About 70% of 10-year-olds in low- and middle-income countries could not read and understand a simple text in 2022, up from 57% pre-pandemic; 89% in Sub-Saharan Africa; estimated lifetime earnings loss of $21 trillion, about 17% of today's global GDP (World Bank, UNESCO, UNICEF, FCDO, USAID, Gates Foundation, 'The State of Global Learning Poverty: 2022 Update', June 2022; part of the 70% is modelled rather than measured). UNESCO's GEM Report estimates 40% of the world's population does not have access to education in a language they speak or understand ('If you don't understand, how can you learn?', 2016, still cited by UNESCO in 2026); in Côte d'Ivoire 55% of grade-5 pupils who spoke the test language at home learned the basics of reading versus 25% of those who did not. UNESCO's 2026 International Mother Language Day material notes only a few hundred of the world's ~7,000 languages are used in education systems and fewer than 100 are represented digitally. Supply of texts is thin: the Global Book Alliance launched in 2018 targeting 50,000 titles in 100 languages; by 2021 the Global Digital Library held just over 6,000 titles in about 90 languages (now '100+ languages'), and most are translated storybooks rather than decodable texts aligned to a national phonics sequence. Context: 272 million children and youth were out of school in 2023 (UNESCO, 2025 SDG 4 Scorecard), so the in-school learning crisis sits alongside a growing access crisis. The specific number of children with zero books in their home language is not reliably quantified ('millions', Global Book Alliance), which is why confidence is moderate. Network policy blocked direct page fetches; figures are from search-result excerpts of the named sources.

**Why existing solutions fail.** The evidence on what works is unusually clear — the 2023 GEEAP 'Smart Buys' report rates structured pedagogy (scripted lesson plans plus learning materials plus ongoing teacher support) and teaching at the right level as 'Great Buys', and hardware-only technology as a 'Bad Buy' — but implementation at scale fails on the materials leg: ministries adopt mother-tongue policies for the early grades (Kenya, Uganda, Ethiopia, Senegal, Nigeria, Mozambique) without a supply of grade-appropriate decodable and leveled texts in each language, because commercial publishers see no market in languages with a few hundred thousand speakers, donor projects (Tusome, SHRP, RARA) produce titles for one language set and end, and open repositories (Global Digital Library, StoryWeaver, African Storybook, Bloom) are translation-driven, so titles are not sequenced to the grapheme-phoneme order a given orthography and curriculum teach. Books that reach schools are often locked in cupboards or pitched above the children's decoding level; the 'one textbook per 3-10 children' problem persists in many systems. RTI's Uganda local-language RCT showed P4 learners 1.5-6 times more likely to read 40+ words per minute in programme schools, but that depended on bespoke materials that were never generalised to other languages.

**What people do today.** Teachers write words on the board and drill by rote; children memorise texts in a language they do not speak; NGOs translate a few hundred storybooks and print them in small runs; teachers hand-copy or photocopy single textbooks; parents buy cheap English/French primers that children cannot decode; community libraries rely on donated foreign-language books; some ministries print a single reader per language per grade with no leveled progression; religious texts become the de facto reading material.

**Proposed intervention.** An open 'decodable reader factory' web platform: for a language, encode its orthography (grapheme inventory, syllable structure, tone marking) and the national or project phonics scope-and-sequence; the system then validates and levels any submitted text by the exact set of graphemes, syllable types and high-frequency words taught up to each week, flags words that are not yet decodable, and assembles print-ready, low-ink A5/A4 booklets (plus phone-readable HTML) from community-authored or openly licensed stories (Global Digital Library, StoryWeaver and African Storybook corpora are CC-BY). Native-speaker reviewers and teachers approve texts through a review workflow; ministries get a per-language dashboard of coverage gaps (which weeks of the sequence have no texts). What is novel: existing repositories level by vocabulary and sentence length, not by decodability against a specific orthography and curriculum order, and none give a ministry a gap map. The core value — orthography-aware leveling, review workflow, print-ready output — needs no AI; LLM drafting can be offered for higher-resource languages only, behind mandatory human review, because model quality in most target languages is poor.

**Works across countries?.** Yes; the engine is language-agnostic by design, and the same need recurs in Francophone West Africa, East Africa, Ethiopia, South Asia, the Pacific and indigenous-language programmes in Latin America and Canada. Each deployment needs a linguist or ministry curriculum unit to supply the scope-and-sequence, which is the genuine localisation cost.

**Sustainable business model.** Core platform and generated texts remain openly licensed (CC-BY) so governments and NGOs can print freely. Revenue from: ministries and donor-funded reading programmes (World Bank, GPE, USAID, FCDO already budget for materials development) paying for language onboarding, custom level packs and gap analyses; publishers paying for the leveling/validation API; print partners paying for print-ready layout bundles; philanthropic grants for the lowest-resource languages. Avoid paywalling children's or teachers' access to the texts themselves.

**MVP feasibility.** Medium-high for the software; the last mile is the limiting factor. A small team can build the web app (auth, Postgres, text analyzer, review workflow, PDF generation) and seed it with CC-BY corpora from the Global Digital Library, StoryWeaver and African Storybook, starting with two or three languages that already have published scope-and-sequences (e.g., Kiswahili, Hausa, Luganda, Chichewa). Hard parts stated honestly: orthography and tokenisation rules must be hand-built per language; quality assurance requires native-speaker reviewers who must be recruited and paid; the output only improves reading if printed, distributed and used, which depends on ministry or NGO logistics outside the software; measuring impact needs an EGRA-style field assessment partner. No regulatory approval or hardware needed.

**Measurable outcomes.** Oral reading fluency and reading-comprehension gains on EGRA in partner schools versus comparison schools; share of grade-2/3 children reaching the national fluency benchmark; titles available per language at each level of the scope-and-sequence (and weeks of the sequence with zero texts); books per child in home language actually in classrooms; cost per 1,000 printed pages delivered; teacher usage (texts read per week per class) from spot-check logs.

- https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/education/publication/state-of-global-learning-poverty
- https://reliefweb.int/report/world/if-you-don-t-understand-how-can-you-learn
- https://www.un.org/en/node/214628

## 15. Teachers are posted to the wrong schools: misallocation leaves rural and poor schools without teachers even where national supply exists

*Domain:* education — teacher shortages and deployment · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** Children in rural, remote and poor schools in South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa who sit in single-teacher or severely under-staffed schools while urban schools hold surplus staff. Concretely: roughly 4 million pupils in India's ~111,000 single-teacher schools (concentrated in Madhya Pradesh, Andhra Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand, Himachal); pupils in remote schools in Tanzania, Malawi, The Gambia, Sierra Leone, Ghana, Uganda and Mozambique; and the teachers themselves, who face opaque, often patronage-driven transfer systems and hardship postings without support. Secondary-level subject gaps (maths, science) are a further layer.

**Scale and severity.** Globally, 44 million additional primary and secondary teachers are needed by 2030 — 12.9 million primary and 31.1 million secondary — with 15 million in Sub-Saharan Africa and 7.8 million in South Asia; primary-teacher attrition almost doubled from 4.62% in 2015 to 9.06% in 2022 (UNESCO and Teacher Task Force, Global Report on Teachers, 2024). A substantial share of the within-country gap is distribution, not supply: a World Bank brief on Tanzania, Malawi and The Gambia estimates that 28% of the variation in staffing between schools in the region cannot be explained by variation in enrolment, and that remote schools are frequently understaffed compared with those near towns; in Tanzania only 36% of schools were in the acceptable pupil-teacher-ratio range. India's UDISE+ 2023-24 reports 110,971 single-teacher schools serving nearly 40 lakh (about 4 million) students, while 31,981 teachers were posted to 12,964 schools with zero enrolment; in 2024-25 single-teacher schools still numbered 104,125 and zero-enrolment schools 7,993 (Ministry of Education UDISE+ reports, via PIB and Careers360). A 2026 UNICEF Innocenti note on Sierra Leone attributes rural and gender disparities partly to uneven deployment practices. Caveat: the 28% and 36% figures were seen only in excerpts because direct fetches were blocked by network policy, and the global 44-million figure mixes supply and attrition needs with distribution problems.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Policy and donor attention goes to headline supply (recruit more teachers, national PTR) rather than equitable distribution within districts; transfer and posting decisions are discretionary, politically mediated and sometimes corrupt, so teachers migrate toward towns and schools with influence; hardship allowances are small or unpaid; school-census data (UDISE+, annual school censuses) exist but are not connected to the moment a posting or transfer is decided, and 'rationalisation' drives are manual, periodic, contested by unions and reversed. A few Indian states run online counselling-based transfer rounds, but these optimise individual preference lists, not school-level deficits, and provide no public audit of fairness. The 2024 UNESCO report itself notes attrition data are missing in many countries, so planners lack the forward view needed to pre-empt vacancies.

**What people do today.** One teacher teaches all grades in multigrade mode or leaves classes unsupervised; parents and communities pay para-teachers or volunteers; head teachers borrow staff informally from nearby schools; districts post newly recruited or contract teachers to the hardest schools and lose them within a year; children walk to larger schools or drop out; states periodically merge or close small schools; teachers pay intermediaries to secure urban postings.

**Proposed intervention.** An open teacher-allocation and transfer-fairness engine that ingests public school-census data (school-level enrolment by grade, sanctioned and in-position teachers by subject, location coordinates, school type), computes each school's norm-based entitlement (e.g., India's RTE 30:1 primary / 35:1 upper primary, subject-wise norms for secondary, or a country's own PTR bands), produces an auditable surplus/deficit map at district level, and then runs a constrained optimisation that proposes transfer plans minimising teacher relocation distance and disruption subject to subject match, gender balance, hardship rotation, spousal and medical constraints — with a simulation mode so officials and unions can see the effect of a rule change before a transfer round, and a public fairness report after it. Novel elements: it reframes transfers as a transparent, two-sided matching problem with school deficits as first-class participants (not just teacher preference lists); it exposes the 'teachers in zero-enrolment schools' and single-teacher-school deficits as live, geocoded indicators; and it is EMIS-agnostic through a simple import schema. It is a classic operations-research product and needs no AI to work.

**Works across countries?.** Yes wherever a school census with teacher counts exists: India (UDISE+ is public at school level with coordinates), many Sub-Saharan countries (Sierra Leone, Ghana, Malawi, Tanzania, Kenya publish annual school census data or share it with partners), and Latin American ministries. Norms and transfer rules are configured per jurisdiction; the political economy of transfers is the main cross-country variable and must be handled through adoption design (union and district consultation), not code.

**Sustainable business model.** Government contracts with state or national education departments (teacher management reforms are routinely financed by World Bank, GPE and FCDO programmes, so procurement budgets exist); open-core model where the deficit-mapping and public fairness reports are free and open-source to build trust, while the optimisation engine, integrations with HR/payroll systems and support are paid. Guardrails: the product should never be sold as teacher surveillance or performance scoring, and all teacher-level data stays inside the government tenant.

**MVP feasibility.** High for the analysis layer, medium for adoption. A small team can ship a web app with auth, Postgres/PostGIS and an optimisation worker (OR-Tools) using India's public UDISE+ school-level data (enrolment, teachers, lat/long) for one or two states, reproducing the single-teacher and zero-enrolment indicators and generating distance-minimising transfer proposals under RTE norms. Hard parts: UDISE+ data are annual and self-reported, so the live picture needs a ministry feed; transfer rules differ by state and must be configured with officials; the software proposes but cannot move a teacher, so impact depends on a government partner adopting it in a real transfer round; no hardware, regulatory approval or logistics network is required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Number of single-teacher schools and pupils in them in partner districts; share of schools within the norm PTR band (versus Tanzania's 36% type baseline); teachers posted to zero- or near-zero-enrolment schools; share of between-school staffing variation explained by enrolment (R-squared before and after a transfer round); average relocation distance per transfer and share of transfers contested or reversed; subsequent attendance and foundational learning scores in schools moved out of deficit.

**Sources consulted:**

## 16. Extreme heat kills isolated, housebound and elderly people at home even when heat warnings were issued

*Domain:* climate-disaster (extreme heat / early warning to action) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** People aged 65+ (and especially 75+) living alone without cooling, disproportionately women: in Europe's summer 2024 heat-death toll, deaths among women were 46.7% higher than among men and the burden was concentrated in those over 75 (Janos et al., Nature Medicine 2025). Geographically: Southern Europe (Italy >19,000 deaths in 2024; Spain 6,743; Germany 6,282; per-capita worst in Greece 574/million, Bulgaria 530/million, Serbia 379/million); temperate cities that lack air conditioning (Pacific Northwest 2021: Multnomah County found 78% of people who died at home lived alone, none of the 54 confirmed hyperthermia deaths had central AC, some bodies were not found for up to a week); South Asian cities and informal settlements where deaths are barely counted (India officially recorded 159 heat deaths in 2024 against ~48,000 reported heatstroke cases; a HeatWatch media count found 733 heatstroke deaths in 17 states Mar-Jun 2024; in 2025 NCDC confirmed only 14 deaths out of 7,192 suspected cases). Outdoor and informal workers are a second large group: ILO (2024) estimates 2.4 billion workers (71% of the global labour force) are exposed to excessive heat, causing ~22.85 million occupational injuries and 18,970 deaths a year, nine in ten exposures happening outside declared heatwaves.

**Scale and severity.** Lancet Countdown 2025 (reported via WHO/PIK): heat-related deaths averaged 546,000 per year over 2012-2021 (a modelled estimate), with the heat-mortality rate up 23% since the 1990s; the economic cost of heat deaths among over-65s hit a record US$261 billion. Europe alone: 62,775 heat-related deaths in summer 2024 (95% CI 36,765-84,379), after 67,873 in 2022 and 50,798 in 2023, i.e. more than 181,000 deaths across three summers, two-thirds in Southern Europe (Nature Medicine, Sept 2025). India's true toll is unknown: official counts are in the low hundreds per year while modelled estimates (GBD 2019 as reported by Indian press: ~228,000) and academic excess-mortality studies (3,400-30,000 excess deaths per heatwave episode, per The Wire's summary) are orders of magnitude higher; former WHO chief scientist Soumya Swaminathan has said India 'most likely' underreports because doctors use a narrow definition. Occupational heat: 18,970 deaths and 2.09 million DALYs per year (ILO 2024). The WMO/UNDRR Global Status of MHEWS 2025 explicitly lists extreme heat as an 'emerging hazard' that many existing early warning systems 'are not equipped to handle'. Caveat: direct fetches of the primary PDFs were blocked by this session's network policy; figures are taken from search-result excerpts that quote the named primary sources.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Heat warnings are one-way broadcasts (national/regional colour codes, TV, apps) that assume the recipient will self-protect; the people who die are precisely those who cannot or will not act: cognitively impaired, socially isolated, without cooling or transport. Multnomah County documented 90-minute waits for rides to cooling centres and bodies undiscovered for days. (2) Where heat action plans exist and include outreach, they work: Ahmedabad's HAP was associated with ~1,190 avoided deaths per year (95% CI 162-2,218) in 2014-15 vs 2007-10 (PMC6236972), but outreach there depends on manual phone lists and health-worker rounds, which do not scale and are absent in most cities. (3) No closed loop: no city knows, on a 40C day, which of its high-risk residents has actually been reached. (4) Mortality surveillance lags by months and in countries like India the narrow case definition hides the toll, so there is no feedback loop and little political urgency. (5) Commercial mass-notification platforms (Everbridge, Genasys, Rave) are built for broadcast, priced for large agencies, and do not model the neighbour/family/community-health-worker network that is the only realistic way to physically check on someone. (6) Cooling centres are under-used because opening hours, location and transport are not matched to who needs them.

**What people do today.** Family members and neighbours phoning or visiting; city 'vulnerable persons' registries (e.g. Paris's CHALEX, Philadelphia's heat line and block captains) where municipal staff call names from a list by hand; opening cooling centres and libraries; door-knocking by volunteers and Red Cross; Ahmedabad-style WhatsApp/SMS alerts to health workers and informal employers shifting hours; in India, ASHA community health workers doing ad hoc checks; utilities suspending disconnections during heat; news media naming heatwaves to raise awareness.

**Proposed intervention.** A closed-loop 'heat check-in operations' platform for municipalities and health systems: (a) ingest open forecasts (NWS HeatRisk via api.weather.gov; Open-Meteo/ECMWF globally; IMD bulletins) and compute neighbourhood-level heat-health trigger thresholds from published city-specific temperature-mortality curves rather than a single national colour code; (b) an opt-in registry where each at-risk person is linked to 1-3 named check-in contacts drawn from their own network (relative, neighbour, building manager, community health worker) plus a municipal fallback; (c) when a threshold trips, the system automatically dispatches check-in tasks to those contacts by SMS, automated voice call and WhatsApp, collects a two-way outcome ('OK' / 'not reached' / 'needs help'), and escalates anyone unreached after a set interval to municipal welfare-check teams, with nearest open cooling site and ride-request routing; (d) a live 'reached / unreached' board for the health department and an end-of-season audit against EMS heat calls and excess mortality. What is novel: it converts a broadcast warning into an accountable, per-person reach confirmation using the social ties that already exist, at neighbourhood resolution, with escalation logic. It needs no AI to function (thresholds and cascades are rules); AI is optional for translation and contact-script drafting. It is not a wearable, not speech-based, and not a dashboard-only product.

**Works across countries?.** High. Heat-mortality exposure-response curves have been published for hundreds of cities worldwide (MCC network), open weather forecasts are global (Open-Meteo/ECMWF), and SMS/voice reach almost all households including those without smartphones. Adaptation needed: consent and data-protection regimes (GDPR in Europe, HIPAA-adjacent in the US), and in LMIC cities the 'contact' role shifts to community health workers (ASHA in India) and employers for outdoor workers. Fragile contexts without reliable mobile networks are harder.

**Sustainable business model.** B2G/B2B subscription priced per registered resident to municipal public-health and emergency departments, regional health authorities, and (in the US) Medicare Advantage plans, home-care agencies and electric utilities, all of which bear heat-related costs or regulatory obligations (utility shutoff moratoria, duty of care). A free tier for community organisations and NGOs, with EW4All/CREWS and climate-adaptation grants subsidising LMIC deployments. Registry data stays under the deploying authority's control; no advertising, no data resale, which keeps the social value intact.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, database and auth by a small team. Real data: NWS API (HeatRisk, forecasts, alerts with polygons), Open-Meteo (free global forecasts), municipal open-data cooling-centre lists; Twilio or equivalent for SMS/voice; PostGIS for neighbourhood thresholds. The MVP can run a real pilot with one city department and a few hundred opted-in residents. Hard parts: recruiting registry sign-ups and reliable contacts (adoption, not code); privacy/consent handling of health-adjacent data; liability if an escalation fails; proving a mortality effect requires multiple summers (use proxy metrics meanwhile); reaching people with no phone at all still needs human rounds. No custom hardware or regulatory approval is required before use.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of registered high-risk residents with a confirmed check-in within 4 hours of a trigger (target >90%); number and resolution time of 'unreached' escalations; EMS heat-illness calls and ED heat admissions in covered neighbourhoods versus matched uncovered ones; excess all-cause mortality on trigger days pre/post deployment (replicating the Ahmedabad evaluation design); cooling-centre utilisation and ride-request fulfilment time; for outdoor-work deployments, recorded heat-illness incidents per 1,000 worker-days.

- https://recercat.cat/handle/10230/72880?show=full

- https://www.pik-potsdam.de/@@multilingual-selector/3a36822d86b2459e888475c82b430a0c/en

## 17. Flood and flash-flood warnings are issued but do not reach, wake, or trigger action by the specific people in harm's way

*Domain:* climate-disaster (floods / last-mile early warning and evacuation) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** People in flash-flood-prone valleys and small jurisdictions that have no local warning system: Kerr County, Texas (July 2025) had no sirens or county warning system; 107 of the ~135 Texas deaths were in Kerr County, including 37 children, many at a summer camp where campers are not allowed phones, and the NWS flash-flood warning with the 'considerable' tag that triggers Wireless Emergency Alerts went out at 1:14 am while people slept. Peri-urban Mediterranean residents (Valencia, 29 Oct 2024: ~225-229 deaths; AEMET red warning at 8 am, regional ES-Alert phone message not sent until 20:11, roughly 90 minutes after officials had drafted it, a delay a judge called 'late and wrong'). People in fragile and conflict-affected states (Derna, Libya, Sept 2023: Libyan Red Crescent reported 11,300 deaths, UN-confirmed counts were lower at >5,000; the met service emailed warnings 72 hours ahead but the evacuation call came at 2:59 am after both dams had already failed at 2:40 and 2:50 am). Residents of small island developing states (only 43% have a multi-hazard early warning system) and least developed countries (lowest system comprehensiveness). Within all of these: camps, schools, care homes, informal settlements, night-time populations and people without smartphones.

**Scale and severity.** WMO/UNDRR Global Status of Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems 2025 (Nov 2025): only 119 countries (60%) report having an MHEWS; 43% of SIDS; 'risk knowledge' (knowing who and what is exposed where) is the weakest pillar, with fewer than one-third of countries reporting that capability; the report also flags a persistent 'digital divide' limiting reach of new alerting technology. WMO's Secretary-General said most Derna casualties could have been avoided had warnings been disseminated and acted on. Disasters caused a record 45.8 million internal displacements in 2024, nearly double the decade average (IDMC GRID 2025), and 29.9 million in 2025 (GRID 2026); Hurricanes Helene and Milton alone drove ~11 million movements. Google Flood Hub now provides riverine forecasts for ~240,000 locations in ~150 countries covering ~700 million people, but as of its 2023 expansion it covered riverine floods only, not flash floods, and alerts reach individuals through Android/Search rather than through local institutions' protocols. Caveat: direct fetches of the WMO/UNDRR, HRW and IDMC documents were blocked by session network policy; figures come from search excerpts quoting those sources, and Derna's death toll remains contested.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Forecasts are produced centrally but the decision to act is local, and the local decision-maker usually has no pre-agreed trigger: Valencia's officials debated for two hours after deciding to alert; Derna's authorities received an email. (2) Dissemination assumes a phone that is on, in signal, and not muted: WEA at 1:14 am in a river canyon with no phones at camp; Kerr County had opted not to fund sirens. (3) Warnings are hazard- and county-shaped ('flash flood warning for Kerr County'), not place-shaped ('cabins on the river bend must move to the ridge road now'), so recipients must translate them under stress. (4) There is no acknowledgement loop, so emergency managers never learn who was not reached until the body count. (5) Commercial mass-notification platforms (Everbridge, Genasys, Rave) are priced and designed for large agencies, require the agency to decide and compose each message, and are out of reach for camps, schools, care facilities, HOAs, small towns and NGO-run settlements. (6) Google Flood Hub and GloFAS solve the forecast, not the last mile, and do not know which specific sites a given user is responsible for. (7) In fragile states the institutional channel between the met service and civil protection has broken down (WMO on Libya).

**What people do today.** WhatsApp and Facebook groups; church and school phone trees; camp staff physically watching river levels; volunteers with megaphones or driving door to door; residents keeping NOAA weather radios; ad hoc sirens; NGO anticipatory-action protocols (Red Cross Early Action Protocols) that operate at national or district scale but not at the level of a specific camp or street; after disasters, lawsuits and inquiries rather than system change.

**Proposed intervention.** A place-based 'trigger-to-protocol' service that sits between official warnings and the people responsible for a specific site. Each protected site (camp, school, care home, informal-settlement block, neighbourhood, small town) registers its polygon, its pre-agreed triggers (e.g. NWS flash-flood warning with 'considerable/catastrophic' tag intersecting the polygon; Flood Hub or GloFAS gauge forecast exceeding a stage; upstream USGS/NOAA gauge threshold; rainfall accumulation), a pre-written action protocol (who moves whom where, by when), and a roster of wardens with multiple contact channels. The backend continuously polls open feeds (api.weather.gov alerts with polygons, MeteoAlarm and WMO Alert Hub CAP feeds, Google Flood Hub API, USGS/NOAA gauges, GloFAS), performs polygon matching, fires the protocol via SMS, automated wake-up voice calls that ring through silenced phones, WhatsApp and satellite messengers, requires acknowledgement, auto-escalates to backup wardens and the jurisdiction's EOC when unacknowledged, and logs an immutable timeline for after-action review. What is novel: it pre-commits the local decision before the night of the flood (the micro-scale analogue of anticipatory-action protocols), makes 'reached/not reached' visible in real time, and runs on open data at a price small institutions can pay. Fully useful without AI (rules and cascades); AI is optional for drafting protocols and translating messages.

**Works across countries?.** High at the data layer: CAP is the global alerting standard and WMO's Alert Hub aggregates national CAP feeds; Flood Hub covers ~150 countries; GloFAS is global; USGS/NOAA and MeteoAlarm cover the US and Europe. Lower at the institutional layer: it needs a responsible local 'warden' per site, so in fragile states it is deployed via NGOs, UN camp managers or community committees rather than government. Flash floods in ungauged basins remain partly unforecastable anywhere, which limits lead time but not the dissemination/acknowledgement value.

**Sustainable business model.** Per-site subscription for duty-of-care institutions (summer camps, schools, care facilities, campgrounds, HOAs, small municipalities) where liability and new post-Texas camp-safety rules create demand; enterprise/NGO licences for camp-management agencies and humanitarian organisations; insurer partnerships (premium credits for sites with verified protocols); EW4All, CREWS and SOFF financing for LMIC deployments. Revenue comes from institutions, not from residents, and the core alerting path never depends on ads or data sales.

**MVP feasibility.** High for a small team: web app with auth, PostGIS database of site polygons, protocol and roster management, a poller for api.weather.gov (alerts carry GeoJSON polygons and impact tags), USGS water-services gauges, MeteoAlarm/Alert Hub CAP XML, and Google Flood Hub API (requires requesting access; terms may constrain redistribution), plus Twilio for SMS/voice with acknowledgement capture and escalation timers. A working pilot with a handful of real sites is achievable in weeks. Hard: Flood Hub API access and redistribution terms; flash-flood lead time in ungauged basins is inherently short; alert fatigue and false-alarm costs if thresholds are poorly set; liability framing; convincing institutions to write protocols in advance; reaching people without any phone still requires human cascades. No custom hardware or pre-use regulatory approval is required, though sirens or satellite messengers can be added as optional endpoints.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median minutes from official warning issuance to site acknowledgement, and share of sites acknowledging within 10 minutes; share of triggered protocols reported executed (people moved to safe locations) and time to completion; number of unreached escalations and their resolution time; false-alarm and missed-event rates per trigger type; in post-event audits, deaths and injuries at covered sites versus comparable uncovered sites in the same event; for emergency managers, the fraction of their population covered by a pre-agreed protocol.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://wmo.int/publication-series/global-status-of-multi-hazard-early-warning-systems-2025
