## 18. Disaster survivors, especially low-income households, fail to obtain the recovery assistance they are eligible for because the aid process is fragmented, document-heavy and opaque

*Domain:* climate-disaster (recovery and aid distribution) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Households displaced or damaged by floods, hurricanes and wildfires who must navigate multiple assistance programs with short deadlines while displaced. In the US: renters, low-income and elderly homeowners, limited-English and rural households. After the July 2025 Texas floods, 3,228 Kerr County households applied to FEMA's Individuals and Households Program; as of 11 Oct 2025 only 46% had even been referred to the review stage, 704 (~22%) were found eligible, and 775 were denied 'largely because people weren't responding or were withdrawing their applications' (Texas Tribune analysis of OpenFEMA data; Texas Appleseed). After Hurricane Ian only 41% of IHP applications had been approved as of Feb 2023, with home inspections lagging far behind applications (Texas Appleseed/NLIHC). GAO (2020, GAO-20-503) found homeowners in the most socioeconomically vulnerable communities received significantly less assistance, and GAO-25-106768 (May 2025) found FEMA still conveys approval likelihood using 2008-2016 data. Outside the US: the 45.8 million people displaced by disasters in 2024 (IDMC) and the ~11 million displaced by Helene and Milton alone; Valencia residents marking the flood's first anniversary with 'anger and grief' over recovery; Derna survivors in a state with no functioning compensation system.

**Scale and severity.** US scale: 27 separate billion-dollar disasters in 2024 and more than US$4 billion paid through IHP that year (CRS/FEMA); referral and approval rates swing wildly by disaster for reasons unrelated to need (Kerr County 46% referred vs. more than 80% after Hurricane Beryl in the same state), which indicates process failure rather than ineligibility. GAO has repeatedly documented that IHP processes are hard to understand and 'likely' depress approval rates, that field staff did not understand application requirements (GAO-20-360), and that outcomes are regressive by socioeconomic status. Global scale: disasters displaced 45.8 million people in 2024 (record) and 29.9 million in 2025 (IDMC GRID 2025/2026); for most of them recovery depends on reaching government ex-gratia, insurance or NGO cash programs with documentation they often lost in the disaster. I could not find a published national multi-year IHP approval rate for 2023-2025, so the US figures are disaster-specific and should not be read as a national average; direct fetches of GAO, CRS and Texas Tribune documents were blocked by session network policy and figures come from search excerpts quoting them.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) The aid landscape is a maze with sequencing rules: FEMA IHP, an SBA loan application that is often a prerequisite for further FEMA grants, NFIP/private insurance determinations, state programs, HUD CDBG-DR money that arrives years later, and dozens of charities, each with its own forms, deadlines (60-day FEMA appeal window) and documentation (proof of occupancy/ownership, ID, insurance letters) that survivors lost or cannot produce while displaced without mail or internet. (2) FEMA's 'not approved' letters are often for fixable paperwork reasons, but the burden to decode the reason and assemble an appeal falls on the survivor; many simply stop responding, which is exactly what Kerr County's denial reasons show. (3) Disaster Case Management is understaffed, slow to stand up and reliant on spreadsheets; legal aid organisations (e.g. Texas RioGrande Legal Aid) are overwhelmed with denials. (4) Existing software solves adjacent problems: DisasterAssistance.gov and the FEMA app only let you apply to FEMA; Crisis Cleanup coordinates debris work orders; Recovers.org matches volunteers; none track a household's status across all programs, surface denial-reason-specific next steps, manage deadlines, or let case managers triage a caseload. (5) FEMA's status data is not exposed to third parties via API, and OpenFEMA aggregates arrive with lag, so no one sees in near-real time which neighbourhoods are falling through (Kerr's low referral rate surfaced only through journalism three months later).

**What people do today.** Nonprofits, churches and long-term recovery groups filling gaps with their own cash; legal aid hotlines drafting appeals one by one; mutual-aid spreadsheets and Facebook groups sharing 'what worked'; GoFundMe and relatives; survivors re-applying from scratch, taking high-interest loans, or abandoning the claim; in Spain and elsewhere, protests and litigation over slow compensation; in LMICs, reliance on NGO cash transfers and community targeting lists.

**Proposed intervention.** A multi-program 'recovery case-navigation engine' built first for disaster case managers, legal aid and long-term recovery groups, with a survivor-facing portal: (a) a rules engine encoding eligibility and sequencing for each program (FEMA IHP categories, SBA, NFIP, state and major charity programs) that tells a household what they can claim and in what order; (b) a document vault with a per-program checklist so a proof-of-occupancy or ID uploaded once is reused across every application, plus guidance on acceptable substitutes when originals were lost; (c) a deadline and status tracker (appeal windows, inspection scheduling, SBA decision as FEMA prerequisite) with SMS/voice reminders, since displaced households change addresses; (d) a denial-reason-driven appeal builder that maps FEMA ineligibility codes to the specific evidence and letter structure that overturns them; (e) caseload triage for case managers and an outreach map that joins OpenFEMA's zip-level IHP registration and award data with program status to show where referral or approval rates are anomalously low, so outreach is targeted to the Kerr Counties before the denials pile up. What is novel: it is cross-program and case-manager-first, driven by the actual denial codes and sequencing rules rather than general advice, and it produces an auditable record of who was helped and what they received. It works without AI; AI is optional for summarising denial letters.

**Works across countries?.** Moderate. The architecture (rules engine + document vault + deadlines + caseload triage + outreach analytics) ports to any country with formal post-disaster assistance programs: Spain's Consorcio de Compensación de Seguros claims and regional DANA aid, Australia's Disaster Recovery Payment and Allowance, India's SDRF/NDRF ex-gratia, the Philippines' DSWD programs, and NGO cash-transfer programs. Each requires encoding that country's rules, which is real work, and in contexts where aid is distributed in kind by NGOs without individual applications the tool adds less. The US has by far the richest open data (OpenFEMA), so it is the natural first market.

**Sustainable business model.** B2B/B2G subscription to disaster case management providers, state and county recovery offices administering CDBG-DR and state programs, legal aid organisations, community foundations and long-term recovery groups, priced per active case; philanthropic and federal recovery grants already fund case management and can fund the software line. Free for survivors, no percentage of aid taken, no sale of survivor data; this keeps incentives aligned with maximising aid actually received rather than volume of applications.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable by a small team as a web app with backend, Postgres, auth and secure file storage. Real data: OpenFEMA APIs (Disaster Declarations, IHP Registrations/Valid Registrations with zip-level counts and award amounts, Housing Assistance by owner/renter), FEMA's public Individual Assistance Program and Policy Guide for rule encoding, SBA disaster-loan program rules, Twilio for reminders. An MVP can launch rules for FEMA IHP plus one state program and run with one legal-aid or case-management partner on a current declared disaster. Hard: keeping rules current as FEMA policy changes; third parties cannot pull a household's FEMA status via API so status must be survivor-reported or screen-captured; security and consent for highly sensitive documents; staying on the right side of unauthorised-practice-of-law boundaries (template-and-checklist, not legal advice); and ultimate outcomes depend on FEMA's own throughput. No hardware or pre-use regulatory approval is needed.

**Measurable outcomes.** Approval rate and referral rate among assisted households versus the county baseline for the same disaster (OpenFEMA provides the baseline); share of denials appealed and appeal success rate; median days from application to first payment; dollars of assistance received per assisted household; reduction in denials coded as non-response/withdrawal; case-manager caseload throughput (cases per manager per week); for the outreach layer, time from a zip's anomalous referral rate appearing to outreach being launched.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://grist.org/extreme-weather/fema-has-denied-or-not-advanced-most-kerr-county-aid-applications-after-deadly-july-4-flood/
- https://texasappleseed.org/blog/fema-applications-stalled-kerr-county-families

## 19. Rural water points fail silently: nobody with a repair budget knows which pumps are broken or for how long

*Domain:* water-air-pollution / drinking water service reliability (rural handpumps and boreholes) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Rural households in sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia that depend on community-managed handpumps and boreholes (Afridev and India Mark II pumps dominate: 42% and 13% of pumps in the nine-country dataset). In 2024, 2,143 million people worldwide were not using safely managed drinking water and 409 million were not even using an improved source (302 million unimproved wells/springs, 106 million surface water, 95 million of them rural) per WHO/UNICEF JMP 2025 data. In 2020 only 30% of sub-Saharan Africans and 13.3% of rural sub-Saharan Africans used safely managed water (JMP via OWID). The people who must act are district water officers with one vehicle and hundreds of points (Zambia: median 0.64 km to the nearest alternative point, 4.17 million people served by 4,026 registered points), area mechanics, water point committees (85% of points are committee-managed), NGOs and professional maintenance providers (FundiFix, Whave, BASEflow, Uptime-funded providers).

**Scale and severity.** Provenance note: web search budget was exhausted and WHO/JMP/UNEP hosts are blocked in this sandbox, so figures were computed directly from primary datasets mirrored on GitHub (OWID ETL/grapher mirrors of JMP 2025 and IHME GBD 2023; openwashdata CSVs; WPDx-derived Zambia analysis). Health burden: IHME GBD 2023 attributes 755,000 deaths in 2023 to unsafe water sources (234,000 under age 5) and 534,000 to unsafe sanitation. Functionality, computed by me from the raw data: (1) Murray et al. 2024 nine-country survey (Ethiopia, Nepal, Malawi, Mali, Niger, Uganda, India, Rwanda, Mozambique; n=1,793 points, median 250 users): 74% functional, 9% partially functional, 17% abandoned/non-functional; 17% had no water on the day of the visit, 13% of all points were down because of a mechanical breakdown; among points with a recorded breakdown (n=633) the median outage was 10 days but the mean was 157 days and the 75th percentile 120 days; 20% of points had been down more than two weeks in the reference period; median pump age was only 3 years (mean 4.6), i.e. young infrastructure is already failing; Ethiopia 32%, Rwanda 26%, Nepal 21%, Niger 20% non-functional. (2) Malawi 2023 routine monitoring (Trocaire/mWater, n=1,365): 57% functional, 27% partially functional and needing repair, 10% not functional, 5% abandoned; of 203 points with a recorded outage length, 68% had been down more than one year (64 more than five years); the most-cited maintenance barrier was lack of technical expertise; only 32% had financial records and 19% a positive balance. (3) Malawi 2020 verification of reported breakdowns (BASEflow, n=57): 28% had no water; 93% had been repaired before and 69% had been repaired more than four times or countless times. (4) Zambia WPDx extract (6,643 rows, cleaned to 4,026): 11.8% dry on the visit day; 14 of 15 contributing programmes never record a status after handover (2,171 points uploaded at handover with zero failures), so the national dataset has no dated functionality history at all. The widely quoted sector figure that roughly a quarter to a third of rural handpumps are non-functional at any time is consistent with these samples but I could not load the RWSN/Foster meta-analyses to re-verify it.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Data platforms (WPDx, mWater, HDX exports) are snapshot inventories, not service records: as the Zambia analysis shows, points are uploaded at handover and never revisited, so status fields encode which NGO uploaded the row rather than whether water flows. Predictive maintenance models do not rescue this: a tuned random forest on Zambian data scored ROC-AUC 0.61 on held-out districts versus 0.64 for simply sorting pumps by age, because there is no feedback loop to learn from. The community-management model (water point committees, 85% of points) lacks technical expertise, spare parts and money (Malawi: 21% keep no financial records; most have no positive balance), so repairs wait for an NGO or district visit. Professionalized, results-based maintenance (FundiFix, Whave, Uptime Catalyst Facility paying per verified functional water point) works but covers a small fraction of points because providers lack cheap, auditable evidence of uptime that funders and districts will pay against. Fault-reporting pilots die of silence: reporters hear nothing back and stop reporting. Smart-handpump sensors (Oxford/Kwale) prove the value of dated records but require hardware on every pump and suffer attrition from damage and signal loss.

**What people do today.** Users walk to the next working point (Zambia median 0.64 km, often far more), queue longer (JMP: 287 million people already spend over 30 minutes per round trip), revert to unimproved wells or surface water (106 million people drink surface water), or buy water: in Accra 2023 (n=116) 92% of households used packaged sachet water as their primary drinking water and 57% bought non-drinking water from commercial taps. Committees collect tariffs (Malawi median 300 MWK; 83% of responding points charge) and hire local mechanics for repeated patch repairs (69% of verified Malawi pumps repaired more than four times). NGOs and districts run periodic paper or mWater surveys; professional O&M companies (central Uganda: 171 repairs across 170 boreholes in 2021-2023) exist only where a donor funds them.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, provider-neutral water point service ledger that closes the feedback loop and turns repairs into auditable, payable outcomes. Components: (1) registry seeded from WPDx/HDX and mWater with short paintable point codes; (2) fault intake over SMS, USSD and WhatsApp on feature phones, with corroboration (many reporters raise one ticket's priority rather than duplicating it) and a transparent, district-tunable priority score (population served, days without water, distance to alternative, pump age, isolation override); (3) work-order dispatch to registered area mechanics with a parts checklist and parts price list per pump model; (4) automatic verification: a message to the original reporter seven days after a logged repair asking whether water is flowing, producing dated functionality records; (5) a verified-uptime statement per provider and district (functional days, days-to-repair, repeat failures) that results-based funders, district councils and donors can pay against, and that is pushed back to WPDx so the public dataset finally has dated status. What is novel: nobody runs the neutral verification-and-payment evidence layer across providers; existing tools stop at data collection or are proprietary to one maintenance company. It is fully useful without any AI; optional LLM parsing of free-text SMS in local languages is a convenience, not the product.

**Works across countries?.** High. WPDx holds records from 60+ countries, pump technologies are standardized across Africa and South Asia, SMS/USSD gateways (Africa's Talking, Twilio) cover the target countries, and results-based maintenance funders operate across Kenya, Uganda, Ethiopia, Malawi, Zambia, Tanzania and elsewhere. Governance differs (district vs national asset registers), so the registry and scoring must be configurable per jurisdiction; language of SMS intake must be localized.

**Sustainable business model.** Per-water-point annual subscription (on the order of a few dollars per point) paid by professional maintenance providers and district councils out of the results-based payments and tariffs the verified-uptime evidence unlocks, plus a verification/reporting fee charged to results-based funders and donors who currently pay for expensive third-party audits. Incentives align with social value because revenue only grows when more points are verifiably working; the functionality data itself stays open (published to WPDx), which prevents lock-in and keeps the public good intact. Risks: SMS cost per ticket at pilot scale and the need for anchor contracts with two or three providers or districts before unit economics work.

**MVP feasibility.** Honest assessment: buildable by a small team as a web application (backend, Postgres/PostGIS, auth with roles for reporter, mechanic, district officer, funder) deployable to the cloud. Real public data: WPDx open data portal and HDX country extracts (CC BY-SA), mWater public API, openwashdata CSV packages (CC BY), WorldPop population rasters for population-served estimates, GADM boundaries. SMS/USSD via Africa's Talking or Twilio webhooks. No custom hardware and no regulatory approval are required. Hard parts: field adoption (someone must paint codes on pumps and tell communities the number), mechanic enrollment, airtime costs, and the risk that verification replies are sparse; also WPDx records are often stale, so the first months of data are the seeding phase. The product has value from day one for a single district or provider even before any funder pays against it.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median and 90th-percentile days from fault report to verified repair (baseline: mean 157 days, p75 120 days in the nine-country data); share of reported faults verified flowing within 14 days; share of registered points with a dated functionality record less than 90 days old (baseline in Zambia WPDx: effectively zero); person-days without water averted (population served times outage days avoided); repeat-failure rate per point per year; volume of results-based payments disbursed against ledger evidence; reduction in reliance on surface or unimproved sources in served communities (household survey).

**Sources consulted:**

## 20. Air-quality data deserts: about a billion people live in countries that do not measure the air, and the worst-exposed places have the thinnest monitoring

*Domain:* water-air-pollution / ambient and household air pollution exposure · *Researcher confidence:* 0.85

**Who experiences it.** Residents, schools, clinics and outdoor workers in the 36% of countries that had no government air-quality monitoring in OpenAQ's 2024 global assessment (a cumulative population of nearly one billion, mostly low- and lower-middle-income countries in sub-Saharan Africa, Central Asia, parts of MENA and Latin America), plus people in countries that monitor only a few cities. Exposure is highest where data is scarcest: from the AQLI 2025 release (2023 data) Asia's population-weighted PM2.5 is 31.3 ug/m3 (97% of 4.56 billion people above 15 ug/m3, 40% above 35) and Africa's is 18.0 ug/m3 (60% of 1.45 billion above 15), versus 10.2 in Europe. Country examples: Bangladesh 60.8 ug/m3 (5.5 years of life expectancy lost), India 41.0 (3.5 years), Pakistan 39.0, DR Congo 32.1, Nigeria 22.9. Household exposure overlaps: 2.06 billion people lacked clean cooking fuels in 2023 (972 million in sub-Saharan Africa, 335 million in India, 168 million in Nigeria).

**Scale and severity.** Provenance note: figures computed from the AQLI 2025 country file on GitHub, from IHME GBD 2023 and WHO/World Bank series mirrored in Our World in Data's grapher repository, and from OpenAQ's published site content; WHO and State of Global Air sites were blocked in this sandbox. Deaths: IHME GBD 2023 attributes 7.90 million deaths in 2023 to air pollution (13.2% of all deaths), 4.92 million to ambient particulate matter and 2.76 million to household air pollution from solid fuels (categories overlap), including 676,000 children under five (about 1,850 per day; UNICEF's figure cited by OpenAQ is around 2,000 per day). India alone: 2.0 million air-pollution deaths. State of Global Air 2024 reported more than 8 million deaths for 2021 (cited via OpenAQ). Exposure: 99.1% of the world's population lives above the WHO annual guideline of 5 ug/m3, 71.4% above 15 ug/m3, and 23.2% (about 1.85 billion people) above 35 ug/m3; population-weighted global PM2.5 is 24.0 ug/m3 and the average life expectancy loss relative to the WHO guideline is 1.86 years (14.8 billion person-years in total), computed from AQLI 2025 data (AQLI's own 2024 headline was 1.9 years). Data gap: OpenAQ's biennial assessment (2024) found 36% of countries, nearly one billion people, are not monitoring air quality, that in the most populous unmonitored countries air pollution is a top-seven risk factor, and that barely over one quarter of countries provide full, easy public access to useful data; the 2022 assessment found only 61% of governments produce any data, leaving over one billion people without it.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Reference-grade monitors cost tens of thousands of dollars each and need skilled calibration, so under-resourced governments do not deploy them (OpenAQ explicitly calls on funders to close this gap). Low-cost sensor networks (PurpleAir, AirGradient, Clarity, AirQo, sensors.AFRICA) depend on local hosts, procurement, power and connectivity and are placed opportunistically (where a partner happens to be), not where they would most reduce uncertainty about population exposure. Satellite and model products exist but are not actionable for a school or clinic: AQLI and the van Donkelaar surface PM2.5 product are annual averages at roughly 1 km, Copernicus CAMS forecasts are daily but coarse (tens of kilometres) and distributed as NetCDF for specialists, and AQLI itself warns its satellite estimates can diverge sharply from ground truth in cities like Ulaanbaatar (19 vs 92 ug/m3). Commercial apps (IQAir, Google/BreezoMeter) are proprietary, interpolate silently and show nothing or a single embassy sensor in data deserts. OpenAQ aggregates what exists but, by design, cannot estimate what is not measured. No open tool answers the two questions decision-makers in these countries actually ask: what is the air like here today, with honest uncertainty, and where should the next ten sensors go.

**What people do today.** People judge the air by visibility, dust and smoke; schools and clinics have no rule for outdoor activity or for advising asthma and COPD patients; journalists and advocates quote single IQAir or US-embassy sensors (whose public feeds have been cut back); researchers run short campaigns whose data vanish after the project; city governments use neighbouring countries' indices or copy annual satellite maps into plans; households burning solid fuels have no information linking their cooking to the 2.76 million annual household air pollution deaths. OpenAQ's data-to-action directory exists precisely because organizations lack guidance on turning sparse data into decisions.

**Proposed intervention.** An open exposure-estimation and monitoring-gap planning service for places with few or no monitors. For any administrative unit (GADM 1/2) it fuses daily CAMS forecasts, annual satellite-derived PM2.5 (van Donkelaar/AQLI) as a bias-correcting prior, NASA FIRMS fire detections (open burning and wildfire smoke), and every OpenAQ or user-registered sensor nearby, producing a daily population-weighted PM2.5 estimate with an explicit uncertainty band rather than a false-precision AQI. On top of that it runs a sensor-placement optimizer: a greedy, submodular selection of candidate sites that maximizes population-weighted uncertainty reduction, so a city, NGO or vendor gets a ranked list of where the next five or fifty sensors should go and what coverage they would buy. Institutional alert rules (schools, clinics, outdoor employers) go out by SMS, WhatsApp and email when the estimate plus uncertainty crosses WHO interim targets, with plain-language actions. A public scorecard ranks data deserts by people-at-risk per monitor to drive funding. The differentiation is uncertainty-first estimation that works with zero local sensors and improves as sensors are added, plus the placement optimizer that nobody offers openly. It needs no AI: geostatistics (kriging or Gaussian-process regression with satellite covariates) and standard optimization are sufficient.

**Works across countries?.** Global by construction: every input (CAMS, van Donkelaar, FIRMS, WorldPop, GADM, OpenAQ) is worldwide. Most valuable in sub-Saharan Africa, Central and South Asia and MENA where monitoring is thinnest and exposure highest; still useful in middle-income countries for siting expansions and in high-income countries for wildfire smoke outside monitored metros. Localization needs: language, national AQI breakpoints (OpenAQ's AQI Hub documents them) and local burning seasons.

**Sustainable business model.** Free public layer (estimates, scorecard, basic alerts) funded by clean-air philanthropy and development programs that already pay for monitoring (Clean Air Fund, World Bank and regional bank clean-air lending, UNEP/WHO country programs). Paid tiers that do not compromise the public good: network-planning reports and APIs for city governments and ministries writing monitoring plans; siting-as-a-service bundled by sensor vendors and integrators; enterprise API for insurers, health systems and large employers needing exposure estimates for unmonitored sites; sponsored sensor placements where a funder pays for sites the optimizer ranks highest. The rule that protects social value: exposure estimates and alerts for individuals and public institutions stay free and open-licensed; what is sold is planning, integration and SLA.

**MVP feasibility.** Honest assessment: buildable by a small team as a web app with a nightly ETL backend, PostGIS database and auth (public viewer, institution admin, planner). Real open data and APIs: OpenAQ API v3 (free key), Copernicus Atmosphere Data Store API for CAMS global forecasts (free registration), NASA FIRMS API (free key), WorldPop rasters, GADM boundaries, AQLI country and district CSVs on GitHub (CC BY), the van Donkelaar surface PM2.5 product (open). No hardware and no regulatory approval are required to launch; value compounds when partners actually install sensors where the optimizer suggests. Hard parts: bias correction between model, satellite and ground data (requires held-out validation against existing monitors), communicating uncertainty without undermining trust, CAMS resolution in mountainous or coastal cities, sensor quality control for user-registered devices, and the compute and storage cost of daily global rasters (mitigated by processing only requested admin units). This is a legitimate software MVP but its headline impact depends on partners deploying sensors and institutions changing behaviour.

**Measurable outcomes.** Mean absolute error and calibration of daily PM2.5 estimates against held-out ground monitors, tracked per region; number of people living in administrative units that moved from no dated exposure estimate to a validated one; sensors deployed through optimizer-generated plans and the measured reduction in estimation uncertainty they produced; number of schools, clinics and employers with active alert rules and documented actions taken (outdoor activity rescheduled, masks distributed, clinic advisories issued) on high-pollution days; countries or cities moving from no monitoring to monitoring with open data, as recorded in OpenAQ's next landscape assessment; downstream, exposure reduction on alert days measured with personal or classroom monitors in evaluation sites.

## 21. Uncollected waste is burned or dumped, and the informal workers who recover it cannot prove what they collect to the systems that pay for recycling

*Domain:* water-air-pollution / waste management, plastic pollution, informal recycling workers · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** People living without municipal waste collection, concentrated in sub-Saharan Africa, South and Southeast Asia and parts of Latin America (UN-Habitat's estimate, cited in UNEP's Global Waste Management Outlook 2024, is 2.7 billion people; Cottom et al. 2024 find uncollected waste is the dominant source of plastic emissions, so the two framings agree on direction even if definitions differ). The informal waste pickers, itinerant buyers and small scrap dealers who recover materials from that waste, commonly estimated at around 20 million people worldwide (an older UN-Habitat 2010 estimate repeated in ILO 2019 material; I could not load a newer primary count, so treat the number as an order of magnitude), disproportionately women, migrants and members of marginalized castes and ethnic groups, working without contracts, health protection or price information. Neighbourhoods next to open burning and open dumps, which emit PM2.5, black carbon and dioxins (WHO published a 2025 technical note on open waste burning). The largest national contributors to plastic pollution: India (9.3 million tonnes in 2020, 5.8 of them burned), Nigeria (3.5 Mt), Indonesia (3.4 Mt), China (2.8 Mt).

**Scale and severity.** Provenance note: plastic figures are computed from the Cottom, Cook and Velis 2024 Nature dataset (SPOT model, CC BY) as mirrored with full citation in Our World in Data's grapher repository; UNEP GWMO 2024 and UN-Habitat figures were only reachable through a secondary page that cites them, so they carry lower confidence. In 2020 the world generated 259.5 million tonnes of municipal plastic waste, of which 52.1 million tonnes (20%) escaped into the environment as pollution: 29.9 Mt (57%) through open, uncontrolled burning and 22.2 Mt (43%) as unburned debris. By pathway, 35.6 Mt (68%) came from waste that was never collected, 12.8 Mt (25%) from uncontrolled disposal sites, 1.8 Mt from littering, 1.0 Mt from recycling rejects and 0.9 Mt lost during collection and transport. UNEP GWMO 2024 (via secondary citation): municipal solid waste of 2.1 billion tonnes in 2023 rising to 3.8 billion tonnes by 2050; direct management costs USD 252 billion (2020), USD 361 billion including health, pollution and climate externalities, and USD 640 billion per year by 2050 without action. Health: open burning feeds into the 7.9 million annual air-pollution deaths (IHME GBD 2023) but its separable share is not quantified in the data I could verify. Policy pressure is real and recent: extended producer responsibility (EPR) regimes for packaging now operate or are being rolled out in India, Kenya, South Africa, Nigeria, Vietnam, Chile, Colombia and the EU, and the UN plastics treaty negotiations centre on measurement, reporting and verification of plastic flows.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Expanding formal collection is capital- and logistics-intensive and, at current trajectories, cannot reach the 2.7 billion unserved before waste volumes nearly double. EPR money, the one growing funding stream, flows through registered producers to registered recyclers and credit platforms; informal collectors and most cooperatives lack the identity, weigh records and chain-of-custody documents required to claim it, so the people doing the recovery are paid local scrap-dealer prices with no transparency. Existing digital players (Recykal, Kabadiwalla Connect, Plastics for Change, Plastic Bank, Mr. Green Africa, TakaTaka) are vertically integrated buyers or aggregators: they own the data, set the price and operate where they have plants, which is good for them but leaves cooperatives dependent and price-blind. City diagnostics (UN-Habitat's Waste Wise Cities Tool) are periodic surveys, so municipalities do not know, even in retrospect, how much waste escapes or where burning happens. Satellite dump detection (Global Plastic Watch, NASA FIRMS) exists but is not tied to any operational collection or payment workflow.

**What people do today.** Households without service burn waste in yards and streets (the single largest plastic pollution pathway), dump into drains, rivers and vacant land, or pay informal cart collectors. Waste pickers sort at kerbside and dumpsites, sell daily to the nearest dealer at whatever price is offered, and keep no records; cooperatives and NGOs run paper ledgers or spreadsheets; brands buy credits from a few large aggregators to satisfy EPR targets; municipalities commission occasional surveys. Recycling rates are reported from facility weighbridges, which omit most informal recovery entirely.

**Proposed intervention.** A neutral materials-recovery ledger and price-transparency service for cooperatives, aggregators and municipalities, owned by neither buyers nor brands. Members (pickers, cart operators) get a QR or phone-number identity; every weigh-in is logged by a mobile-web or WhatsApp/USSD flow (material grade, kilograms, buyer, price, location, photo of the scale ticket), creating a tamper-evident chain of custody from street to bale. The service publishes anonymized local price indices by material grade so pickers and cooperatives know the going rate, and it generates the documentation that national EPR portals and credit buyers require, letting cooperatives sell recycling credits or receive producer payments directly rather than through intermediaries. A second layer uses open satellite fire detections (NASA FIRMS) and dump-site detections to map open burning hotspots by ward, so collection expansion and the ledger's recruitment target the wards that emit most. What is novel is the combination of neutral data ownership, open price transparency and EPR-grade traceability for the informal sector; vertically integrated competitors cannot credibly offer the first two. It is useful without AI; OCR of scale tickets is optional.

**Works across countries?.** High in principle: cooperatives and picker federations exist in India, Brazil, Colombia, Argentina, South Africa, Kenya, Ghana, Nigeria, Indonesia and the Philippines, EPR packaging rules are spreading, and the plastics treaty will push every signatory towards flow accounting. Specifics differ: which documents an EPR authority accepts, credit prices, material grades and languages; the product must be configured per regime and will land first where EPR rules already recognize or are moving to recognize informal-sector chain of custody (India, Kenya, South Africa, Colombia).

**Sustainable business model.** Transaction fee on EPR credit documentation and sales facilitated through the ledger (paid out of producer obligations, which exist by law), subscription for aggregators, municipalities and brands that need audited traceability and ward-level recovery data, and data licensing for corporate and treaty reporting. Pickers and cooperatives use it free, keep ownership of their records and can export them, with governance seats for picker federations so the platform cannot squeeze them. The model is aligned with social value because revenue rises only when more tonnes are documented and paid for at transparent prices. Risks: dependence on regulators accepting the documentation, and the need for anchor cooperatives and one producer-responsibility organization per country to reach volume.

**MVP feasibility.** Honest assessment: a working web MVP (backend, database, auth with roles for member, cooperative admin, buyer, auditor) with a mobile-web intake flow is feasible for a small team, and real open data exists for the targeting layer (Cottom et al. 2024 dataset via Dryad/OWID, NASA FIRMS, OpenStreetMap, WorldPop) and for EPR context (India's CPCB publishes registered producer and recycler lists). The hard parts are not software: prices must be crowdsourced because no public recyclables price API exists for these markets; weighing, cash and trust remain physical and local; regulatory acceptance of informal chain of custody varies by country; onboarding pickers requires field partners (cooperatives, WIEGO-type organizations). No custom hardware is required (phone cameras and existing scales suffice), and no regulatory approval is needed to operate the ledger itself, but realizing EPR revenue depends on each regime's rules. Lower feasibility than the two problems above because adoption depends on physical operations and partnerships, which should be stated plainly.

**Measurable outcomes.** Tonnes of material with verifiable chain of custody recorded per month and per ward; average price received by members versus the local benchmark index before and after joining (income uplift, not app downloads); share of cooperative revenue arriving directly from EPR credits or producer payments; number of members with a documented identity and sales history usable for credit or social protection; reduction in satellite-detected open burning and dump fires in served wards versus matched control wards; municipal recovery rates revised upward by documented informal recovery; audit pass rate of ledger documentation with EPR authorities.

## 22. Smallholder grain farmers in sub-Saharan Africa lose a fifth of their harvest after harvest and then 'sell low, buy high' because they cannot judge when storing pays

*Domain:* food-nutrition (post-harvest loss, storage, smallholder market timing) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Smallholder cereal (maize, sorghum, millet, rice) farming households across sub-Saharan Africa, concentrated in West Africa (Nigeria, Ghana, Senegal, Mali, Burkina Faso), East Africa (Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda, Ethiopia) and Southern Africa (Malawi, Zambia, Mozambique, Zimbabwe); secondarily smallholders in South Asia. The same households are net food buyers in the lean season, so the loss hits them twice: as lost product and as higher repurchase prices. Women, who do most on-farm storage and small-lot selling, and farmer cooperatives/aggregators that hold pooled grain are the operational decision-makers.

**Scale and severity.** Global: FAO SDG indicator 12.3.1a (Food Loss Index) puts food lost between harvest and retail at 13.3% in 2023 (13.0% in 2015), i.e. no progress toward SDG 12.3; fruit and vegetables 25.4%, cereals and pulses 8.4%. Regional: FAO's 2024 SDG 12.3.1 release gives sub-Saharan Africa about 23% and Western Africa about 27%, versus about 10% in North America and Europe (figures read from two GitHub-hosted fact-check ledgers that cite the FAO SDG 12.3.1 data; FAO itself warns the index is modelled and unreliable below aggregate level). APHLIS estimates 10-23% of African cereal production is lost post-harvest; the World Bank (2011) valued SSA grain losses at about US$4 billion/year, equal to the annual calories of 48 million people (both cited in World Bank/Brookings working paper 108109, 2016). Measured household-survey maize losses are lower (1.4-5.9%, Kaminski & Christiaensen 2014, LSMS-ISA), so the true physical-loss range is contested; I treat 5-20% as the honest band. The price-timing loss is better measured: Cardell & Michelson (American Journal of Agricultural Economics, 2023), using WFP retail prices from 1,038 markets in 30 African countries over 2000-2021, find the average harvest-to-lean-season maize price return is 41.7% in single-season countries (35-39% in double-season countries), yet prices fail to rise in 16.3% of market-years (up to 50% in Mauritania) and fall ~11.5% when they do fall, so farmers who cannot borrow rationally sell at harvest. Burke, Bergquist & Miguel (NBER w24476, 2018) show Kenyan farmers systematically 'sell low and buy high' and that harvest-time credit yielded a 28% return. Hermetic storage works (meta-analysis, Ngoma et al. 2023: conventional bags show 23x more weight loss and 42x more insects than hermetic bags) but adoption 'remains low' (Okori et al. 2022, Uganda survey of 306 farmers). Context: UNEP Food Waste Index 2024 separately counts 1,052 Mt of consumer-stage waste in 2022 (631 Mt household). NOTE: primary FAO/APHLIS/World Bank sites were unreachable in this session; figures were verified via GitHub-hosted copies and citations of those reports.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Hardware-first solutions (PICS hermetic bags, metal silos, ColdHubs solar cold rooms) need upfront cash and physical distribution and so stay at low adoption; they also do nothing about the sell-timing decision. (2) Market-price SMS/app services (Esoko, M-Farm and many national MIS) broadcast spot prices but not the decision that matters: the expected net return to storing this lot for N weeks in this market, net of expected storage loss, mold/aflatoxin risk, and the farmer's cost of cash. Cardell & Michelson show harvest prices only partly predict whether storage will pay, so raw price feeds are insufficient. (3) Warehouse-receipt systems and commodity exchanges reach only aggregated, graded volumes near cities. (4) APHLIS, the best loss dataset, is built for researchers and donors, not operations, and its API returned 401 to automated access in a project we reviewed. (5) Credit constraints (Burke et al.) mean information alone does not change behaviour unless it is wired into the lender's or cooperative's harvest-time financing decision.

**What people do today.** Farmers sell most grain at harvest to pay school fees and debts, store the rest in polypropylene sacks with pesticide dusts (losses to weevils, larger grain borer, mold), then buy back grain in the lean season at 30-40% higher prices. Cooperatives aggregate and hold grain on rules of thumb; informal traders arbitrage the seasonal gap instead of farmers; village savings groups provide small bridging loans. Some NGOs run bundled 'store-and-loan' programs by hand on spreadsheets.

**Proposed intervention.** A store-or-sell decision and storage-risk engine for farmer cooperatives, aggregators, agri-lenders and extension services (B2B, with SMS/USSD digests to members, not a consumer app). For each market x crop it builds the empirical distribution of harvest-to-lean-season returns from 20+ years of WFP market prices (per Cardell & Michelson's method), combines it with APHLIS-derived expected storage loss by crop, storage type and climate zone, a humidity/temperature mold-risk score from open weather data, and the cooperative's or lender's cost of money, and outputs per-lot recommendations: sell now, store N weeks, store only in hermetic bags, or take a harvest-time loan against stored grain. It records outcomes (actual sale price, measured weight loss) to recalibrate. What is novel is operationalising a well-established academic result (returns to storage are large on average but fail in a predictable minority of market-years) into a lot-level tool that prices loss risk, price risk and credit together; nothing in the market does this, and it needs no AI beyond standard seasonal statistics.

**Works across countries?.** High. WFP DataBridges prices cover 30+ African countries plus South Asia, Latin America and the Middle East; APHLIS covers most of SSA; the seasonal-return method handles single- and double-season countries. Parameters (crops, seasons, storage types) are configuration, not code. Weakest in countries with thin market-price coverage.

**Sustainable business model.** Pay-side is the institution that gains from better timing: (a) agri-lenders and MFIs offering harvest-time storage loans (the tool improves repayment and lets them price loans by market-year risk); (b) cooperatives and aggregators (seasonal subscription per tonne stored); (c) hermetic-bag and silo distributors who want demand triggered by risk alerts; (d) NGOs and government extension on donor contracts for a free farmer tier. Farmer-level data is never sold; recommendations are not tied to any buyer, which preserves the social value. Risk: cooperatives have low willingness to pay, so lenders and distributors must anchor revenue.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web app (backend + Postgres + auth) by a small team. Real data: WFP DataBridges API (market prices for ~1,000+ markets, OAuth client-credentials; public datasets free; verified via WFP-VAM's open client repo), Open-Meteo (free weather/humidity), APHLIS loss tables (may need registered access or a bulk download; one project reported 401 on automated calls), FAO SDG 12.3.1 and UNICEF/WB context data. Core engine is pandas-level statistics. Hard parts: SMS/USSD delivery cost and partner onboarding to reach farmers; validating storage-loss priors in the field; trust and liability if a 'store' recommendation precedes a price fall (must show probabilities, not certainties). No hardware or regulatory approval required. Honest caveat: impact depends on at least one cooperative or lender partner in the first season.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of tracked lots sold in the lean season rather than at harvest; realised sale price vs harvest-week price net of measured storage loss (USD/tonne gained); measured grain weight loss (%) in tracked stores vs cooperative baseline; lender repayment rate on storage loans; calibration error (how often 'store' was recommended in a market-year when prices fell vs the historical 16% base rate); household lean-season grain purchases (kg) among member farmers.

## 23. Eligible low-income Americans losing or never receiving SNAP and WIC food benefits because of paperwork, recertification and the 2025 work-requirement expansion

*Domain:* food-nutrition (food insecurity, food-assistance access and retention) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** Low-income US households eligible for SNAP or WIC but not enrolled or churned off: about 4 million eligible SNAP non-participants (38 million eligible vs ~34 million participating, FY2022), with the lowest take-up among the elderly (55%), eligible non-citizens (50%) and households with earnings (76%); about 5.2 million WIC-eligible people not served (11.83 million eligible, 6.63 million served in 2023), especially children aged 1-4 (47.9% coverage), pregnant women (49.3%) and rural/nonmetro families (24.0% coverage). Since July 2025 the newly exposed group is adults aged 55-64 and parents whose youngest child is 14-17, who now face the 3-months-in-36 ABAWD time limit, plus gig and variable-hour workers who must document 80 hours/month. Food banks, clinics, legal-aid offices and school/college navigators absorb the fallout.

**Scale and severity.** USDA ERS reported 47.4 million people, including 13.8 million children, in food-insecure households in 2024 (about 1 in 7 households); USDA then terminated the annual food-security survey on 20 Sept 2025, removing the national measure going forward. Take-up (USDA FNS, 'Trends in SNAP Participation Rates FY2020-FY2022', Oct 2024): 88% of eligible individuals participated in FY2022, but only 55% of eligible elderly, 50% of eligible non-citizens, 76% of earned-income households; WIC reached 56.1% of eligible people in 2023 (USDA FNS WIC Eligibility and Program Reach 2023), the best since 2016 but still leaving ~5 million unserved. Public Law 119-21 (H.R.1, section 10102, effective 4 July 2025) raised the ABAWD exemption age from 55 to 65 and lowered the dependent-child age from 18 to 14 (values and statute references confirmed in PolicyEngine's open parameter files). Observed effect: national SNAP enrollment fell from 42.2 million (May 2025) to 36.6 million (May 2026), a drop of more than 13% (AP via PBS NewsHour, 21 Aug 2026); CBPP's tracker recorded every state falling and 36 states down 5%+ (Jan 2025-Jan 2026, ~4.3 million fewer people); Arizona fell 43-51%; Pennsylvania fell 220,000 (11%), of which the state attributes 'just under half' (98,000) to the law, leaving ~122,000 unexplained departures consistent with administrative loss. Causal evidence: work requirements cut participation among subject adults by 53% with no employment effect (Gray et al., NBER w28877); roughly half of cases fail recertification and about half of those re-enter soon after, i.e. churn (NBER Reporter 2024); applicant-initiated flexible interviews raised approvals ~6 points in a 65,000-applicant LA experiment (NBER w31239); closing an enrollment office cut participation 7-9% (NBER w34529). The largest benefits-outreach nonprofit, Benefits Data Trust, laid off 273 staff effective 24 Aug 2024 and closed (Pennsylvania WARN notice). State administrative cost share rises from 50% to 75% in Oct 2026, further shrinking caseworker capacity. NOTE: USDA/CBPP/CBO sites were unreachable in this session; figures were verified via GitHub-hosted documents that quote and link those reports.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) State eligibility portals are determination systems, not retention systems: they do not warn a household before its ABAWD clock runs out, track whether an exemption (age 65, child under 14, veteran, homeless, tribal, medically unfit) applies, or help assemble monthly proof of 80 work hours. (2) Consumer screeners (mRelief, Propel/Providers, Benefit Kitchen, PolicyEngine's household calculator) answer 'am I eligible?' but stop at application; churn happens months later at recertification and periodic reports. (3) Human navigation capacity collapsed just as burden rose: Benefits Data Trust closed in 2024, states face a 50%-to-75% admin cost shift, call-centre delays and 9%+ payment-error penalties push agencies toward procedural denials rather than outreach. (4) No tool generates the verification artefacts (hours logs, employer letters, exemption evidence, good-cause statements) in the state's accepted format, and notices are inconsistent across 50+ agencies. (5) WIC's low coverage of 1-4-year-olds and rural families is a retention problem too (families drop off after infancy), which outreach campaigns alone have not fixed.

**What people do today.** Households reapply after being cut off (losing 1-3 months of benefits each churn cycle), rely more on food pantries (Pennsylvania food banks report rising demand), call legal-aid hotlines and 211, borrow from family, or stop trying. Navigators at food banks and clinics keep spreadsheets of client recert dates; some community colleges run manual SNAP clinics. Agencies themselves triage by closing cases that miss any deadline.

**Proposed intervention.** A benefit-continuity engine for the navigators who already serve these households (food banks, FQHC clinics, legal aid, WIC clinics, college basic-needs offices): a case register that, for each consenting client, encodes state-specific SNAP/WIC rules (using the open-source PolicyEngine-US rules engine, which already models H.R.1's ABAWD changes) to compute their ABAWD clock, exemption eligibility, recertification and periodic-report deadlines and WIC category transitions; sends reminders by SMS/email in the client's language; produces ready-to-submit verification packets (work-hours log, exemption affidavits, employer letter templates, good-cause and fair-hearing request letters) in the format each state accepts; and surfaces procedural-denial patterns to the navigator organisation. What is novel is the focus on retention and procedural loss (where most eligible people are now lost) rather than initial screening, delivered as infrastructure for navigators rather than another consumer app, and the fusion of an executable statute model with document generation. It is useful without any AI (rules + templates); optional OCR of agency notices is the only AI touchpoint.

**Works across countries?.** The problem is universal: administrative non-take-up of means-tested food and cash benefits is documented in the UK (Pension Credit, Healthy Start vouchers), France (RSA non-take-up ~1/3, DREES) and elsewhere (these non-US figures are from prior knowledge, not verified in this session). The architecture (statute-as-code rules engine + deadline tracking + document generation) ports, but every jurisdiction's rules and forms must be re-encoded, so the first product is US-only and policy-exposed.

**Sustainable business model.** Payers are institutions that bear the cost of food insecurity, not clients: Medicaid managed-care plans and health systems (food insecurity raises medical costs and they already fund SDOH navigation), Feeding America member food banks (whose SNAP-outreach contracts draw a 50% federal administrative match), legal-aid and hospital community-benefit budgets, and states that want to reduce churn-driven error rates. Per-navigator-seat SaaS with a free tier for small pantries; client data never sold or shared with agencies without consent. Risk: dependence on US policy cycles and on navigator organisations whose budgets are themselves squeezed.

**MVP feasibility.** Highly feasible as a web app with backend, Postgres and auth: PolicyEngine-US is open source with a public API for eligibility and benefit amounts; USDA FNS publishes the SNAP State Options and policy database, state participation data and WIC reach reports; state rules for 3-5 pilot states can be encoded first; Twilio/SendGrid for reminders; PDF generation for packets. Hard parts: no API into state eligibility systems (clients or navigators must enter determinations and notices), heterogeneous notice formats, HIPAA/PII-grade security and consent, and earning navigators' trust. No hardware or regulatory approval required; a working MVP with one food-bank partner is realistic for a small team.

**Measurable outcomes.** Recertification completion rate and procedural-denial rate for tracked clients vs the partner organisation's prior-year baseline; months of benefits preserved per client (converted to dollars); re-entry gap (days without benefits) after a closure; share of ABAWD-subject clients correctly moved to an exemption; WIC retention of children past age 1 and 2; navigator time per case; appeals won. Vanity metrics to avoid: sign-ups, screenings completed.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/Yazan-O/benefitline/blob/f0863b4cc6b731c4fe3011ece1e52ce1c3bbca44/data/SOURCES.md
- https://github.com/mehtama1234/cultural-geopolitical-theme-mining/blob/main/analysis/projects/us-safety-net-access/snap-administrative-burden-paper-scan-v1.md
- https://github.com/Harry-Hayman/harryhayman/blob/main/src/content/blog/pennsylvania-snap-enrollment-drop-not-all-eligibility.mdx
- https://github.com/tjmuehleman/cleandataforall/blob/main/blog/every-state-snap-rolls-fell.html
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/PolicyEngine/policyengine-us/main/policyengine_us/parameters/gov/usda/snap/work_requirements/abawd/in_effect.yaml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/PolicyEngine/policyengine-us/main/policyengine_us/parameters/gov/usda/snap/work_requirements/abawd/age_threshold/exempted.yaml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/PolicyEngine/policyengine-us/main/policyengine_us/parameters/gov/usda/snap/work_requirements/abawd/age_threshold/dependent.yaml

- https://github.com/opensandiego/mealscount-backend

## 24. Two in three children with severe wasting are never treated, and the therapeutic-food (RUTF) pipeline that treats the rest breaks without warning, worsened by 2025 aid cuts

*Domain:* food-nutrition (child acute malnutrition treatment, nutrition supply chain) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.65

**Who experiences it.** Children under five with wasting, 42.8 million (6.6%) in 2024, of whom 12.2 million are severely wasted (UNICEF/WHO/World Bank Joint Child Malnutrition Estimates, 2025 edition), concentrated in South Asia and the Sahel/Horn of Africa; national wasting prevalence is 11.6% in Nigeria and 10.9% in Niger (World Bank WDI), and roughly 1 in 10 Senegalese children are acutely malnourished. Their caregivers, mostly rural mothers walking to community clinics; community health workers who screen (and who in Senegal went unpaid and halved in number after 2025 US aid cuts); district nutrition officers and NGOs (Helen Keller Intl, ALIMA, Action Against Hunger, MSF) who manage caseloads and RUTF stock.

**Scale and severity.** Severe wasting carries about 11.6 times the mortality of a well-nourished child (Olofin et al. 2013, pooled 10 cohorts, 53,809 children). Only roughly 1 in 3 severely wasted children receives treatment (Global Action Plan on Child Wasting, 2020); a review of 44 coverage assessments in 21 countries found mean CMAM coverage of 38.3%, likely overestimated because assessments favour better programmes (Rogers et al. 2015); Isanaka et al. (2021) show annual caseload is several times point prevalence so true coverage is lower still; baseline coverage was ~1% in Gombe State, Nigeria before a recent programme (GiveWell 2024). Even treated children relapse at 22-63% within six months (King et al. 2025). Supply shock, 2025-26: after US foreign-aid cuts (USAID had funded Senegal's nutrition staff and the US was UNICEF's largest donor; UNICEF buys and distributes most of Senegal's RUTF), NPR reported on 13 June 2026 that screenings in one Senegalese district fell from over 180,000 children (Oct-Dec 2024) to under 87,000 (Jul-Sep 2025), about 30% of children against an 80% target; the Diourbel regional warehouse, which held ~4,000 boxes, now receives about half the RUTF it did; clinics are turning families away and making a less effective local millet-maize-groundnut substitute; community health workers are unpaid and in some areas halved. Simplified protocols can cut RUTF per recovered child by 32-50% (ComPAS, OptiMA; Bailey et al. 2020, Daures et al. 2025) but are not planned into supply. NOTE: UNICEF, WHO and WFP sites were unreachable in this session; figures were verified via an evidence-review repository and an IHME modelling repository on GitHub that cite the primary studies, plus a GitHub-archived copy of the NPR report.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Community-based management of acute malnutrition (CMAM) depends on active case-finding by CHWs and on an unbroken RUTF supply; both are financed by volatile donor money, and 2025 cuts removed the staff and the product at the same time. (2) Supply-chain systems (OpenLMIS, DHIS2 logistics modules) are facility-level, backward-looking monthly reports; they record a stockout after it happens and do not forecast admissions or flag a pipeline break weeks ahead. (3) RUTF forecasting is done annually from static prevalence, ignoring seasonal lean-period surges, screening-coverage changes and displacement, so districts over- or under-order. (4) Coverage assessments (SQUEAC) are expensive one-off surveys, so programmes fly blind on who is missed; relapse after discharge is unmonitored. (5) Simplified, lower-dose protocols that stretch scarce RUTF exist but district planners lack tools to model their stock implications and justify switching.

**What people do today.** Clinics ration RUTF, shorten treatment, or make local substitutes of millet, maize and groundnuts that are less effective; families are turned away and many stop coming ('Malnutrition is coming back', regional supervisor, Diourbel); NGOs bridge with philanthropic funds and emergency reallocations; mothers are trained to measure MUAC themselves (family MUAC, Niger: 12,893 mothers trained, lower cost than CHW screening); some programmes trial simplified protocols ad hoc.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, facility-level wasting caseload and RUTF pipeline early-warning system for district nutrition officers and implementing NGOs: it ingests each facility's historical admissions and stock (CSV upload or DHIS2 API), forecasts weekly admissions from seasonality, lean-season calendars, rainfall anomalies (Open-Meteo), IPC/CH phase (WFP DataBridges food-security endpoints) and screening coverage; converts forecasts into weeks-of-stock per facility under standard vs simplified protocols; raises pipeline-break alerts 4-8 weeks ahead with suggested inter-facility reallocations; and keeps a lightweight family-MUAC follow-up register to catch relapse. What is novel is being forward-looking and facility-level (existing LMIS tools report the past at district aggregates), being open-source and cheap enough for a district or a single NGO to adopt without a national procurement, and explicitly modelling simplified-protocol scenarios so planners can stretch supply during shortages. It needs no AI: seasonal time-series models and inventory arithmetic are sufficient.

**Works across countries?.** Applicable across the ~70 countries running CMAM, since DHIS2 is the health information system in most of them and RUTF logistics follow the same UNICEF-standard units; seasonal calendars and protocols are configuration. Strongest where admissions data exist monthly; weakest in conflict settings where facilities report sporadically.

**Sustainable business model.** The hardest of the three. Payers are governments, UNICEF and INGOs whose budgets are shrinking; realistic model is open-source core plus a hosted, supported tier sold to INGOs and country programmes (per-district annual fee), analytics subscriptions for RUTF manufacturers and procurement agencies that need demand forecasts, and philanthropic funding for the public-good layer. Keeping the core open and data owned by the ministry preserves the social value; the risk is revenue dependence on the same aid system that is contracting.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app (backend + Postgres + auth) by a small team: DHIS2 exposes an open REST API and a public demo instance for development; UNICEF's SDMX data warehouse is open (verified via UNICEF's unicefData package) for prevalence baselines; WFP DataBridges provides IPC-CH and Global Outlook food-insecurity data; Open-Meteo for rainfall. Forecasting is standard seasonal modelling. Hard parts: real facility admissions and stock data require a ministry or NGO partnership and are often incomplete; low connectivity at facilities (needs offline CSV paths); validating forecasts needs at least one season; ethical risk if reallocation advice is wrong. No custom hardware and no regulatory approval are needed because it is planning decision support, not clinical care. Honest assessment: feasible technically, but impact is gated on a field partner, so lower certainty of real-world use than problem 2.

**Measurable outcomes.** RUTF stockout-days per facility per quarter; share of admitted children whose treatment was interrupted by stockouts; forecast error (MAPE) of weekly admissions vs actual; lead time of pipeline-break warnings that proved correct; screening coverage (children screened / target population) and admissions-to-screening ratio; cure, default and death rates per Sphere standards; relapse at 6 months among followed-up discharges; RUTF cartons per recovered child.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/anshaji/nutri-evidence-review/blob/main/CARE_review/cmam-report/CMAM_REPORT.md
- https://github.com/anshaji/nutri-evidence-review/blob/main/CARE_review/brief/01_brief.md
- https://github.com/ihmeuw/vivarium_research/blob/main/docs/source/models/intervention_models/wasting_treatment/ethiopia_wasting_treatment/index.rst
- https://github.com/byte-pipe/tech-news/blob/1c0315e578e427f3762124f8c017571dea74fd82/data/2026-06-13/content/newsfeed-malnourished-kids-cant-get-the-therapeutic-food-th.md
- https://github.com/unicef-drp/unicefData
- https://github.com/OpenLMIS/openlmis-ref-distro
- https://github.com/WFP-VAM/DataBridgesAPIClient
