## 25. Pest and disease outbreaks reach smallholders only after the damage is visible: no area-level early warning for the fields next door

*Domain:* agriculture · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** Smallholder farmers growing staples in food-insecure regions, above all maize growers in sub-Saharan Africa (roughly 300 million Africans depend on maize) and rice/wheat growers in the Indo-Gangetic Plain and South/South-East Asia. Savary et al. (2019) found pest and pathogen yield losses are systematically higher in the food-insecure hotspots (SSA, Indo-Gangetic Plain, West Asia/North Africa) than in surplus-producing regions (US Midwest, NW Europe, southern Brazil/Argentina). Fall armyworm, which arrived in Africa in 2016 and Asia in 2018, is the archetype: FAO's 2018 Global Early Warning-Early Action report put its potential impact at 21-53% annual maize loss across 12 African maize-producing countries. Women and the poorest farmers are hit hardest because they cannot afford reactive spraying and are last to be visited by extension agents (Kenya reports about 1 extension officer per 1,093 farmers vs the FAO-recommended 1:400; India about 1 per 1,000 farming households).

**Scale and severity.** Savary et al., Nature Ecology & Evolution 2019 (989 assessments from 219 experts in 67 countries): global yield losses to pests and pathogens of 21.5% for wheat, 30.0% rice, 22.6% maize, 17.2% potato and 21.4% soybean, covering 84% of world production of those crops, with losses highest in food-insecure hotspots. FAO (2021 IPPC review, widely quoted): up to 40% of global crop production is lost to pests each year; plant diseases cost over USD 220 billion/yr and invasive insects at least USD 70 billion/yr. FAO EWEA report Jan-Mar 2018, citing CABI's 2017 evidence note: unmanaged fall armyworm could cause 21-53% annual maize loss in 12 African countries, 8.3-20.6 million tonnes/yr worth USD 2.48-6.18 billion. Ugandan farmers in the Makerere/Zindi fall-armyworm dataset self-report average maize losses of 31% (farmer-reported, not measured). The 2020 desert locust upsurge in East Africa cost FAO roughly USD 120 million in emergency response in 2020 alone, with documented localisation and timing gaps despite six-week outlooks (secondary synthesis). Caveat: the FAO 40% figure is a ceiling estimate and the Savary numbers are expert-elicited, not measured field trials; the FAW tonnage is a potential-loss model from 2017.

**Why existing solutions fail.** The field is crowded but almost entirely reactive and individual. Plantix (>10 million downloads) and PlantVillage Nuru (about 65% accuracy on cassava, roughly 50,000 Kenyan farmers) diagnose a photo after symptoms are visible, by which time an outbreak has already spread through the neighbourhood and the loss is largely locked in. CABI Plantwise (about 90 countries, >5,000 plant clinics, 13,200 plant doctors) is a human diagnostic/extension layer, not a forecast-led warning system. FAO's FAMEWS collects scouting and pheromone-trap data into a global map, but the alert rarely flows back to the farmer in the next village; a 2026 West-African product research dossier summarises it as 'village relays feed FAMEWS; what is missing is the return of the alert to the neighbouring producer'. Public warning systems (FAO DLIS for locusts, FAMEWS, EMPRES-i+) are loosely coupled stacks: weather forecasts in one module, field surveillance in another, heuristic decision rules in a third, so warnings are regionally correct but spatially coarse, producing both missed areas and blanket spraying where pests are absent, which erodes farmer trust. Extension ratios of 1:1,000+ mean the human channel cannot scale.

**What people do today.** Farmers walk fields and look at leaves (slow, usually late); ask neighbours or the local agro-dealer, who has an incentive to sell product; use calendar-based prophylactic spraying regardless of actual pressure (expensive, drives resistance, health risk); rely on radio bulletins and plant-clinic days that may be weeks away; apply home remedies (ash, soap, neem); or simply accept the loss. Better-resourced farmers photograph symptoms into Plantix or Nuru, and a few trained lead farmers relay diagnoses to 50-100 neighbours without smartphones.

**Proposed intervention.** An open, area-based pest-pressure early-warning and scouting network that closes the loop between one farmer's observation and the neighbouring farms. Core engine: deterministic, published biophysical models (degree-day generation timing for fall armyworm and stem borers, infection-period rules for potato late blight and wheat rusts, rainfall/vegetation breeding-suitability for locusts) computed per grid cell from free weather data (Open-Meteo hourly/16-day forecasts and 80-year archive, no API key; CHIRPS rainfall). Layered on top: structured, geotagged scouting reports (trap counts, larvae per plant, photo-verified sightings) from farmers, lead farmers and plant doctors, which upgrade a cell from 'conditions favourable' to 'confirmed, scout now' and push text alerts (SMS/WhatsApp, feature-phone friendly) to every registered farm within a configurable radius, with the economic threshold and IPM-first action for that crop stage. Extension officers get a web console showing where to scout and where not to spray. Records export in FAMEWS-compatible form rather than competing with it. What is novel: it is predictive and neighbourhood-level rather than reactive and individual, it treats a farmer's report as a public good for the next village, and it publishes its false-alarm and lead-time statistics. It works without AI (the phenology models are 1970s-2000s crop science); image triage is an optional add-on.

**Works across countries?.** High. The pests are transboundary (fall armyworm is now in 80+ countries across Africa and Asia; locusts span the Sahel to South Asia; late blight and rusts are global) and the phenology models are biophysical, needing only local temperature/rain inputs plus per-region calibration of thresholds and sowing dates. FAO FAMEWS and CABI PlantwisePlus already operate multi-country, so there are institutional counterparts in most target countries. Localisation of crop calendars, languages and economic thresholds is the real work.

**Sustainable business model.** Farmers never pay. Revenue from (a) cooperatives/FPOs and contract-farming companies paying per-member-season for alerts and scouting dashboards; (b) governments, NMHS and NGOs licensing district deployments and scouting-network management (replacing paper surveillance); (c) insurers and lenders buying pest-pressure indices for pest-index covers and portfolio risk; (d) input distributors paying for demand forecasts. Guardrails to protect the social value: no pay-per-spray or sponsored product recommendations, IPM-first thresholds, open model code, and published alert accuracy. Donor funding is appropriate for seeding scouting networks in the first countries.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web MVP by a small team: Next.js or Django front end, Postgres/PostGIS, email/phone OTP auth, a scheduled worker pulling Open-Meteo forecasts and archive per grid cell, 3-5 published degree-day/infection-period models in plain code, a geotagged report form with photo upload, an alert engine via Twilio or Africa's Talking, and a map console. Real public data exists: Open-Meteo (free, CC BY 4.0, no key), CHIRPS rainfall, FAO GIEWS crop calendars, EPPO/CABI pest datasheets for thresholds and development parameters. What is hard: FAMEWS and national surveillance data are not available as open APIs, so ground truth must come from your own user reports at first (cold start); model calibration per region; false alarms; SMS costs; and demonstrating impact needs matched-village comparisons. No custom hardware or regulatory approval is required, though pheromone traps improve signal and cost money.

**Measurable outcomes.** Lead time in days between first confirmed detection in an area and alert receipt by neighbouring farms; share of alerted farmers who scouted within 72 hours and share who found the pest (true-positive rate) versus false-alarm rate; measured yield loss (crop-cut or farmer-recall) in alerted vs non-alerted villages; number of prophylactic sprays avoided per hectare; cost per farmer-season reached; proportion of area under confirmed pressure that received a timely IPM action; and reports exported into national/FAO surveillance.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/m-rui001/pdfonly-stem/HEAD/out/html/02390b16014c12aa3404a45094dfb051cacb3659/index.html (mirror of Savary et al. 2019, Nature Ecol & Evol, doi:10.1038/s41559-018-0793-y)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/clulab/indra-to-lucene/HEAD/src/test/resources/text/21_i8343en.txt (mirror of FAO Global Early Warning-Early Action Report on Food Security and Agriculture, Jan-Mar 2018, fall armyworm section)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ZindiAfrica/Computer-Vision/82cf494cd4fa219b7af27b6abd7f8d6898ea9d08/Image%20Classification/Makerere%20Fall%20Armyworm%20Crop%20Challenge/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Panta-Rhei-Research/site/HEAD/impact/papers/pest-disease-livestock-stress-early-warning.md (secondary synthesis of FAO DLIS/FAMEWS, Plantwise, Plantix landscape)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/misterkante/alerteagri/HEAD/docs/JUSTIFICATION_RECHERCHE.md (cites PLOS One FAMEWS village-relay study and FAO Benin)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/gadda00/victor-portfolio/HEAD/services/guides/ai-for-farmers.html (Kenya extension ratio, Nuru/Plantix reach; secondary)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/dabi-team/someData/HEAD/data/Software/2206.11784.txt (arXiv 2206.11784, Plantwise clinic and plant-doctor counts)
- https://api.github.com/search/code?q=%2240+percent+of+global+crop+production%22+pests (FAO 2021 40%/USD 220bn/USD 70bn figures as quoted in mirrored texts)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-meteo/open-meteo/main/README.md

## 26. Counterfeit and diluted farm inputs (seed, fertiliser, agrochemicals) make modern inputs unprofitable and keep adoption low

*Domain:* agriculture · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** Smallholder farmers buying from local agro-input retailers in East and West Africa and South Asia, where inputs are repackaged from large bags into small lots and sold unbranded. Over 70% of Africans living under USD 1.25/day are engaged in smallholder farming (IFAD 2011, cited in Ashour et al. 2019). The best-measured case is Uganda's maize belt (Bold et al. 2017; Ashour et al. 2019), but regulators in Kenya (PCPB, KEPHIS, KEBS), Tanzania, Nigeria, Ghana, India (CIB&RC, state seed certification) and Bangladesh all run anti-counterfeit programmes, indicating the same structure. Victims are disproportionately farmers with the least ability to test or to absorb a failed season, and farm workers and families exposed to mislabeled chemicals.

**Scale and severity.** Bold, Kaizzi, Svensson and Yanagizawa-Drott, Quarterly Journal of Economics 2017 (369 urea samples from 129 randomly sampled retail shops in Uganda's two main maize regions, plus 30 hybrid-seed samples): on average 30% of nutrient is missing from fertiliser, 95% of samples are diluted by 10-50%, and hybrid maize seed contains less than 50% authentic seed. Economic consequence: the mean rate of return on fertiliser bought in local markets is about -15%, versus roughly 50% for authentic product; if all samples had been authentic, 99% would have yielded returns above 70%. Ashour, Gilligan, Hoel and Karachiwalla, Journal of Development Studies 2019 (IFPRI, Uganda glyphosate): the average bottle is missing 15% of active ingredient, 31% of samples contain less than 75% of the advertised concentration, farmers believe 41% of herbicide is fake, yet the price gap between a fully authentic and a zero-glyphosate bottle is only 5.8% of the average price, so the market does not punish fakes. The authors attribute about 60% of low-quality herbicide to agro-dealers and informal sellers removing or reusing labels. Market-wide estimates for East Africa of 15-30% counterfeit inputs circulate but I could not verify them from a primary source, so the continental scale is lower-confidence than the Ugandan measurements.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Anti-counterfeit tools protect the label, not the contents. Scratch-code and SMS verification (KEPHIS seed labels, mPedigree-style codes) confirm a package left a licensed factory, but Bold et al. show quality is destroyed downstream: wholesalers bulk out fertiliser or dye grain to look like hybrid seed, and retailers repack large bags into small unbranded lots and store them in the open, so there is no code to scratch. Official product registers exist (PCPB, KEPHIS, MAAIF, CIB&RC) but are PDFs or web tables a farmer cannot query at the shop counter, and they say nothing about a specific dealer or batch. Lab testing is expensive and one-off (the QJE study was a research project, not a running system), and results are never published at dealer level, so dealers face no consequences. Finally, because yields are noisy, farmers cannot learn quality from their own harvests (Bold et al.'s calibrated learning model), so reputation alone does not discipline the market; the authors show this is why the market neither corrects nor fully collapses.

**What people do today.** Buying tiny quantities to trial on part of a plot; sticking to one trusted dealer or brand; paying modest price premiums that, per Ashour et al., recover only a fraction of quality differences; relying on neighbours' experience; reverting to saved seed and no fertiliser (which is the low-adoption equilibrium the papers document); occasional complaints to the regulator by SMS (Kenya's KEPHIS 1393 line) with little feedback; and NGO or cooperative bulk purchasing directly from manufacturers where such schemes exist.

**Proposed intervention.** An open input-quality ledger that moves accountability from the label to the dealer and the batch. Four components: (1) a registry API that ingests official registered-product lists (PCPB, KEPHIS, CIB&RC, state seed catalogues) into one queryable database so any product name, registration number, batch or dealer can be checked by web, SMS or WhatsApp text and returns registered / banned / expired / not found; (2) a dealer-level quality record built from structured, geotagged farmer reports with a cheap, standardised evidence protocol (seven-day paper-towel germination test photographed day by day, lot numbers, expiry photos, price paid), so that patterns at one shop become visible instead of anecdotal; (3) a sentinel-sampling workflow for regulators, NGOs and seed companies: randomised mystery purchases, chain-of-custody, partner-lab nutrient or germination results, and publication per dealer and batch, turning expensive one-off studies like Bold et al. into a continuous public dataset; (4) a dispute channel that routes substantiated cases to the regulator and records the outcome. The novelty is publishing measured quality at the point where dilution actually happens, with an open scoring method, rather than another QR code. It is useful without any AI; image models for label matching are optional.

**Works across countries?.** Medium-high. The lemon-market mechanism and the existence of a national register are common to Uganda, Kenya, Tanzania, Nigeria, Ghana, Ethiopia, India, Bangladesh and Pakistan. What differs is register format and legal exposure: registers range from clean tables to scanned PDFs, defamation rules for publishing dealer scores vary, and regulators differ in willingness to act on crowdsourced evidence. A country-by-country adapter for registers and a conservative evidence standard are needed.

**Sustainable business model.** Farmers never pay for lookups or reporting. Revenue from (a) seed and agrochemical companies paying for brand-protection analytics and a verified-dealer programme (they lose the most from fakes); (b) regulators and donors funding sentinel sampling and lab testing, which the platform manages at lower cost than ad-hoc studies; (c) microfinance institutions and input-credit lenders paying for dealer quality scores to decide which shops their loans can be redeemed at; (d) insurers bundling quality assurance with input-linked policies. Social-value guardrails: no pay-to-list or pay-to-improve-score, open scoring code, farmer reports owned by farmers, and company sponsors cannot see or influence individual dealer records.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web MVP: Postgres with product, batch, dealer and report tables; auth for farmers, dealers, regulators and lab partners; scrapers/importers for two or three registers (Kenya PCPB registered pest-control products list, KEPHIS seed variety list, India CIB&RC registered pesticides) which are public web pages or PDFs; an SMS/WhatsApp lookup bot via Africa's Talking or Twilio; a germination-test wizard with photo upload; dealer map and public batch pages. Hard parts, stated honestly: register data is messy and sometimes offline, there is no official API; ground truth for nutrient content needs physical sampling and lab tests, which is a logistics and funding task outside the software; publishing dealer-level scores carries defamation risk, so the MVP should publish only lab-verified and multiply-corroborated findings; and the cold-start problem means partnering with one cooperative or regulator from day one. No custom hardware or pre-use regulatory approval is required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Lab-measured nutrient content and germination rates at sampled shops over successive seasons (the Bold et al. metric) in covered vs uncovered districts; share of lookups returning unregistered/banned products and the trend over time; repeat-offence rate per dealer after publication; regulator enforcement actions traceable to platform evidence; fertiliser and hybrid-seed adoption rates and measured yields among users; share of farmer reports resolved with a documented outcome; and the gap between price and measured quality (which should widen if the market starts to price quality).

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AMindToThink/PublicPaperKeyWords/HEAD/data/txtpapers2024-04-21/Agricultural_Technology_In_Africa/Lemon_technologies_and_adoption%5Bcol%5D_measurement%2C_theory_and_evidence_from_agricultural_markets_in_Uganda.txt (mirror of Bold et al. 2017, QJE 132(3):1055-1100, doi:10.1093/qje/qjx009, ZORA accepted version)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AMindToThink/PublicPaperKeyWords/HEAD/data/txtpapers2024-04-21/Agricultural_Technology_In_Africa/Do_Beliefs_About_Herbicide_Quality_Correspond_with_Actual_Quality_in_Local_Markets__Evidence_from_Uganda.txt (mirror of Ashour et al. 2019, J. Development Studies 55(6):1285-1306, doi:10.1080/00220388.2018.1464143)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bashman1/AgriTrust/HEAD/suggestion/improved_problem_statements.md (secondary; describes PCPB/KEPHIS/KEBS register gap)
- https://api.github.com/search/code?q=KEPHIS+seed+label+SMS+verify+counterfeit+Kenya (Kenya-Hub agrovet directory noting KEPHIS SMS 1393 reporting line)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bjvca/Seed_systems_project/HEAD/papers/clearinghouse_training_paper/seed_systems_pap.bib (seed-systems literature pointers, Uganda National Seed Policy)

## 27. Weather-timing failures (false-start sowing, dry spells, harvest-time rain) and disaster losses hit smallholders who get no field-level decision support, feeding crop failure, debt and, in India, suicide

*Domain:* agriculture · *Researcher confidence:* 0.7

**Who experiences it.** Rain-fed smallholders in sub-Saharan Africa, South Asia and Central America who must decide when to sow, top-dress, spray and harvest on the basis of rains they cannot forecast. Africa loses the largest share of its agricultural GDP to disasters (7.7%, FAO 2023). In India, about 125 million small and marginal farming households, half of them indebted, face monsoon variability with the thinnest buffers: NSS 77th round (2019) puts average monthly agricultural-household income at Rs 10,218 against average outstanding loans of Rs 74,121. Feature-phone users are the excluded majority in the poorest regions (GSMA 2024: smartphones were 54% of sub-Saharan connections and 53% of covered people do not use mobile internet; Benin end-2025: 78.1% unique mobile subscribers but 64.9% mobile-internet subscribers).

**Scale and severity.** FAO, The Impact of Disasters on Agriculture and Food Security 2023 (quoted in FAO Statistical Yearbook 2023, Box 1): disasters caused USD 3.8 trillion of crop and livestock production losses in 1991-2021, USD 123 billion per year, 5% of global agricultural GDP; Asia USD 1,720 billion in absolute terms, Africa USD 580 billion but the highest share of agricultural GDP (7.7%), Americas 7.5%, Europe 7.2%, Asia 3.6%. WMO State of the Global Climate 2024 (WMO-No. 1368): 'Only half of all countries worldwide have adequate multi-hazard early warning systems'; 2024 brought Sahel floods affecting large cropland areas, severe drought in Zimbabwe, Zambia, Botswana and Namibia with major agricultural impacts, and the highest number of new weather-related displacements since 2008. WMO/UNDRR Global Status of MHEWS 2025: 119 countries report a system (113% more than ten years earlier) but only 43% of small island developing states. Downstream in India, NCRB ADSI 2022: 11,290 suicides in the farming sector (5,207 cultivators, 6,083 agricultural labourers), up 3.75% from 10,881 in 2021; NSS 77th round (MoSPI Report 587, 2019): 50.2% of agricultural households indebted, average loan Rs 74,121. Caveat: suicide causation is multi-factor (NCRB lists indebtedness, crop failure and family issues); I present crop failure and debt as documented contributors, not as the sole cause, and the disaster-loss figures are modelled production losses, not farm-level survey data.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Weather information exists but is not turned into field decisions. National agromet advisories (for example India's Gramin Krishi Mausam Sewa SMS service, regional FEWS NET outlooks) are district-level and generic ('light to moderate rain likely'), not tied to a specific farmer's crop stage and plot. Commercial forecast vendors (Ignitia, Tomorrow.io, the defunct aWhere) sell forecasts, but a forecast is not a sowing decision, and working with raw model output 'requires expertise in binary formats, grid systems and map projections' (Open-Meteo). Smartphone apps exclude the half of covered people who are not online. Forecast skill in the tropics is limited to about two weeks, while onset rules (Sivakumar 1988: 20 mm in 3 days with no 7-day dry spell in the following 30 days) need a longer horizon, so services either overpromise or go silent. Early-warning coverage is still missing in half of countries, and when warnings exist they rarely say what to do on a given plot this week. Safety nets fail on execution: India's PMFBY guidelines require claims within 2 weeks of calculation with 12% penal interest beyond 30 days and a 7-day district grievance response, yet delayed settlements are a persistent criticism, and a 2017-2022 state panel found farm-credit growth and insurance uptake move independently, so farmers borrow without cover.

**What people do today.** Sowing on the first storms and re-sowing after a false start (double seed and labour cost); staggered or split planting to hedge; indigenous indicators (winds, flowering trees, bird behaviour); radio bulletins and waiting to see what neighbours do; harvesting early or late to avoid rain, then losing grain to mould (aflatoxin infection rises from 10-20% of grains at storage entry to 54-79% after six months in Benin granaries, IITA); after a failed season, selling livestock, borrowing from commission agents at about 1.5% per month, migrating for wages, or filing insurance grievances with no evidence of the weather event. In 2026 Zimbabwe's Met service and FEWS NET explicitly warned farmers not to plant on the first storms given a 65% chance of below-normal rain.

**Proposed intervention.** A rule-based, explainable field-decision engine that converts open weather data into crop-stage-specific decision windows and delivers them as plain text to feature phones and to a web console for extension agents and cooperatives. Each farmer registers plot location, crop and sowing date; the engine computes, per plot, (a) sowing: published onset criteria applied to observed rainfall plus the 16-day ensemble forecast's dry-spell probability, with an explicit 'this is a 16-day view of a 30-day rule' honesty flag; (b) top-dressing and spraying windows from rain and wind forecasts; (c) harvest and drying windows from rain-free days and humidity for safe grain moisture; (d) a dated weather-evidence record (rainfall, dry-spell length, extreme heat) for the plot that the farmer can attach to a crop-insurance claim or grievance. Every advisory states the rule and the data behind it. Novelty: it ships decisions and decision rules, not forecasts; it is open and auditable; it serves USSD/SMS users, not only app users; and it logs outcomes (did the farmer sow, did a false start occur) so that its own value can be measured. It is useful without any AI; the science is agro-meteorology from the 1980s plus modern open ensembles. Fabregas, Kremer and Schilbach (Science 2019) found phone-delivered agricultural information raises yields by about 4% and recommended-input adoption by 22% at near-zero marginal cost, which is the evidence base for this delivery channel.

**Works across countries?.** High for the engine, medium for the rules. Open-Meteo and CHIRPS are global, and FAO/GIEWS crop calendars cover most countries. Onset and dry-spell thresholds were derived for Sudano-Sahelian West Africa and must be recalibrated for bimodal East Africa, monsoon South Asia and highland systems; national meteorological services should be partners, not competitors, since WMO's Early Warnings for All initiative is pushing exactly this last-mile gap. Language, units and crop sets are country work.

**Sustainable business model.** B2B2C. Cooperatives, FPOs and contract-farming firms pay per member-season; microfinance institutions and index insurers bundle the service with loans and policies because timely decisions reduce defaults and basis-risk disputes, and the plot-level weather record cuts claim-verification cost; governments and national met services white-label it as the last mile of their advisory mandate; donors fund the first countries. Farmers get the text channel free; the main variable cost is SMS, so partnerships with telcos (zero-rated USSD) matter. Guardrails: no sale of farmer location data, no input-company sponsorship of advisories, open rules.

**MVP feasibility.** Straightforward as a web MVP with backend, Postgres/PostGIS and auth: a scheduler pulls Open-Meteo forecasts and ERA5-based archive per registered plot (free, no key), a rules module implements onset, dry-spell, spray and harvest-window criteria, a notification service sends SMS/USSD text via Africa's Talking or Twilio, and a console shows plots, rule firings and outcomes. Real public data: Open-Meteo, CHIRPS, FAO GIEWS crop calendars, national met-service bulletins. Honest difficulties: tropical forecast skill beyond 5-7 days is modest and the product must display uncertainty rather than hide it; onset rules need local calibration and a season of data to tune; SMS cost at scale; measuring impact credibly requires a comparison group; and the insurance-evidence feature only has teeth where insurers or courts accept third-party weather records. No custom hardware or regulatory approval is required for use.

**Measurable outcomes.** Re-sowing (false-start) rate and seed/labour cost per hectare among users vs comparable non-users; share of sowing events that fell inside a verified viable window; yield per hectare and input wasted; days of lead time before dry spells or harvest rain; share of users on feature phones; number and settlement rate of insurance claims or grievances filed with the platform's weather record and the time to settlement vs the 2-week/30-day PMFBY benchmarks; season-over-season retention; and cost per farmer-season.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/IDOEFRAIM/Agribot-AI/HEAD/backend/sources/raw_data/fao_publications/pdfs/extracted_text/cc8166en.txt (mirror of FAO 2023 World Food and Agriculture Statistical Yearbook, doi:10.4060/cc8166en, Box 1 quoting FAO 2023 Impact of Disasters on Agriculture and Food Security, doi:10.4060/cc7900en)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/dvlastnik/rag-mendelu/HEAD/data/drough/WMO-1368-2025_en.md (mirror of WMO State of the Global Climate 2024, WMO-No. 1368)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/viraj43/RAG_benchmark/HEAD/results/raw/q205_Firecrawl.txt (captured UN report listing with WMO/UNDRR Global Status of Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems 2025 summary: 119 countries, 43% of SIDS)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mandavi-singh/krishigpt-nano/HEAD/data/corpus_v2/raw/wiki__Agriculture_in_India.txt (NCRB ADSI 2022 farming-sector suicide figures as cited)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bebhuvan/measure-of-india/HEAD/docs/reference/farm-households.md (NSS 77th round Situation Assessment Survey 2019, MoSPI Report 587, Statement 5.7.1: Rs 74,121 and 50.2%)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rathodvamshi/Ai-bharat/HEAD/knowledge_base/txt%20files/PMFBY%20Revised%20Operational%20Guidelines.txt (mirror of PMFBY Revised Operational Guidelines: claim timelines, 12% penal interest, grievance mechanism)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AdityaSharma1303/Studying-Agri-and-Credit-relation-in-India-/HEAD/README.md (student panel analysis of RBI credit vs PMFBY uptake 2017-2022; low weight)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/misterkante/alerteagri/HEAD/docs/JUSTIFICATION_RECHERCHE.md (cites Fabregas, Kremer & Schilbach 2019 Science 366:eaay3038; Sivakumar 1988; GSMA 2024; ARCEP Benin; IITA aflatoxin study)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Michelle-Vava/kurima/HEAD/ROADMAP.md (cites FEWS NET Zimbabwe Sept 2026 and CGIAR on shifting seasons)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-meteo/open-meteo/main/README.md
- https://api.github.com/search/code?q=Sivakumar+1988+onset+rains+%2220+mm%22+dry+spell+false+start+sowing (ClimSeasonR implementation of the onset criterion)

## 28. Scam victims have no clear path to assert payment rights, report once, or recover money (older adults and non-English speakers hit hardest)

*Domain:* financial · *Researcher confidence:* 0.78

**Who experiences it.** Consumers who are socially engineered into sending money (investment/'pig-butchering', government and bank impersonation, 'digital arrest', romance, tech-support, job scams). Hardest hit: adults 60+ (US IC3 2024: 147,127 complaints, $4.885bn, the highest per-victim losses, ~$83k average per reporting elder victim per AARP restatement); limited-English-proficiency immigrants (the FTC formally found Spanish speakers more likely to be victimised and less likely to report; Chinese-speaking communities targeted by consulate/police impersonation, ~$164k average loss per reporting victim in IC3 PSA 190328); middle-class Indians targeted by 'digital arrest' (senior citizens filed 103,488 complaints reporting Rs 4,005 crore in 2025, ~4x the average complaint); Global South adults in Kenya, Nigeria, Brazil, Indonesia where GASA finds the highest exposure rates (Kenya 83% of adults encountered a scam in GASA 2025).

**Scale and severity.** Caveat: primary sites (ftc.gov, ic3.gov, gasa.org, ukfinance.org.uk, psr.org.uk, sansad.in) were unreachable from this session's network; figures below were read from GitHub-hosted transcriptions/figure registries that cite those primaries by URL. US: FTC Consumer Sentinel Data Book 2024 (Mar 2025) — $12.5bn reported fraud losses in 2024, +25% on 2023, 6.47m reports; investment fraud $5.7bn, imposter scams $2.95bn, government imposters $789m, job scams $501m; FTC 2025 preliminary ~$15.9bn (+24%). FBI IC3 2024 Internet Crime Report — $16.6bn losses (+33%), average loss per incident up from $14,197 to $19,372; victims 60+: $4.885bn (+43%), 7,500+ elders lost >$100k each; IC3 2025: $20.9bn, elder losses $7.7bn. FTC's 'Protecting Older Consumers' report estimates true elder losses far higher after under-reporting (transcription cites ~$82bn for 2024; treat as indicative). Global: GASA Global State of Scams 2024 (58,329 respondents, 43 countries) estimated US$1.03 trillion stolen in 12 months, only 4% of victims recovered any money; GASA 2025 (46,000 adults, 42 markets, changed method) estimates $442bn, 57% of adults experienced a scam, 23% lost money, 27% never report, 36% don't know whom to report to. UK: UK Finance Annual Fraud Report 2026 — £1.28bn payment fraud in 2025 incl. £576.4m APP scams (248,070 cases); GASA/Cifas UK 2025 survey: £9.4bn, 20% of adults had money taken. India: MHA answer to Rajya Sabha (Q.1345, 11 Feb 2026) — Rs 22,495 crore (~$2.6bn) reported lost to cyber fraud in 2025 across 2.4m NCRP complaints; Rs 55,649 crore reported 2021-25. Indonesia: OJK Satgas PASTI cumulative public losses Rp 139.67 trillion (~$8.6bn) since 2017 (Bisnis.com, Jan 2025). Severity beyond money: GASA 2025 — 69% of victims report significant stress, 14% cut normal spending; sextortion-linked suicides among minors documented by FBI/HSI.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Defences are mostly pre-loss awareness ('check grammar', 'too good to be true') — GASA 2025 shows 73% of adults feel confident spotting scams yet 57% were scammed, and AI removes the cues people rely on. (2) Reporting is fragmented and single-agency: in the US a victim must separately contact their bank (with different legal rules for cards/Reg E vs. authorised wires/UCC 4A), FTC ReportFraud, IC3, state AG, the platform, phone carrier and any crypto exchange; 36% of victims globally don't know whom to tell, 27% never report, and reports rarely feed back any outcome. (3) Recovery depends on knowing time-boxed rights the victim has never heard of: Reg E dispute windows, chargeback windows, the UK PSR 13-month APP claim window, India's 'golden hour' NCRP/1930 freeze. The UK shows the process matters: after mandatory APP reimbursement (Oct 2024) in-scope reimbursement rose to 89% of lost value, 82% of claims closed within 5 business days, and independent evaluation found in-scope APP losses fell ~21% (~£73m/yr). The US has no equivalent for authorised payments (GASA: only 30% of those who reported to their payment provider recovered anything). (4) Victims are re-victimised by 'recovery scams' because there is no trusted post-loss process. (5) Bank-side tools (Confirmation of Payee, India's Financial Fraud Risk Indicator, Singapore ScamShield) require bank/telco integration and do not help after money has left.

**What people do today.** Victims google 'how to get money back', call their bank's general line (often after the dispute window), file with one agency (FTC or IC3) and hear nothing, post in Reddit/Facebook groups, call AARP Fraud Watch helpline (human, US-only), hire 'fund recovery' firms that are frequently scams themselves, or stay silent out of shame (27% never report; isolation tactics are a documented core of the script). Family members try to intervene but have no standing with the bank. In India victims dial 1930 / cybercrime.gov.in if they know it exists; in the UK Action Fraud and the bank, then the Financial Ombudsman.

**Proposed intervention.** A 'fraud rights and recovery case engine': a web app where a victim (or a trusted contact/caregiver they authorise) describes one incident once — payment rail (card, ACH/Faster Payments/UPI, wire, crypto, gift card, cash), dates, counterparties, evidence — and a deterministic, jurisdiction-specific rules engine (rules-as-code, no AI required) computes which legal protections apply (e.g., US Reg E unauthorised-EFT rights vs. authorised-push with no reimbursement right; UK PSR mandatory APP reimbursement eligibility and the 13-month deadline; India NCRP freeze and RBI compensation pilot conditions), every deadline, and the exact ordered action list. It then auto-generates and tracks all filings from one evidence pack: bank dispute letter with the correct regulatory citations, FTC/IC3/Action Fraud/NCRP submissions, platform and crypto-exchange abuse reports, carrier reports, chargeback requests, plus a Financial Ombudsman/escalation path when the bank misses statutory response times. Differentiators: (a) rights-and-deadline engine per rail and country, maintained as versioned open rules; (b) report-once routing with status tracking and reminders; (c) a built-in 'recovery-scam shield' that warns when anyone offers paid recovery; (d) trusted-contact mode that gives families a structured role; (e) anonymised, consented aggregation of counterparties (crypto addresses, payee names, phone numbers, URLs) checked against public datasets such as Chainabuse, FTC Do-Not-Call complaint data and published PSAs, feeding a 'is this payee already reported' lookup. AI is optional (mapping free text to the structured incident), the core works without it.

**Works across countries?.** Yes, by jurisdiction packs: the engine's logic is a per-country rule set over payment rails and regulators. Start with US (Reg E/Reg Z/UCC 4A, FTC, IC3, state AGs), UK (PSR reimbursement rules, Action Fraud, FOS) and India (NCRP/1930, RBI pilot), then Australia (Scamwatch, Scams Prevention Framework), Singapore, EU (PSD2/PSD3). The scam scripts are globally shared (GASA: 43 countries), so the incident taxonomy and evidence pack generalise; only the rights and routing layer is local.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for victims, always, and never a percentage of recovered funds (that is the recovery-scam model). Revenue from institutions that bear the losses or the duty of care: white-label/API licences for credit unions, community banks, fintechs and payment firms (who must meet Reg E/PSR response deadlines and want structured, complete claims); employer and insurer benefits (EAPs, identity-theft insurance); senior-living operators and elder-care networks; legal-aid and consumer-protection organisations. A second line is a consented, de-identified counterparty-intelligence feed for banks and platforms. Grants/philanthropy (AARP, Cifas-type bodies) can seed jurisdiction packs. The institutional customer's incentive (faster, cleaner, lawful claims) is aligned with the victim's.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web MVP by a small team: auth, case/incident schema, rules engine (versioned JSON/YAML rules per country/rail with deadline arithmetic), document generation (dispute letters/report pre-fills), reminder scheduler (email/SMS), status tracking, trusted-contact sharing, and a counterparty lookup. Real public data: FTC Consumer Sentinel Data Book and state-level tables (PDF/CSV), FTC Do-Not-Call reported-calls dataset (data.gov), FBI IC3 PSAs and annual reports, PSR APP reimbursement dashboard, UK Finance reports, Chainabuse (public reports/API), published scam-script corpora (e.g., I4C advisories). Hard parts: most agencies have no submission API (FTC, IC3, Action Fraud are web forms), so 'filing' means pre-filled packets and tracked manual submission; keeping legal rules current needs a review process; defamation/abuse risk in a payee lookup requires conservative language ('reported by N users', never 'scammer') and evidence thresholds; no regulatory approval is needed to operate, but bank partnerships are slow. No custom hardware or logistics.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of cases with the bank dispute filed inside the statutory window (vs. baseline); median hours from loss to first bank notification; dollars reimbursed/charged back per case and per 1,000 cases (verified by uploaded bank outcome); share of victims who complete reports to two or more competent bodies (baseline: 27% report nowhere); recovery-scam re-victimisation rate among users (self-reported at 90 days); for institutional customers: claim completeness rate, time-to-resolution against PSR/Reg E deadlines, and reduction in complaint escalations to the ombudsman. Vanity metrics (sign-ups, page views) excluded.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/email-sendauth/draft-sendauth/HEAD/research/ic3-fraud-data.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GemScan/GemScan/HEAD/research/2_problem_statement_and_market_research.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/fyncom/karmacall-website/HEAD/src/pages/blog/global-scam-crisis-2025-economic-defense.mdx
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/siderightapps-hub/beatthescam-site/HEAD/content/uk-scam-statistics.json
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BU1lDR/SACH/HEAD/deck/fact_pack.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vaniachow/elder-fraud-dataset/HEAD/fraud_typology.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Basit3751/ftc-fraud-2024-analysis/HEAD/README.md

## 29. Means-tested benefits and tax credits that eligible households never receive, and benefits lost to paperwork (non-take-up and procedural churn)

*Domain:* financial · *Researcher confidence:* 0.75

**Who experiences it.** Low-income households entitled to means-tested support who do not claim or lose it at renewal: in Great Britain ~1.2m households not claiming Universal Credit, ~2.7m people not claiming Council Tax Support, pensioners not claiming Pension Credit (gateway to other help) and Attendance Allowance; in the US eligible elderly (only 55% of SNAP-eligible elderly participate), working households with earnings (76%), eligible non-citizens (50%), WIC-eligible families (56% reach), EITC-eligible workers (about 1 in 5 do not claim), and Medicaid enrollees disenrolled for paperwork reasons. Disproportionately older people, disabled people, carers, people with limited digital access or literacy, and non-native speakers — the groups least able to navigate forms.

**Scale and severity.** Caveat: gov.uk, fns.usda.gov, irs.gov and policyinpractice.co.uk were unreachable from this session; figures come from GitHub-hosted copies including a scraped copy of the IRS EITC participation table, PolicyEngine's source metadata for DWP take-up statistics, and two independent transcriptions of Policy in Practice's report. UK: Policy in Practice 'Missing Out 2025' (Feb 2025) estimates £23bn of income-related benefits and social tariffs unclaimed per year in Great Britain (restated as £24bn in Nov 2025); components transcribed: Universal Credit £7.5-9.6bn (~1.2m households), Attendance Allowance £5.2bn, Pension Credit £3.5bn, Council Tax Support £2.8-3bn (~2.7m people), broadband social tariffs unclaimed by ~97% of eligible households. DWP 'Income-related benefits: estimates of take-up FYE 2024' (published 30 Oct 2025) is the official series; modelled Pension Credit take-up is around two-thirds (PolicyEngine parameter 0.70 citing DWP; UKMOD 0.69 for guarantee credit), and working-age take-up is no longer published. Take-up assumptions for UC range from 55% (PolicyEngine) to 80% (Resolution Foundation) — a 25-point uncertainty on the largest working-age benefit. US: USDA 'Trends in SNAP Participation Rates FY2020-FY2022' — 88% of eligible individuals in FY2022 (record high) but 55% of eligible elderly, 76% of households with earnings; WIC reached 56.1% of eligible people in 2023 (24.0% in non-metro areas); IRS EITC participation 80.8% in TY2021 and TY2022 (76.3% in TY2020). Churn: of 56.1m Medicaid renewals due Mar-Dec 2023, 9.7m people were disenrolled for procedural reasons — 71% of all terminations (medicaid.gov unwinding data); a 2026 working paper finds states with higher procedural-termination rates also saw larger SNAP enrolment declines through shared eligibility workers. Severity: lost income is concentrated on the poorest; Policy in Practice reports its council clients identified £2bn of unclaimed support for 1.2m people in one year, implying the gap is recoverable at scale.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Calculators stop at 'you may be entitled to £X': Turn2us, Entitledto and Policy in Practice's Better Off Calculator (UK) and Benefit Kitchen/mRelief-style screeners (US) do not assemble the application, chase evidence or track the decision — the UK landscape review found 'no application help' and that proactive discovery exists only as council-procured analytics (LIFT), not for citizens. (2) Administrative burden is the binding constraint, not awareness alone: 30+ page Attendance Allowance forms, 300+ different local Council Tax Support schemes, SNAP recertification intervals, Medicaid renewal notices — and the 71% procedural-termination share shows people lose benefits they still qualify for. (3) Government 'tell us once' life-event services are rare (Estonia's proactive family benefits are the exception); the UK's Tell Us Once only cancels the deceased's entitlements, it does not open the survivor's. (4) Commercial 'claims companies' fill the gap for tax allowances by taking 35%+ of the award. (5) Government AI pilots are focused on call steering and fraud detection, and algorithmic welfare tools have a record of wrongly excluding people (Robodebt AU$1.8bn settlement), so trust is low.

**What people do today.** People rely on Citizens Advice, Age UK, StepChange or food-bank volunteers (human advice that does not scale: 64% of welfare advisers report difficulty getting UC information), run multiple online calculators, ask on Facebook groups, pay claims firms, or simply forgo the money. For renewals they wait for a letter, miss it, lose the benefit and reapply from scratch. US food banks and community health workers do ad-hoc SNAP/WIC help desks; Code for America's GetCalFresh shows digitised application assistance works but exists for one programme in one state.

**Proposed intervention.** An 'entitlement-to-award' pipeline for trusted intermediaries (food banks, housing associations, credit unions, GP social prescribers, libraries, employers) built on open rules-as-code: (1) multi-programme screening via the public PolicyEngine API (US and UK, Canada) and OpenFisca (France) including local schemes (e.g., encoding England's Council Tax Support schemes), (2) automatic assembly of the actual application — pre-filled official PDF/online forms with a per-programme evidence checklist and plain-language explanations, (3) a case tracker with statutory decision timescales, mandatory-reconsideration/appeal deadlines and recertification dates, with SMS/email nudges to the claimant and the caseworker, (4) event-triggered re-screening (birth, bereavement, job loss, turning pension age, rent change) so entitlements are opened when they arise, and (5) outcome capture (award letter uploaded, £/$ confirmed) so impact is measured as money received, not calculations run. What is novel: closing the gap between 'eligible' and 'paid', across programmes, with recertification handled as a first-class workflow, and a 'benefits cliff' check before each income change. No AI is required (rules engine + forms + scheduling); AI can optionally map interview notes to form fields with human review.

**Works across countries?.** Applies wherever self-applied means-tested programmes exist and rules are encoded: US (federal and state via PolicyEngine US), UK (PolicyEngine UK), Canada (PolicyEngine Canada), France (OpenFisca/mes-aides). Elsewhere the rules layer must be authored first; countries with registry-targeted cash transfers (much of the Global South) have a different failure mode (registry errors) and are a poorer fit. Form assembly and deadline logic are local; the case model and intermediary workflow are universal.

**Sustainable business model.** Free to claimants and never a share of the award. Subscriptions or outcome-based contracts with intermediaries and local authorities that already pay for this (Policy in Practice reports £416 return per £1 for one council; councils, housing associations and utilities have arrears-reduction and social-tariff obligations), credit unions and lenders (income maximisation reduces default), NHS/social-prescribing budgets, and employers (benefits-cliff guidance for low-wage staff). Philanthropic funding for the open rules and form libraries. Aligned incentive: customers pay for confirmed awards and reduced churn.

**MVP feasibility.** Web MVP by a small team is realistic: auth and role model (claimant, caseworker, organisation), household/case schema, PolicyEngine public API integration for screening (US and UK live today), a form-mapping layer for 3-5 high-value programmes (e.g., Pension Credit, Attendance Allowance, Council Tax Support; or SNAP + EITC in one US state), PDF generation, deadline scheduler and outcome capture. Real public data: PolicyEngine and OpenFisca APIs and parameter repos, IRS EITC participation tables, USDA participation reports, DWP take-up ODS tables, medicaid.gov unwinding data, official application forms. Hard parts: no government submission APIs in most places (submission is a pre-filled packet plus tracked manual filing or assisted online entry); keeping 300+ local schemes current; data protection for sensitive household data (GDPR/DPA, HIPAA-adjacent in US); liability for wrong advice means conservative wording and human review. No hardware, no regulatory approval to operate.

**Measurable outcomes.** Confirmed awards per 100 screened households and annualised value secured (verified by award letters); conversion funnel eligible → application submitted → decision → paid, with median days at each step; recertification completion rate and procedural-termination rate for tracked cases vs. local baseline (e.g., Medicaid 71% procedural share); cost per £/$1 of benefits secured for the paying organisation; reduction in arrears for housing/utility partners; appeal success when mandatory reconsideration is used. Not counted: calculations run or users registered.

**Sources consulted:**

## 30. Predatory and unlicensed digital loan apps that harvest contacts, over-charge and extort borrowers in Kenya, Nigeria, Indonesia, Pakistan, the Philippines and India

*Domain:* financial · *Researcher confidence:* 0.5

**Who experiences it.** Low-income smartphone users borrowing small sums (Kenya: ~$40 typical digital loan; India: Rs 3,000-20,000) for emergencies, gig workers, small traders and students in Kenya, Nigeria, Indonesia, Pakistan, the Philippines and India — and the friends, relatives and employers whose numbers are scraped from borrowers' phones and then harassed, including people who never borrowed. Kenya's digital-credit borrowers grew from ~200,000 (2016) to over 2 million (FinAccess 2019); Kenya's central bank licensed 252 digital credit providers by July 2026; Indonesia's regulator reports thousands of illegal online lenders ('pinjol') blocked.

**Scale and severity.** Caveat: regulator sites (centralbank.go.ke, fccpc.gov.ng, ojk.go.id, rbi.org.in) were unreachable from this session; evidence comes from a peer-reviewed study's public artefacts and secondary copies, so scale is less firmly quantified than for the other two problems and confidence is lowered. Peer-reviewed measurement: Akanji, Egele and Stringhini, 'The Cost of Convenience' (ACM ASIA CCS 2026, DOI 10.1145/3779208.3785263) analysed 434 loan apps from Indonesia, Kenya, Nigeria, Pakistan and the Philippines against national regulations and Google's Financial Services policy; the published result file flags 315 distinct app packages for requesting permissions the rules prohibit (contacts, fine location, external storage), including 145 Nigeria-approved, 44 Philippines-approved, 26 Indonesia-approved and 25 Kenya-approved apps — i.e. over-collection is common even among regulator-approved lenders, and static/dynamic analysis traced data exfiltration. Pricing: Kenyan digital lenders charged ~7.5% per 30 days (~90% APR) while bank rates were capped at 14%; a county-level study estimates ~2.3m additional digital borrowers and ~$2.8bn of mobile lending during the 2017-19 rate cap as borrowers were pushed outside the regulated perimeter. Harm: Kenya's data-protection regulator issued determinations against lenders (e.g., Whitepath, Jan 2025: enforcement notice and KES 450,000 compensation to one complainant); Nigeria's FCCPC regulates digital money lenders under an interim framework after mass defamatory 'debt-shaming' messages to contacts; India's I4C/advisory corpora describe recovery agents sending morphed images of female relatives and police have linked loan-app harassment to suicides (widely reported 2020-2024; I could not reach official counts). Indonesia's Satgas PASTI reports blocking roughly 7,500 illegal online-loan entities and cumulative public losses of Rp 139.67 trillion across all illegal financial activity since 2017 (Bisnis.com, Jan 2025; illegal-loan share not verified). Numbers of people harmed per year are not reliably published anywhere; severity (harassment of entire contact lists, extortion, suicides, data theft) is well documented.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Licensing registries exist (CBK's Directory of Digital Credit Providers, FCCPC's approved digital money lenders list, OJK's licensed fintech lending list, SEC Philippines' recorded online lending platforms, RBI's regulated-entity lists) but are PDFs updated irregularly, use legal entity names that differ from app and brand names, and borrowers never consult them at the moment of borrowing. (2) Google Play's Financial Services policy requires licence proof, but illegal apps are distributed as sideloaded APKs via WhatsApp/Telegram/SMS and third-party stores, and the ASIA CCS study shows even store-approved, regulator-approved apps violate permission rules. (3) Interest-rate caps on banks without covering digital lenders pushed borrowers to 90% APR products (Kenya 2016-19). (4) Complaint channels are split across the financial regulator, the data-protection authority, credit bureaus, police and the app store, with no single evidence pack; third parties whose contacts were scraped have no obvious remedy. (5) Enforcement is reactive and slow relative to app churn (operators rebrand under new package names within weeks).

**What people do today.** Borrowers ask in WhatsApp groups or Reddit whether an app is 'RBI approved' (third-party 'approved' lists are often unofficial, as the ASIA CCS authors note), screenshot threats and post them on social media to shame lenders, change SIMs, warn contacts in advance, borrow from a second app to repay the first, or pay inflated balances to stop the harassment. Journalists and NGOs compile ad-hoc lists; some victims file police reports or data-protection complaints (the Kenya ODPC cases show this can win compensation but is rare).

**Proposed intervention.** An open 'loan-app licence and conduct verifier' with an evidence-to-complaint router: (1) ingest each country's official registry (CBK, FCCPC, OJK, SEC-PH, SECP, RBI) on a schedule into a versioned, normalised open dataset with alias matching between legal entity, brand, developer account and Android package name; (2) a lookup (web + share-to-check link) that returns 'current official registry match found' / 'no sufficiently confident match' (never 'illegal', to avoid defamation), the app's declared Play Store permissions scored against that country's prohibited-permission list (the ASIA CCS policy-to-permission mapping is published), and any public enforcement actions or regulator advisories; (3) a total-cost-of-credit calculator that converts '2.5% per week plus processing fee' into APR and the rupee/shilling cost of the loan; (4) a harassment evidence kit — timestamped call/SMS logs and screenshots hashed on upload — that auto-generates complaints to the correct regulator and data-protection authority (rights requests for erasure of scraped contacts, CRB disputes), plus a template notice for third parties whose contacts were scraped; (5) a public, aggregated dashboard of reports per app that app stores, telcos and regulators can subscribe to. Novelty is cross-country registry normalisation + app-to-registry matching + permission auditing + routed complaints in one place; it is useful without AI (registries, rules, forms). Optional AI: OCR of threat screenshots into structured incidents.

**Works across countries?.** Directly applicable to the five countries in the ASIA CCS study plus India (RBI Digital Lending Guidelines 2022) and extensible to Mexico, Colombia, Brazil, Vietnam and Thailand, which have the same sideloaded-app harassment pattern and published lender registries. Each country needs a registry parser, a prohibited-permission mapping and a complaint-routing pack; the app matching, cost calculator and evidence kit are shared.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for borrowers and for people harassed as contacts. Paid: data/API licences for app stores, telcos, credit bureaus and banks that want a current licence-status and conduct feed; B2G monitoring dashboards for regulators and data-protection authorities (several already outsource app monitoring); grants from financial-inclusion and digital-rights funders (consumer-protection work in these markets is actively funded). Explicitly avoid lead generation or paid placement for lenders — a licensed-lender list may be shown only as the regulator's list with disclosed costs, never ranked for money.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web MVP but with more upkeep risk than the other two problems: backend with auth, registry ingestion jobs (PDF parsing of CBK/FCCPC/OJK/SEC lists — the Kenya data-rights and LoanWatch projects show these parsers are tractable), an alias-matching table, Play Store listing/permission scraping or use of the AndroZoo research corpus for manifests, APR calculator, evidence upload with hashing, complaint-template generator, and a public API. Real public data: the regulator directories above, Google Play listings, the ASIA CCS prohibited-permission mappings and result CSVs, Kenya ODPC determinations, OJK Satgas PASTI blocklists. Hard parts: registries change format without notice and lack stable identifiers; name matching produces false negatives that must be worded carefully; legal exposure in some jurisdictions when lenders dispute a 'no match' result; the tool cannot block sideloading or stop harassment by itself, only speed up redress and shift demand to licensed lenders. No custom hardware; no regulatory approval needed to publish public-registry data, but some regulators may object to scraping.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of lookups where the user reports choosing a licensed lender (ex-ante checks before borrowing); complaints filed through the tool that receive a regulator acknowledgement and resolution (harassment ceased within 14 days, scraped contacts deleted, compensation awarded); app-store delistings or regulator advisories traceable to submitted evidence; registry freshness (days between official update and ingestion); false-match rate measured against regulator confirmations; reduction in effective APR paid by returning users who switch lenders. Not counted: page views or number of apps indexed.
