## 31. The first hours of a search for a missing person with dementia are uncoordinated and depend on luck

*Domain:* safety-emergency (missing persons / search and rescue) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.5

**Who experiences it.** People living with dementia who leave home or a care setting on foot and become disoriented, and the families, home-care workers, care homes and local police who have to find them. WHO estimates more than 55 million people live with dementia worldwide (2021), roughly 60% of them in low- and middle-income countries, with about 10 million new cases a year; the Alzheimer's Association puts the US figure at about 7 million (2024) and Japan's government at roughly 4-5 million. Highest incidence is in ageing societies (Japan, Korea, Italy, Germany, UK, US) but the wandering behaviour itself is universal.

**Scale and severity.** NOTE: primary hosts (alz.org, npa.go.jp, le.fbi.gov, who.int) were network-blocked in this session; figures below are from those reports as known to the researcher and were NOT re-fetched here. Alzheimer's Association: about 6 in 10 people living with dementia will wander at least once, many repeatedly; if a wanderer is not found within 24 hours, up to half suffer serious injury or death (figure traces to Koester & Stooksbury's lost-person studies). Japan's National Police Agency reported a record ~19,000 dementia-related missing-person reports in 2023 (up from ~18,700 in 2022; the series has roughly doubled since 2012), with on the order of 500 of them found dead each year. The FBI's NCIC file logs roughly 550,000-600,000 missing-person entries a year in the US (2022-2023), of which 'endangered/disability' categories are a substantial minority. Lost-person-behaviour research (Koester, ISRID, >200,000 incidents from several countries) shows dementia subjects are typically found close to the point last seen (roughly half within about 1 km, ~95% within ~3 km), often along roads or stuck in vegetation or water: the search problem is tractable, which is precisely why the current ad hoc approach wastes lives.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) US Silver Alert programmes are a state-by-state patchwork, often require a vehicle or a delay before activation, and broadcast to everyone instead of tasking anyone. (2) Hardware trackers (Project Lifesaver, GPS pendants, AirTag-style tags) require the person to wear a device and a family able to pay; adherence is poor exactly in the moderate-stage population that wanders. (3) MedicAlert + Safe Return helps once a stranger has already found the person; it does not help find them. (4) Japan's municipal 'SOS networks' are fax/e-mail broadcasts bounded by municipal lines, require pre-registration that only a small share of families complete, and have no sector assignment or feedback loop. (5) The UK's Herbert Protocol is a paper form kept at home. (6) Professional SAR tooling (CalTopo/SARTopo, ISRID-based planning) is excellent but is deployed hours later by trained teams for wilderness incidents, not in the first 60-180 minutes of an urban dementia walk-away. The gap is the window between 'noticed missing' and 'professional response', when a structured community search has the highest payoff.

**What people do today.** Families drive the neighbourhood, call former addresses and the person's old workplace, and post photos to Facebook, Nextdoor, LINE or WhatsApp groups; care homes pull staff off shifts to walk the streets; police take a report and may wait before escalating; some neighbourhoods run volunteer patrols coordinated by group chat with no map, no sector log and no record of what has already been checked.

**Proposed intervention.** A web platform that converts a dementia walk-away into a planned, sectored community search within minutes, without hardware. Pre-registration: families or care homes store a verified profile (recent photo, gait, clothing habits, former homes, favourite routes, medical needs, consented contacts). Activation: a verified family member, care home or police user opens an incident; the system builds a probability map by applying published ISRID lost-person-behaviour distance and 'find location' statistics for the dementia profile onto the OpenStreetMap road, transit, footpath and water network around the point last seen (pathway-following and 'stuck in vegetation/water' behaviours are modelled, not just radii). It then cuts the area into walkable sectors, pushes sector assignments by SMS/WhatsApp/web link to opted-in volunteers, shops, transit staff and taxi fleets within the ring, records sectors as cleared with timestamps, re-prioritises as the clock runs, and hands the full log to police or SAR when they take over. Novelty: it is tasking, not broadcasting; it encodes decades of search-theory statistics into a tool usable by a non-expert in the first hour; it is hardware-free and works for the majority who will never wear a tracker. It is useful with zero machine learning (the statistics are tabulated), though image matching from CCTV/doorbell uploads could be added later.

**Works across countries?.** High. OpenStreetMap coverage is global, lost-person-behaviour statistics are pooled across countries, and the same workflow fits Japan's SOS networks, UK Herbert Protocol forces, US Silver Alert states and ageing middle-income countries (China, Thailand, Brazil). Variation is in who may activate an incident (data-protection law, police practice) and in messaging channels (SMS vs LINE vs WhatsApp), both of which are configuration, not architecture.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for families and volunteers (alerts are never paywalled). Paid institutional tiers for care homes and home-care agencies (per-bed/per-client licence for pre-registration, drills and incident logs), municipalities and long-term-care insurers (Japan's municipal LTCI budgets already fund SOS networks and would pay to modernise them), and police forces (incident management seat licences). Secondary revenue from anonymised search-outcome data to improve public search guidance. Social value is preserved because the money comes from institutions whose liability and labour cost the tool reduces, not from the person at risk.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable by a small team as a web app: auth with role-based verification (family, care home, police, volunteer), Postgres/PostGIS, OSM data via Overpass or a regional extract, Leaflet/MapLibre maps, Twilio-class SMS/WhatsApp, and a tabulated lost-person-behaviour parameter set (published in Koester's 'Lost Person Behavior' and ISRID papers; the summary distance tables are citable parameters, not a proprietary dataset). No regulatory approval is needed before use. Hard parts: (a) preventing misuse (someone using it to locate a person who left on purpose, e.g. a domestic-abuse victim): require verification via a police report number or an accredited care organisation before a search goes live; (b) volunteer density: cold-start in a new area; seed with care homes, transit operators and existing neighbourhood-watch groups; (c) police buy-in for handover, which needs one pilot force; (d) data-protection compliance for profiles of people who cannot consent (use carer/guardian consent and strict retention).

**Measurable outcomes.** Median minutes from activation to found; share of incidents resolved within 3 hours and within 24 hours; injuries and deaths per 1,000 reported wanderers versus the jurisdiction's baseline (Japan's NPA publishes this annually, giving a ready control series); number of distinct sectors cleared per volunteer-hour; police officer-hours per incident; repeat-wanderer time-to-found on second and later incidents; share of care-home residents with a complete profile.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/snapshots/who/2024-07-30/ghe.feather.dvc
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/etl/steps/data/garden/who/2024-07-30/ghe.meta.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/dag/health.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/owid-datasets/master/README.md

## 32. Survivors fleeing intimate-partner violence cannot find an available shelter bed in the hours when leaving is possible

*Domain:* safety-emergency (gender-based violence / emergency coordination) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.5

**Who experiences it.** Women (and their children) leaving a violent partner, plus the hotline workers, advocates, police officers and hospital social workers who try to place them. Most acute in countries that have shelter systems but chronic under-capacity (US, UK, EU member states under the Istanbul Convention, Canada, Australia); in most low- and middle-income countries the problem is upstream (there are almost no shelters at all), so the coordination fix applies mainly to high- and upper-middle-income systems and to NGO safe-house networks elsewhere.

**Scale and severity.** NOTE: nnedv.org, womensaid.org.uk, wave-network.org, unwomen.org and unodc.org were network-blocked in this session; figures are from those reports as known to the researcher and were NOT re-fetched. The researcher did confirm via OWID provenance that UNODC's intentional-homicide portal (published 2026-04-08) carries victim counts by relationship to perpetrator, the basis of femicide estimates. WHO (2021): about 1 in 3 women (roughly 736 million) experience physical or sexual intimate-partner violence or non-partner sexual violence in their lifetime. UNODC/UN Women (Nov 2024): about 51,100 women and girls were killed by an intimate partner or family member in 2023, roughly 140 a day. NNEDV's 18th 'Domestic Violence Counts' one-day census (September 2023, ~1,600 US programmes): about 77,000 victims served in one day and about 13,300 requests for help unmet that same day, roughly 60% of them for emergency shelter or housing, with programmes citing lack of beds and staff as the reasons. Women's Aid (England) reports that around two-thirds of refuge referrals were declined in recent years, chiefly for lack of space, and a persistent shortfall of well over a thousand bed spaces against the Council of Europe benchmark of one family place per 10,000 inhabitants; WAVE's European country reports show most member states far below that benchmark. The leaving moment is the most lethal: separation is the highest-risk period for intimate-partner homicide.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Capacity is genuinely short, so any software is partial, but the current system also wastes the capacity that exists: advocates learn about vacancies by phoning shelters one at a time, so beds in the next county or city sit empty while a survivor is told there is nothing. (2) Shelter directories (US 211, DomesticShelters.org, national hotlines' lists) are static and say nothing about tonight's availability, family size, accessibility, pets, language or whether boys over 12 are accepted. (3) The UK's Routes to Support (Women's Aid) proves a live-vacancy register works, but it is manually updated, subscription-only, UK-only and not request-based. (4) Earlier US attempts at multi-shelter 'request fan-out' (e.g. the Safe Shelter Collaborative) stalled on shelter adoption and funding, not on concept. (5) Federal homelessness data systems (HMIS) exclude DV shelters by law (VAWA confidentiality), so no bed-board exists for this population, and shelters are rightly reluctant to expose location or occupancy. (6) Hotel-voucher and rapid-rehousing programmes are funded in silos, so advocates cannot see them as placement options either.

**What people do today.** Hotline and shelter advocates call down a phone list, sometimes dozens of shelters, repeating the survivor's story each time; police drive survivors to another jurisdiction; programmes buy motel nights out of discretionary funds; survivors sleep in cars, with friends, in hospital emergency departments, or go back to the abuser; shelters keep informal text-message groups with neighbouring shelters about vacancies.

**Proposed intervention.** A confidential bed-availability and placement exchange for domestic-violence programmes, modelled on hospital bed boards but built for VAWA-style confidentiality. Shelters publish typed capacity (units by family size, accessible units, pet-friendly, languages, acceptance rules, hotel-voucher and rapid-rehousing slots) with a time-decay so stale entries fade; no addresses are ever published. An advocate submits one structured, minimised placement request (family composition, needs, radius, risk flags, no narrative) that fans out simultaneously to all matching programmes; the first to accept holds the unit, and the system coordinates transport and a time-boxed handover. Every unmet request is logged automatically, producing a continuous, programme-attributed unmet-need series that state coalitions and funders can use (today that evidence exists only as NNEDV's once-a-year count). Novelty: fan-out requests with first-accept locking (not a directory), typed units rather than 'beds', built-in confidentiality so shelters will actually participate, and the automatic unmet-demand ledger that turns daily failure into funding evidence. It works without any machine learning.

**Works across countries?.** Strong in countries with shelter networks and statutory confidentiality rules: US (state coalitions), UK (would complement Routes to Support), Canada, Australia, the 46 Council of Europe states bound by the Istanbul Convention's shelter standard, and NGO safe-house networks in India, South Africa, Brazil and Mexico. Weak where shelters barely exist; there the same request protocol can route to NGO safe houses, hotel partners and police-run 'one-stop' centres, but it cannot create capacity.

**Sustainable business model.** Survivors and frontline advocates never pay. Revenue from (a) state and provincial coalitions and ministries that already fund hotline and referral infrastructure (per-programme or statewide licences, as local authorities pay for Routes to Support in the UK), (b) health systems and police forces that currently absorb unplaced survivors in emergency departments and custody suites, (c) philanthropic and government funders who pay for the verified unmet-need data to allocate shelter funding. No advertising, no data sale: the aggregated series is the product for funders, and individual records are purged after placement.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app: organisation-scoped auth and roles, Postgres with row-level security, geospatial matching, encrypted request payloads, SMS/e-mail fan-out, audit logs. Real data to seed: HUD's open Housing Inventory Count (which lists DV shelter programmes and bed counts by Continuum of Care), state coalition programme directories, the National Domestic Violence Hotline's public listings, and in England the publicly described structure of Routes to Support; availability itself must come from shelters, which is the hard part. Honest difficulties: chicken-and-egg adoption (solve by piloting with one state coalition that mandates updates for its member programmes), VAWA/GDPR-grade confidentiality review, shelter fear of being 'rated', and the fact that software cannot add beds, so the headline metric must be time-to-placement and wasted-vacancy rate, not total capacity. No regulatory approval is required, but a legal review of confidentiality obligations is essential before a live pilot.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median time from advocate request to confirmed bed; share of requests placed the same day; number of phone calls per placement (baseline measured during pilot); vacant-bed-nights per programme per month (wasted capacity recovered); distance survivors travel to placement; share of placements that are 'first-accept' within 30 minutes; survivor return-to-abuser within 30 days where follow-up exists; the daily unmet-request count compared with NNEDV's annual census for the same programmes.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/snapshots/unodc/2026-06-12/homicide.xlsx.dvc
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/etl/steps/data/garden/unodc/2026-06-12/homicide.meta.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/dag/main.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/dag/migrated.yml

## 33. People who cannot evacuate on their own die at disproportionate rates because no one is assigned to get them out when a warning is issued

*Domain:* safety-emergency (disaster evacuation / public safety information) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.45

**Who experiences it.** Older people, people with mobility, sensory or cognitive disabilities, dialysis and oxygen-dependent patients, and people in residential care, mainly in flood, hurricane/typhoon, wildfire and tsunami zones: US Gulf and Atlantic coasts and wildfire-prone West, Japan's coastal and river municipalities, Bangladesh and the Philippines, Southern Europe's wildfire belt, and increasingly any city subject to flash floods. Also the municipal civil-protection staff, community volunteers and care providers who are expected to help but have no shared plan.

**Scale and severity.** NOTE: wmo.int, bousai.go.jp and other primary hosts were network-blocked in this session; figures are from those reports as known to the researcher and were NOT re-fetched. EM-DAT (provenance confirmed via OWID, version 2026-10-12) records tens of thousands of natural-disaster deaths in a typical year and far more in bad years (about 86,000 in 2023, dominated by the Türkiye-Syria earthquake). The excess burden on people who need help to evacuate is consistent across events: after Japan's 2011 earthquake and tsunami the death rate among registered people with disabilities in affected municipalities was roughly twice that of the general population; nearly half of Hurricane Katrina's (2005) roughly 1,000+ Louisiana deaths were people aged 75 or older; about two-thirds of Hurricane Ian's (2022) roughly 150 Florida deaths were people aged 60 or older; most of the 102 people killed in the 2023 Lahaina wildfire were older adults, and the fire was fought without an effective evacuation order reaching them. Japan amended its Disaster Countermeasures Basic Act in 2021 to make 'individual evacuation plans' for people needing support a municipal duty, yet Cabinet Office surveys in 2023 showed only around one in ten municipalities had completed plans for everyone on their priority lists. WMO/UNDRR report that about one-third of the world's population, mostly in least-developed countries and small island states, is still not covered by multi-hazard early-warning systems, and that countries with weak coverage have several times higher disaster mortality; the 'Early Warnings for All' initiative (2022) explicitly names last-mile dissemination and acting on warnings as the weakest links.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Special-needs registries (common in US counties, mandated in Japan) are static lists: they record who needs help but assign no helper, no route and no vehicle, go stale within a year, and FEMA itself warns they create a false expectation of rescue. (2) Alerts (Wireless Emergency Alerts, Japan's J-Alert, sirens, CAP feeds) are broadcast, not personal: a flood warning does not tell a wheelchair user which shelter has an accessible entrance or who is coming for them. (3) Shelter directories rarely encode accessibility, power for medical devices or pet rules. (4) Commercial evacuation-modelling tools (traffic and fire spread) serve emergency managers, not the person on the second floor. (5) Volunteer buddy schemes exist as paper agreements in some neighbourhoods and collapse when the volunteer is away or the phone tree breaks. The missing piece is a maintained, individual plan linked to a named helper and automatically activated by the official warning for that exact location.

**What people do today.** Families or neighbours improvise pick-ups; care homes rely on staff who may not be on shift; municipalities hand out paper plan templates and hold occasional drills; residents sign up for county registries that are never called; in Japan welfare commissioners (minsei-iin) keep hand-written lists; in Bangladesh and the Philippines community volunteers go door to door with megaphones, which works where volunteers exist and fails where they do not.

**Proposed intervention.** A community evacuation-assistance platform that turns a registry into an activated plan. Residents (or carers, with consent) register needs and location; the system pulls official hazard layers (FEMA National Flood Hazard Layer, USGS/NOAA, Japan's open hazard-map and national land data, Copernicus/GDACS, OSM) to generate an individual plan: nearest shelter that meets the person's accessibility and medical-power needs, route, required transport type and a named primary and backup helper drawn from vetted volunteers, care providers or paid transport partners. It subscribes to official Common Alerting Protocol feeds (NWS api.weather.gov, MeteoAlarm, IFRC Alert Hub, J-Alert relays where available) and, when a warning polygon intersects a registered address, tasks the helpers automatically, tracks acknowledgement and 'person evacuated' confirmation, and escalates unanswered tasks to the municipality with a live list of who is still unaccounted for. Between events it runs drills and nags stale records. Novelty: the link between geofenced official warnings and a pre-assigned human for each vulnerable person, with shelter accessibility encoded, giving emergency managers a real-time 'still at home' list instead of a dead registry. Entirely useful without AI.

**Works across countries?.** High. CAP is the global alerting standard, OSM is global, and the legal duty already exists in Japan (individual evacuation plans), the US (ADA obligations in emergency planning) and EU civil-protection guidance; the same model fits typhoon-prone Philippines and Vietnam, cyclone-prone Bangladesh (with volunteer networks as the helper pool) and wildfire-prone Australia, Greece and Portugal. Differences are in hazard-data availability and helper pools, not in workflow.

**Sustainable business model.** Municipal and prefectural civil-protection licences (Japan subsidises individual evacuation-plan creation; US counties fund registries and could redirect that budget), care-home and home-care operators (whose regulators require evacuation plans), health insurers and dialysis providers (patients who miss sessions after evacuation failures are expensive), and utilities with medical-baseline customer lists. Residents and volunteers never pay. No advertising; aggregated readiness metrics are reported to the funding municipality, which is the customer.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app: auth and consent management, Postgres/PostGIS, map UI, CAP feed polling (api.weather.gov is open and documented; MeteoAlarm and IFRC Alert Hub publish CAP; GDACS has an open API), FEMA NFHL and OSM for hazard and shelter layers, SMS/WhatsApp tasking and acknowledgement. No regulatory approval is needed before a pilot, but a municipality or care operator is needed as sponsor to recruit residents and helpers. Hard parts: liability if a helper fails to show (mitigate with backup assignment and municipal escalation, and clear terms), keeping records current (drills and periodic confirmation), helper reliability during a real event (helpers may be evacuating too, so paid transport partners matter), and in LMICs sparse hazard data and intermittent connectivity (SMS-first design). Physical logistics (vehicles) are a partner dependency, honestly a constraint, but the coordination layer is software.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of registered residents with an assigned, drilled helper; helper acknowledgement rate and median minutes from warning to acknowledgement in drills and real events; share of registered residents confirmed evacuated before hazard impact; number 'unaccounted for' at impact versus the registry baseline; post-event injuries and deaths among registered residents versus comparable unregistered populations; registry staleness (share of records confirmed in the last 12 months); for Japan, municipal completion rate of individual plans before and after adoption.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/snapshots/emdat/2026-10-21/natural_disasters.xlsx.dvc
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/etl/steps/data/garden/emdat/2026-10-21/natural_disasters.meta.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/etl/master/dag/natural_disasters.yml
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rfordatascience/tidytuesday/master/data/2025/readme.md

## 34. People cannot verify whether a payee, caller, job offer or investment is a scam at the moment money (or a passport) is about to move

*Domain:* information-integrity / scam detection · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Everyone who receives unsolicited contact, but losses concentrate in: adults over 60 (FBI IC3 2024: ~147,000 complaints and ~$4.9B reported losses from the 60+ group, the highest of any age bracket); 18-29-year-olds (FTC data: more likely than older adults to report losing money, especially to job and online-shopping scams); migrant and low-income workers in the Philippines, Indonesia, India, Kenya, Ethiopia, Uganda, Nepal, and Latin America who answer fake overseas job ads and end up in Southeast Asian scam compounds; diaspora communities targeted in their own language (Chinese-, Spanish-, Vietnamese-speaking communities in the US/Canada/Australia); and small businesses hit by invoice/BEC fraud. Geographies with the highest reported per-capita losses include the US, UK, Australia, Singapore, Hong Kong; underreported but large in India, Brazil, Nigeria and across Africa (mobile-money fraud).

**Scale and severity.** US: FTC Consumer Sentinel release (Mar 2025) reported $12.5B in fraud losses for 2024, up ~25% from 2023, from ~2.6M fraud reports; the share of reporters who lost money rose from 27% to 38%; investment scams were the costliest category (~$5.7B), imposter scams second (~$2.95B); bank transfer/payment (~$2B) and cryptocurrency (~$1.4B) were the largest loss channels. FBI IC3 2024 Annual Report (Apr 2025): 859,532 complaints, $16.6B losses (+33% YoY), investment fraud ~$6.6B, BEC ~$2.8B, tech-support ~$1.5B, with cryptocurrency involved in ~$9.3B. Underreporting is severe: the FTC's own 2023 estimate adjusting for unreported fraud was roughly $158B, and FTC-affiliated research (Anderson, 2021) found under 5% of mass-market fraud victims report to any government body. Global: GASA Global State of Scams 2024 extrapolated ~$1.03 trillion lost over 12 months from a 58,000-person survey (wide error bars); GASA's 2025 edition, using a revised method, estimated ~$442B. UK Finance Annual Fraud Report 2025: £1.17B lost in 2024, including ~£450M to authorised push-payment (APP) fraud. Australia National Anti-Scam Centre: A$2.03B reported losses in 2024. Singapore Police: S$1.1B in 2024. Supply side: OHCHR (Aug 2023) estimated at least 120,000 people in Myanmar and ~100,000 in Cambodia were coerced into online scam operations, most recruited via fraudulent job advertisements; UNODC (2025) estimated East/Southeast Asian scam centres generate roughly $37-44B per year. Non-financial harm: documented suicides and severe psychological injury among pig-butchering victims. NOTE: these are recalled from the named reports; none could be re-fetched in this session (network policy), so treat exact values as approximately +/-10%.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Signals are siloed by industry: banks see payee accounts and mule patterns, telcos see numbers, platforms see profiles and ads, registrars see domains, securities regulators publish warning lists - none of these are joined at the moment a consumer decides. (2) Blocklists are reactive and the attackers' infrastructure is disposable (new domain per campaign, SIM boxes, spoofed caller ID), so Google Safe Browsing, Truecaller/Hiya and carrier spam labels only cover URLs and phone numbers, not bank payees, crypto wallets, company names, job postings, or social-media profiles. (3) Warnings are generic ('beware of scams') and arrive at the wrong time; Confirmation-of-Payee style checks exist only in a few jurisdictions (UK, Netherlands; EU instant-payment regulation phases it in from late 2025). (4) Reporting is fragmented (FTC, IC3, state AGs, Action Fraud, Scamwatch, bank, platform) with no feedback loop, so most victims report nowhere and the signal is lost. (5) Regulator warning lists (FCA, ASIC, MAS, SEC/FINRA, CSA, IOSCO I-SCAN) are in incompatible formats and not searchable from a consumer's phone at the point of decision. (6) Platform enforcement is weak: UK and Australian data attribute a large share of APP fraud to contacts originating on Meta platforms and Telegram. (7) Mandatory reimbursement (UK, Oct 2024) shifts the loss to banks but does not prevent the transaction.

**What people do today.** Googling the phone number, URL or company name plus 'scam'; posting on Reddit r/Scams or country forums (Whirlpool, MoneySavingExpert); asking a younger relative; phoning the bank; Truecaller/Hiya; ScamAdviser and WHOIS lookups; reverse-image-searching romance-scam profile photos; refusing all unknown calls (with collateral cost of missing legitimate medical/government calls); bank 'cooling-off' interventions at branches; community Facebook groups warning one another; in source countries, NGOs and embassies maintaining informal lists of fraudulent recruiters. All are slow, manual, and inconsistent, and none feeds back into a shared signal.

**Proposed intervention.** A 'check before you pay / apply / invest' verification and report-routing service. One query - a domain, phone, email, crypto address, company name, payee name, or pasted job/investment offer text - returns an explainable, evidence-linked risk report assembled by joining open signals that today sit in separate places: Google Safe Browsing, PhishTank/OpenPhish/URLhaus, RDAP domain registration age and registrar, certificate-transparency first-seen dates (crt.sh), OpenCorporates/Companies House/SEC EDGAR/FINRA BrokerCheck for entity existence and licensing, every national regulator's warning list (FCA, ASIC, MAS, SEC, CSA, SEBI, CVM, IOSCO I-SCAN) normalised into one open schema, crypto abuse reports (Chainabuse, Etherscan labels), FTC Sentinel and Scamwatch public aggregates, and the service's own structured report graph. What is novel: (a) a single, versioned, openly published dataset of regulator warnings worldwide - this does not exist in usable form; (b) 'report once, route everywhere': a structured report is filed to the correct agencies (FTC, IC3, Action Fraud, Scamwatch, platform abuse desks, registrar) through their intake forms/APIs and the reporter gets status updates; (c) entity-level correlation (the same wallet, payee name, phone or recruiter reused across reports) rather than URL-only matching; (d) an embeddable widget/API so credit unions, payment apps, remittance firms, marketplaces and job boards can show the check at the moment of payment or application. It is useful with no AI at all - it is essentially a join across public lists plus domain metadata heuristics; an LLM is optional for parsing pasted offers or screenshots.

**Works across countries?.** Scam typologies are global and the join works anywhere with public corporate registries and regulator warning lists (all OECD countries, India, Singapore, Philippines, Nigeria, Brazil, South Africa). Phone-number and bank-payee reputation requires per-country partnerships and is the least portable layer. Language localisation is required; job-scam prevention has highest value in labour-sending countries (Philippines, Indonesia, India, Kenya, Ethiopia, Nepal).

**Sustainable business model.** Free, rate-limited lookups for individuals. Revenue from B2B API/widget licences to credit unions, community banks, fintechs, remittance and payment apps, marketplaces and job boards - they already buy fraud tooling, and in the UK mandatory APP reimbursement created a direct financial incentive to stop payments before they leave. Government and consumer-protection agency contracts for the structured report-routing pipeline. Grants from anti-fraud coalitions and foundations. Guardrails: never sell query data, publish aggregate scam-signal data openly, provide an appeals process for businesses flagged in error.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable by a small team as a web app with backend, Postgres and auth. Real, free or low-cost data available now: Google Safe Browsing v4 API (free key), PhishTank API (free), abuse.ch URLhaus API (free), OpenPhish community feed, RDAP (free, standardised), crt.sh (free), OpenCorporates API (free for non-profit use, rate-limited), SEC EDGAR full-text search (free), FCA Register API (free), FINRA BrokerCheck/MAS Investor Alert List/IOSCO I-SCAN (scraping), Chainabuse (API on request), Etherscan labels, FTC Consumer Sentinel Tableau/CSV exports and Scamwatch monthly data. Hard parts (stated honestly): no open phone-reputation dataset exists (Truecaller is closed); bank payee/mule data is closed to banks; scrapers of regulator lists are brittle; false positives can defame legitimate small businesses, so evidence standards and appeals are mandatory; adversaries can file fake reports against competitors; agency intake forms change; and the real difficulty is distribution - reaching people in the seconds before they pay - which is why the embeddable B2B surface matters more than a standalone site.

**Measurable outcomes.** Loss averted, not lookups: share of high-risk verdicts after which the user did not proceed (two-week follow-up); A/B comparison of fraud loss per 1,000 members at partner credit unions with vs without the widget; precision/recall of verdicts against later-confirmed regulator or IC3 listings; median lead time between an entity's first report in the system and its appearance on an official warning list; number of routed reports acknowledged by agencies and resulting takedowns; false-positive appeals upheld (should be low and falling).

**Sources consulted:**

- BLOCKED by session network policy (403 on CONNECT), not read: https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/03/new-ftc-data-show-big-jump-reported-losses-fraud-125-billion-2024
- BLOCKED by session network policy (403 on CONNECT), not read: https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports/2024_IC3Report.pdf
- BLOCKED by session network policy (403 on CONNECT), not read: https://www.ohchr.org/en/press-releases/2023/08/hundreds-thousands-trafficked-work-online-scammers-se-asia-says-un-report

(FTC, 2021) 'To Whom Do Victims of Mass-Market Consumer Fraud Complain?'

## 35. Local news deserts: tens of millions of people live where no one independently reports on their local government, courts and schools

*Domain:* information-integrity / lack of trustworthy local information · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Residents of rural and small-metro counties, disproportionately poorer, older, less broadband-connected and less college-educated than the national average (Medill finds news-desert counties have poverty rates well above the US average). In the US this is ~55 million people in counties with no or only one local news source (Medill 2024). Also: residents of UK districts with no local outlet (~4.8M people per PINF's 2023 Local News Map), roughly half of Brazil's municipalities (Atlas da Noticia), many Canadian communities after 500+ outlet closures since 2008 (Local News Research Project), and regional Australia (PIJI tracking). Secondary sufferers: local officials who face no scrutiny and residents who instead get their 'local news' from partisan 'pink slime' sites, Facebook groups and Nextdoor.

**Scale and severity.** US: Medill State of Local News 2024 (Oct 2024) counted 206 counties with no local news source and 1,563 with only one; more than 3,200 newspapers have closed since 2005, ~127 in the prior 12 months (about 2.5 per week); roughly 55 million Americans live in counties with no or a single news source; newspaper newsroom employment has fallen from ~75,000 in 2005 to well under 30,000; the 2025 edition (late 2025) reported continued losses (I cannot verify its exact county count in this session). NewsGuard (2024) counted ~1,265 partisan-funded sites masquerading as local news - more than the ~1,200 remaining US daily newspapers. UK: Public Interest News Foundation Local News Map (2023): ~4.8M people (7% of the population) live in local news deserts. Brazil: Atlas da Noticia (2023/24): roughly half of municipalities (~2,700) are news deserts, home to ~26M people. Peer-reviewed harm evidence: counties that lose a newspaper see municipal borrowing costs rise (Gao, Lee & Murphy, Journal of Financial Economics 2020: +5 to 11 basis points), lower turnout and fewer candidates in local races (Rubado & Jennings 2020), more nationalised/straight-ticket voting (Darr, Hitt & Dunaway 2018), and weaker government transparency and higher costs (Hayes & Lawless 2018/2021). Figures are recalled from these named reports and could not be re-fetched in this session; the 2025 US counts in particular should be re-verified.

**Why existing solutions fail.** The advertising model that funded local reporting collapsed (US newspaper ad revenue fell from ~$49B in 2005 to under $10B) and most digital ad spend goes to Google and Meta; hedge-fund consolidation strips remaining newsrooms. Philanthropy (Press Forward's ~$500M pledge) is an order of magnitude smaller than the lost revenue and non-profit startups cluster in metros, not rural counties. Public funding is politically fraught, though New Jersey's Civic Information Consortium and state tax credits (California, Illinois, New York) show limited models. Nextdoor and Facebook groups carry rumour and complaint, not records. Civic-tech tools (Councilmatic, Open States, City Bureau's Documenters) cover dozens of cities out of ~90,000 US local governments. Generic AI summarisers lack provenance and no reporter on the ground; AI-generated pink-slime sites actively fill the vacuum with partisan content. Nobody has built the boring infrastructure layer: systematic ingestion of the public records every jurisdiction already publishes.

**What people do today.** Facebook/Nextdoor community groups; regional TV (DMA-level, rarely county-level); local radio; church, school and word of mouth; residents reading council PDFs and watching meeting video themselves; City Bureau's paid Documenters programme; Patch; independent Substack newsletters; libraries; legal notices in the last surviving paper; local officials' own social media accounts (unchecked).

**Proposed intervention.** A records-first 'civic wire service'. Automated ingestion of the public records local governments already publish - meeting agendas, minutes, video, budgets, zoning cases, procurement, legal notices, school-board packets, court dockets - from the handful of portal platforms most jurisdictions use (Legistar/Granicus API, CivicPlus/CivicClerk RSS and iCal, BoardDocs, Diligent Community, Municode; ModernGov in the UK). Records are normalised into a structured local-decisions database (who voted for what, amounts, dates, parcels) with provenance links to the exact page or video timestamp, turned into templated plain-language briefings per locality, and delivered through channels people already use (email, SMS, RSS, posts to the local Facebook group), and syndicated free to remaining local outlets, libraries and broadcasters. A 'documenter' layer lets residents annotate and flag meetings; cross-jurisdiction comparison ('your county raised water rates 12%, neighbours 3%') is possible only because the data is structured. What is novel: coverage of every jurisdiction with a portal rather than reporter-first coverage of chosen cities; provenance on every sentence, which pink-slime cannot offer; and a structured open dataset of local government decisions that does not currently exist anywhere. It works without AI (structured fields plus templated text); an LLM improves summarisation of scanned PDFs and transcripts but every claim must link to a record.

**Works across countries?.** Strong wherever open-meeting and transparency laws require online publication: UK (ModernGov API across ~300 councils), Canada (eSCRIBE), Australia (InfoCouncil), Brazil (Portal da Transparencia and municipal camara sites), much of the EU. Weak where governments do not publish records online. Language and portal connectors are the per-country work.

**Sustainable business model.** Subscriptions from libraries and local governments (transparency-as-a-service and online legal-notice publishing as states move notices off print, which is an existing paid obligation); syndication licences to newsrooms and broadcasters; local institutional sponsorship; foundation grants (Press Forward, Knight, Google News Initiative); and a paid API tier for structured zoning/procurement/permit data used by real-estate, construction and legal customers, with a public-interest firewall (the briefings and base data stay free and open; the paid tier is API volume and alerting). No partisan funding, no ad targeting, published funding sources.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, Postgres and auth. Real data: Legistar WebAPI (public JSON for hundreds of jurisdictions), CivicPlus/CivicClerk RSS and iCal feeds, BoardDocs (scrapable), Granicus and YouTube meeting video (auto-captions), the Census Bureau's list of ~90,000 local governments and TIGER geography, Open States API for state legislation, state public-notice portals. Hard parts: extreme portal heterogeneity and PDF-only towns with poor OCR; an accuracy and corrections workflow so errors do not destroy trust; reaching residents who never opt in (hence pushing into existing Facebook groups and surviving outlets); staying visibly non-partisan; and per-jurisdiction QA cost as coverage scales. No regulatory approval is required; public records are open.

**Measurable outcomes.** Counties receiving at least a weekly record-backed briefing; share of briefing sentences with a resolvable source link (target 100%); corrections per 1,000 items; public-comment sign-ups and meeting attendance in covered vs uncovered jurisdictions; records requests filed by residents; pickup/citation by surviving local outlets; resident civic-knowledge survey scores; longer-run and harder to attribute: contested seats and turnout in local elections, and municipal borrowing spreads.

**Sources consulted:**

- BLOCKED by session network policy (403 on CONNECT), not read: https://localnewsinitiative.northwestern.edu/projects/state-of-local-news/2024/
- Not fetchable this session; figures recalled from: Medill School, Northwestern University, 'The State of Local News 2024' (and 2025 edition); NewsGuard 2024 pink-slime site count; Public Interest News Foundation 'Local News Map' 2023 (UK); Atlas da Noticia 2023/2024 (Brazil); Local News Research Project (Canada); Gao, Lee & Murphy (2020) 'Financing dies in darkness? The impact of newspaper closures on public finance', Journal of Financial Economics; Darr, Hitt & Dunaway (2018) Journal of Communication; Rubado & Jennings (2020) Urban Affairs Review; Hayes & Lawless (2018, 2021).

## 36. Victims of non-consensual deepfake intimate imagery must find and remove the content themselves, site by site, with no tooling built for them

*Domain:* information-integrity / deepfakes, digital manipulation, provenance · *Researcher confidence:* 0.45

**Who experiences it.** Overwhelmingly women and girls: roughly 99% of sexually explicit deepfakes depict women (Security Hero 2023). Teenage girls in middle and high school are a fast-growing victim group as 'nudify' apps spread through schools (US, UK, South Korea, Spain, Australia); women in public-facing roles (journalists, politicians, streamers, teachers); content creators whose images are repurposed; and adult victims of real (non-synthetic) image-based abuse, who face the same removal burden. Geographies with documented surges: US, UK, South Korea, Australia, Spain, India, Brazil; the January 2026 mass generation of sexualised images via X's Grok affected users worldwide.

**Scale and severity.** Prevalence is poorly measured but the direction is clear. Security Hero 'State of Deepfakes' (2023): ~95,800 deepfake videos found online, up ~550% since 2019, 98% sexually explicit, 99% depicting women. Graphika (Dec 2023): 24 million visits to 34 'nudify' websites in a single month, with referral links on Reddit and X up more than 2,000% year-on-year. Minors: Thorn (Mar 2025, US survey of 13-20-year-olds) found about 1 in 8 personally know someone targeted by deepfake nudes and roughly 1 in 17 had been targeted themselves; Internet Matters (UK, 2024) estimated ~13% of UK teenagers had some experience with nude deepfakes. UK Revenge Porn Helpline reported a record ~22,000 cases in 2024 (real and synthetic intimate-image abuse combined) and has had over 300,000 images removed since 2015. South Korea's 2024 school deepfake crisis produced 800+ police cases in a year, many involving minors as both victims and perpetrators. Documented harms include job loss, school withdrawal, severe anxiety, and suicidality. Legal response is recent: US TAKE IT DOWN Act signed 19 May 2025 (criminalises publication; covered platforms must remove within 48 hours of a valid request, with a May 2026 compliance deadline and FTC enforcement); EU Directive on combating violence against women (2024) criminalises non-consensual deepfake sharing with transposition by 2027; UK criminalised creation in 2025; Australia's eSafety Commissioner has statutory removal powers. Global victim totals cannot be stated with confidence; figures are recalled from the named reports and not re-verified in this session.

**Why existing solutions fail.** StopNCII.org and NCMEC's Take It Down rely on perceptual hashes of images the victim already possesses, so they cannot catch newly generated variants, crops or re-renders, and only work on participating platforms (not most porn aggregators, nudify sites or Telegram). Each platform has a different reporting flow, often demanding ID, and search engines de-index only on request. DMCA takedowns do not apply because the victim holds no copyright in a deepfake. The new 48-hour removal duty applies only to covered platforms and only after the victim files a valid notice per platform, with no tracking of compliance. Paid takedown services (Rulta, BranditScan, Ceartas) cost roughly $50-300+ per month and are built for adult creators, not victims. Law enforcement is under-resourced and victims rarely have court-ready evidence. Content is re-uploaded repeatedly, so a one-time removal is not a solution.

**What people do today.** Reverse image search (Google Lens; PimEyes, which is paid and ethically fraught); manually filing reports site by site and using Google's NCII removal form; paying creator-oriented takedown services or lawyers; contacting the UK Revenge Porn Helpline, Cyber Civil Rights Initiative, or Alecto AI (a startup offering consented face search for victims); deleting social accounts; reporting to schools and police; in Australia, filing with the eSafety Commissioner. Most victims do a subset of this alone, repeatedly, under acute distress.

**Proposed intervention.** A free, victim-side takedown orchestration system that exploits the new statutory deadlines. (1) A case workspace with legally sound evidence capture: timestamped screenshots, private URL archiving, perceptual hashes (PDQ/pHash), and an exportable chain-of-custody package for police, schools and courts. (2) A maintained registry of every major platform's, host's, registrar's and search engine's intimate-image reporting pathway - form URL, required fields, and legal basis (TAKE IT DOWN Act 48h, UK Online Safety Act, EU DSA Article 16 notice, GDPR Article 17, Australian eSafety) - that generates and submits notices, tracks each deadline, and escalates automatically to the hosting provider (via RDAP abuse contacts), registrar, search-engine de-indexing, and an FTC/eSafety/Ofcom complaint when a deadline lapses. (3) Integrated hash submission to StopNCII and NCMEC Take It Down. (4) Optional, strictly consent-gated re-upload monitoring on known abuse sites, with biometrics deleted after matching. (5) Anonymised aggregate compliance data showing which platforms actually honour the 48-hour rule, published to regulators and the press. What is novel: no free, legally structured notice-and-escalation engine with deadline tracking and compliance scorecards exists for victims; it turns a new legal right into something a traumatised person can actually exercise. It is useful without any AI (registry, templates, tracking, hashing); matching and classification are optional add-ons.

**Works across countries?.** Statutory removal duties now exist in the US, UK, EU (from 2027 for criminal law; DSA notice duties already apply), Australia, South Korea and several Canadian provinces, so the notice engine has legal teeth in most high-income jurisdictions. The platform-pathway registry is global by construction; where no law applies, platform terms of service still support removal. Trauma-informed localisation and partnerships with national helplines are required per country.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for individual victims, funded by foundation grants and government victim-services funding (e.g., US VOCA-type funds, UK Home Office, EU Daphne-type programmes). Paid tiers for organisations managing many cases: schools and universities (K-12 incidents are now common), employers, victim-advocacy organisations, law firms. A platform-compliance API for which platforms pay to receive structured, verified notices and to evidence compliance with 48-hour duties to regulators. Hard rules: never monetise victim data, require identity verification and consent before any face matching so the tool cannot be turned into a stalking service, and route any content involving minors to NCMEC/IWF without storage.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, database and auth by a small team. Real components available now: headless-browser evidence capture into a private archive; open perceptual hashing (PDQ, pHash); NCMEC Take It Down hash submission (public, for minors) and StopNCII organisational partnership; RDAP for host/registrar abuse contacts; Google's and Bing's NCII removal forms; DSA Article 16 notice endpoints for EU-covered platforms; a manually curated registry of platform reporting forms (most have no API and change often - this is ongoing maintenance, not a one-time build). Hard parts, honestly: face-based discovery requires either an ethically problematic provider or building an index of abuse sites, which carries legal risk and mandatory CSAM-handling obligations (minors' content must never be stored and must be reported); verifying that the requester is the depicted person without re-traumatising them; adversarial sites that ignore notices; and trauma-informed design and support staffing. No regulatory approval is required, but CSAM-reporting compliance and data-protection review are non-negotiable from day one.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median time from notice to removal per platform; share of reported URLs removed within 48 hours and within 7 days; 30- and 90-day re-upload recurrence per case; number of evidence packages used in police reports or court proceedings; victim-reported distress before and after (brief validated screens) and self-reported hours spent on removal; platform compliance scorecards cited by regulators; explicitly not 'cases opened' or 'notices sent'.

**Sources consulted:**

- BLOCKED by session network policy (403 on CONNECT), not read: https://www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/senate-bill/146
- Not fetchable this session; figures recalled from: Security Hero 'State of Deepfakes 2023'; Graphika 'A Revealing Picture' (Dec 2023) on nudify sites; Thorn 'Deepfake Nudes and Young People' (Mar 2025); Internet Matters UK deepfake report (2024); UK Revenge Porn Helpline annual report 2024; Korean National Police Agency 2024 deepfake case statistics (as reported by Yonhap/Reuters); TAKE IT DOWN Act, S.146, 119th Congress (signed 19 May 2025); EU Directive (EU) 2024/1385 on combating violence against women; Australian eSafety Commissioner image-based abuse scheme.

## 37. Step-free journeys break on missing data: there is no connected, live picture of accessible transit paths, station elevators, sidewalks and crossings

*Domain:* accessibility-disability (mobility impairment, wheelchair use, blind/low-vision pedestrian navigation, inaccessible built environment) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Wheelchair and mobility-scooter users; people with walking or stamina impairments (older adults, arthritis, cardiopulmonary disease, amputation); blind and low-vision pedestrians who depend on curb ramps, tactile paving and marked crossings; and secondarily parents with strollers and delivery workers. Concentrated in cities with legacy rail systems (New York, London, Paris, Boston, Chicago, Berlin) and in car-dependent US suburbs with discontinuous sidewalks. Also the paratransit-dependent population that transit agencies must serve under ADA/equivalent laws. Population context (training knowledge, not re-verified in this session because who.int was blocked): WHO Disability and Health fact sheet (2023) estimates 1.3 billion people, 16% of the world, live with significant disability; WHO wheelchair guidance estimates roughly 1% of any population needs a wheelchair (~80 million people).

**Scale and severity.** RESEARCH NOTE (applies to all three problems): in this session the shared web-search budget was exhausted before any search ran, and the egress proxy blocked WHO, BLS, WebAIM, ADA.gov, data.ny.gov, OSM taginfo and all non-GitHub hosts. Primary sources actually read are GitHub-hosted (W3C, US GSA, CISA, University of Washington, MobilityData, Microsoft, Sozialhelden, DAISY, UK GDS). Figures marked 'not re-verified' are from training knowledge with named source and year. VERIFIED PRIMARY EVIDENCE: (1) GTFS specification (google/transit, read 2026-11-02): the stop-level `wheelchair_boarding` field is Optional and '0 or empty' means 'No accessibility information for the stop'; `pathways.txt`, the only way to model stairs, elevators and ramps inside a station, is Optional; `levels.txt` is required only when elevators are modelled. A feed can be fully valid while saying nothing about accessibility. (2) GTFS-Realtime reference: the only accessibility-related real-time primitive is a generic `ACCESSIBILITY_ISSUE` alert effect and a per-trip WheelchairAccessible override; there is no standard elevator/escalator status feed. (3) Project Sidewalk (University of Washington) has had to crowdsource 3.4M+ labels of curb ramps, obstacles, surface problems and missing sidewalks across 50+ cities in 10 countries because this data does not otherwise exist; its docs state AI detection of curb ramps reached human-level accuracy only in 2025 and that AI is used mainly to validate human labels (precision threshold 0.92). (4) AccessMap (UW) routing requires bespoke per-city OpenSidewalks data pipelines; the OpenSidewalks schema is still at v0.3 (released 2026-01-27), i.e. research infrastructure. (5) Microsoft discontinued its Soundscape blind-navigation product and open-sourced a 'not turnkey' subset. (6) Wheelmap/accessibility.cloud is place-level (entrances), not path-level. NOT RE-VERIFIED (training knowledge): NYC MTA: roughly 30% of 472 subway stations were ADA-accessible as of 2024, with a 2022 class-action settlement committing to 95% by 2055; London Underground: roughly one third of stations step-free; US National Transit Database figures commonly cited show demand-response (paratransit) trips costing agencies several times a fixed-route trip (on the order of $40-60+ per trip vs under $10). Severity: one out-of-service elevator strands a wheelchair user mid-journey; unreliability causes trip avoidance, missed medical appointments, isolation and employment exclusion, and shifts riders to paratransit that is far more expensive for agencies.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Google and Apple Maps offer wheelchair-accessible transit routing only in a subset of cities and inherit agency GTFS, where the accessibility fields are optional and often empty; they cannot model within-station paths unless the agency publishes pathways.txt, and live elevator outages (published, if at all, in agency-specific formats) rarely trigger re-routing. OpenStreetMap sidewalk, curb-ramp and crossing coverage is sparse and inconsistent, so pedestrian routers silently assume a sidewalk exists. Wheelmap answers 'can I enter this place' not 'can I get there'. The research tools with the right data model (AccessMap, Project Sidewalk, OpenSidewalks) are per-city, grant-funded and not integrated into anything riders use daily; Soundscape's discontinuation shows even big-tech consumer apps in this niche were not sustained. Critically, no product distinguishes 'no barrier' from 'no data', so users cannot calibrate trust.

**What people do today.** Checking agency elevator-status pages or social-media accounts before leaving; phoning stations; memorising known-good routes and never deviating; travelling with a companion; booking ADA paratransit one to two days ahead and accepting wide pickup windows; wheelchair-accessible taxis/ride-hail where available (supply is poor); peer WhatsApp/Facebook groups sharing outages; or simply not travelling.

**Proposed intervention.** An open 'step-free journey graph' and alerting service. (1) Ingest every GTFS feed catalogued in the Mobility Database, extract stops, pathways, levels and wheelchair flags, and compute a per-feed accessibility data-completeness score. (2) Normalise agency elevator/escalator outage feeds (MTA, TfL, BART, WMATA, etc.) into one schema and attach outages to graph edges. (3) Merge sidewalk, curb-ramp and crossing data from OpenSidewalks, Project Sidewalk's open GeoJSON API and OSM, each edge carrying a confidence score and source. (4) Route against a personal mobility profile (max slope, max curb height, needs elevator, can manage N stairs, needs tactile/signalised crossings) and return confidence per leg; let users 'watch' saved routes and get an alert when an elevator on that route fails. (5) Publish per-city scorecards: data coverage, outage frequency and duration per station, time to repair. What is novel: explicit uncertainty ('no data' vs 'accessible'), outage-aware re-routing with alerts, profile-based rather than binary routing, and agency accountability metrics derived from the same data. It works without AI (graph fusion plus routing); open computer-vision models such as RampNet are optional for filling sidewalk gaps.

**Works across countries?.** GTFS and OSM are global standards and the Mobility Database catalogs feeds worldwide; A11yJSON/accessibility.cloud gives interoperability with place-level data. Coverage degrades gracefully where feeds lack pathways or cities lack sidewalk data, and the scorecard makes that gap visible, which is itself useful for advocacy and procurement. Cities with informal transit and no GTFS are out of scope for v1.

**Sustainable business model.** Riders use it free. Revenue from transit agencies and city transport departments for: the outage-normalisation service and rider alerts, accessibility reporting against ADA/Equality Act/EU obligations, and paratransit-shift analytics (each trip moved from demand-response to fixed route saves the agency tens of dollars); data licensing to mapping, real-estate and mobility companies; sponsored city onboarding by disability organisations. The core graph is open-licensed so the public value does not depend on the company surviving.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web application (map UI, routing API, Postgres/PostGIS, auth) deployable to the cloud by a small team. OpenTripPlanner (or a pgRouting graph) consumes GTFS with pathways and supports wheelchair routing; OSM via Overpass; Project Sidewalk exposes an open data API (GeoJSON/CSV, verified in its README); the Mobility Database provides feed discovery (API and CSV, verified in its README; the CSV host was blocked from this session). MTA and TfL publish elevator/outage APIs (training knowledge, not re-verified). Start with two or three cities that already publish pathways.txt and outage feeds. Hard parts: heterogeneity of agency outage feeds; sparse sidewalk data outside a handful of cities; keeping the graph fresh; liability if a route is wrongly marked accessible (mitigated with confidence levels and user verification); push alerts eventually need a mobile wrapper. No custom hardware, regulatory approval or physical logistics required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of planned trips completed without a fallback (self-reported and inferred), 'stranded by outage' incidents per 1,000 trips, paratransit trips per registered rider before and after adoption, median minutes from elevator failure to user alert, data-completeness percentage per city over time, agency outage duration trends, and retention among wheelchair-using riders.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/transit/master/gtfs/spec/en/reference.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/transit/master/gtfs-realtime/spec/en/reference.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ProjectSidewalk/SidewalkWebpage/master/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ProjectSidewalk/SidewalkWebpage/master/docs/ai-subsystems.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ProjectSidewalk/SidewalkWebpage/master/docs/onboarding-a-city.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AccessMap/accessmap/main/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OpenSidewalks/OpenSidewalks-Schema/main/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OpenSidewalks/opensidewalks-data/main/README.md

## 38. High-stakes online services (benefits, health, banking, government forms) are unusable for people with cognitive, learning and age-related impairments because accessibility practice stops at WCAG

*Domain:* accessibility-disability (cognitive and intellectual disability, learning disabilities, dementia and ageing, inaccessible websites and digital services) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.5

**Who experiences it.** People with intellectual disability (meta-analyses put prevalence around 1% globally; training knowledge, not re-verified), dyslexia and other learning disabilities (commonly cited at 5-10% of the population; not re-verified), ADHD, autism, acquired brain injury and mental-health conditions that affect concentration and memory; and older adults. VERIFIED (W3C COGA gap analysis): among people over 60, 38.4% experience Age Associated Memory Impairment, 15.3% Mild Cognitive Impairment and 8.8% dementia, and the global share of people aged 60+ is expected to reach 21.1% by 2050. Overlapping group: adults with low literacy (OECD PIAAC 2023 reports roughly one in five adults in participating countries at or below Level 1 literacy; not re-verified). Geographies: every 'digital by default' jurisdiction: UK (Universal Credit, NHS), US (SNAP/Medicaid portals, SSA), EU member states, Australia (myGov/NDIS), India (direct-benefit portals).

**Scale and severity.** VERIFIED PRIMARY EVIDENCE: the W3C COGA 'Making Content Usable' document cites research that seniors complete only 55.3% of attempted online tasks, and states that its own cognitive-accessibility guidance is 'supplemental guidance beyond the requirements of WCAG... not required for conformance', so standard accessibility audits do not measure these needs. The COGA gap analysis states that inability to use these interfaces makes people 'dependent on others for things that they could do themselves, increasing the crippling cost of care and reducing human dignity'. The W3C WAI-Adapt specification (personalisation semantics for symbols, simplification and help) exists only as a working draft. GSA's data dictionary confirms that the automated accessibility scan used across federal sites (axe-core) 'only catches problems a computer can detect automatically'. NOT RE-VERIFIED: WHO estimates 55+ million people living with dementia (2023), with nearly 10 million new cases a year. Severity: failure on a transactional journey means lost benefits, missed medical appointments, financial harm and forced dependence on carers. Honest caveat: hard outcome data quantifying harm specifically from cognitive inaccessibility is thin, which is why confidence is lower than for the other two problems.

**Why existing solutions fail.** WCAG 2.2 contains few testable cognitive criteria and COGA's guidance is explicitly optional, so compliance programmes stop at WCAG. Automated checkers detect only machine-testable issues and nothing about memory load, error recovery or clarity. Easy Read is produced manually, slowly and expensively, mostly for leaflets rather than live interactive journeys, and lags every service change. Generic plain-language tools and LLM rewriters change wording but do not address process design (COGA Objective 4: help users avoid and correct mistakes; Objective 6: do not rely on memory; Objective 5: help users focus), are not validated with users who have cognitive disabilities, and sit outside service owners' delivery workflows. WAI-Adapt semantics have near-zero deployment. Because no comparable metric exists, cognitive accessibility never gets prioritised against other backlog items.

**What people do today.** Relying on family members, carers, support workers or charities to complete forms (losing privacy and autonomy); phoning helplines or visiting offices in person; printing pages; abandoning applications and losing entitlements; charities producing Easy Read PDFs that drift out of date; screenshotting pages to ask friends; browser reader modes and text-to-speech.

**Proposed intervention.** A cognitive-accessibility co-validation platform for transactional journeys. (a) A service owner defines a journey (e.g. 'renew a benefit', 'book a hospital appointment'). (b) A structured audit maps the journey to COGA's eight design objectives and relevant WAI-Adapt attributes. (c) A paid, consented remote-testing panel of people with intellectual, learning and age-related cognitive disabilities runs real tasks on the live service through an instrumented task runner that records task success, time, errors, abandonment and where help was needed (no biometrics, easy-read consent, supporter co-consent where capacity requires). (d) Results become a standardised task-success score per journey, a prioritised fix list, and a validated plain-language 'how to do this' companion with symbol support that can be tagged with WAI-Adapt semantics. (e) Scores can be published to a public register. What is novel: measuring task success with the actual population and turning it into a comparable score plus a fairly paid tester marketplace (creating employment for people with intellectual disabilities), instead of merely rewriting text. It is useful without AI; AI only drafts simplifications and clusters failure patterns, always under human validation.

**Works across countries?.** COGA and WAI-Adapt are language-neutral; Easy-to-Read standards exist across the EU (Inclusion Europe), UK, Australia and Nordic countries; symbol sets differ by country and must be localised. Legal drivers exist in most target markets: UK Accessible Information Standard, European Accessibility Act (applicable since 28 June 2025; not re-verified), ADA Title II web rule (2026-27 deadlines), Australia's Disability Discrimination Act. Each language needs its own tester panel, which limits speed of expansion.

**Sustainable business model.** SaaS subscription plus per-study fees paid by government departments, health systems, banks, utilities and benefits administrators that carry legal duties; a fixed, published share of each study fee goes to testers; free tier for small charities; the public register and methodology stay free and open so scores remain credible. Never sell tester data. Revenue is aligned with social value because clients pay for measured improvement for the population being served.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web application with backend, database and auth: journey definitions, a COGA checklist engine, tester onboarding with accessible consent flows, a task runner (link plus guided steps, self-report, optional screen-recording consent), scoring, reports and payouts (e.g. Stripe Connect). No large external datasets are required; COGA patterns and WAI-Adapt vocabularies are open W3C documents. Hard parts: recruiting and safeguarding testers with intellectual disability (consent and capacity, exploitation risk, fair pay across countries), statistical validity of small panels, willingness of service owners to be publicly scored, and the risk of being perceived as a thin AI wrapper if the panel is skipped. No hardware and no regulatory approval, but research-ethics-grade governance is needed from day one.

**Measurable outcomes.** Task completion rate and error rate for panel users per journey before and after remediation; application abandonment rate on the live service; helpline and in-person visit volume for that service; median time-to-complete; number of journeys with published scores; tester earnings and repeat participation; proportion of recommended fixes shipped within 90 days.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/coga/master/gap-analysis/index.html
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/coga/master/content-usable/index.html
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/adapt/main/index.html
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/adapt/main/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-documentation/main/data/Site_Scanning_Data_Dictionary.csv
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/daisy/ace/master/README.md

## 39. The long tail of public-sector websites, online forms and PDFs stays inaccessible to blind, low-vision and keyboard-only users despite hard legal deadlines

*Domain:* accessibility-disability (blindness and low vision, motor impairment, inaccessible websites and documents, public services) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Blind and low-vision people using screen readers and magnifiers (WHO Blindness and Vision Impairment fact sheet, 2023: 2.2 billion people with near or distance vision impairment, of whom at least 1 billion have impairment that could have been prevented or is unaddressed; not re-verified), people with motor impairments using keyboard or switch access, and people with low vision who need adequate contrast. They are residents of the roughly 17,000 US entities with .gov domains (VERIFIED from CISA's registry read in this session: 16,999 domains, including 9,611 city, 3,052 county, 1,132 special-district, 293 tribal and 76 school-district domains) plus the many more local bodies on .org/.us (US Census of Governments counts roughly 90,000 local governments; not re-verified), EU public bodies under Directive 2016/2102, and UK public bodies under the 2018 public-sector accessibility regulations. Small governments are where the problem is worst because they have no accessibility staff.

**Scale and severity.** VERIFIED PRIMARY EVIDENCE: the US GSA Site Scanning programme (axe-core based) summary read in this session covers 12,212 federal URLs across 943 domains and 135 agencies; 6,519 of them (53%) lack a <main> landmark, which the programme's own dictionary calls basic accessibility practice for screen-reader users; in the all-URL snapshot 12,317 of 18,653 (66%) lack it. GSA's dictionary states its scan 'only catches problems a computer can detect automatically... not a substitute for a human accessibility review'; DAISY's Ace README likewise states 'only a limited portion of accessibility checks can be automated'. The UK GDS runs a dedicated accessibility monitoring and public-report platform for public-sector bodies (repo read), showing governments themselves treat this as an unresolved compliance problem. NOT RE-VERIFIED (webaim.org blocked): WebAIM Million 2025 found detectable WCAG 2 failures on 94.8% of the top one million home pages, averaging about 51 errors per page, with low-contrast text on roughly 79% and missing alt text on roughly 55% of pages. Legal context (ada.gov blocked, training knowledge): the DOJ ADA Title II web rule (April 2024) requires WCAG 2.1 AA for state and local government web content and mobile apps by 24 April 2026 for entities serving 50,000+ people and 26 April 2027 for smaller entities and special districts; the European Accessibility Act applies from 28 June 2025. Severity: inability to apply for benefits, pay taxes and fines, read emergency notices, register to vote or access school information; litigation is the dominant enforcement channel.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Enterprise monitoring and remediation suites are priced and designed for large organisations with accessibility teams, not for a town of 8,000 people. Overlay widgets do not fix underlying markup and are widely distrusted by screen-reader users. Automated checkers find a minority of issues and say nothing about PDF tagging or reading order; document remediation is manual and priced per page, so backlogs persist indefinitely. Government monitoring programmes (GSA, GDS) are internal, produce no resident-facing feedback loop and do not reach local entities. Small entities have no inventory distinguishing live forms from archival content (archived content is exempt under the Title II rule), so they cannot triage and often freeze.

**What people do today.** Residents phone or visit offices, rely on sighted relatives, use Be My Eyes or Aira volunteers/agents to read and fill forms, OCR PDFs themselves, or file complaints and lawsuits. Governments post accessibility statements saying 'contact us for an accessible version', buy overlay widgets, or deprioritise the work until sued.

**Proposed intervention.** An open continuous monitor plus resident barrier-reporting and remediation queue built for the long tail. (1) Seed from open registries (CISA .gov list, GSA federal website index, EU and UK public-body lists). (2) Crawl with a headless browser plus axe-core and a PDF analyser that detects untagged PDFs, missing document language, scanned-image-only PDFs and form fields without labels. (3) Classify documents into live forms / current notices / archive to build each entity's document inventory and apply the archive exemption correctly. (4) Publish a public scorecard per entity and let residents report a barrier against a specific page or document, producing a structured, timestamped grievance record (Title II requires a grievance procedure). (5) Give the entity a prioritised remediation queue ranked by traffic, task criticality and legal exposure, with concrete fix guidance and evidence export for audits. What is novel: public accountability plus a resident loop plus document triage aimed at entities that cannot afford a vendor, with all scan data open. It works without AI; AI only proposes alt text or tag structure for human review.

**Works across countries?.** WCAG and axe-core are global; EU member states must monitor public bodies under Directive 2016/2102 and publish lists of them; the UK, Australia and India (GIGW) have equivalent regimes. Only the registry-seeding step and the legal exemption rules are country-specific.

**Sustainable business model.** Free public scan and scorecard for every entity; paid tiers for the remediation workflow, document inventory, grievance management and audit evidence packs at prices small entities can pay (hundreds to low thousands of dollars per year); bulk agreements through municipal leagues, state associations and county IT consortia; grants from disability funders for the open dataset. Scores and raw scan results stay open so the public value does not depend on sales, and the company cannot drift into overlay-style compliance theatre.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible: a Node or Python crawler using Playwright plus axe-core, a PDF analyser (pikepdf/pdfcpu for tag trees, language and form fields; heuristic OCR detection), Postgres, auth, public scorecard pages, resident reports and remediation tickets. Public data verified in this session: CISA dotgov-data CSV (16,999 domains with type, organisation, city, state) and GSA Site Scanning reports and data dictionary (GSA also offers an API, not reachable from this session). Hard parts: crawl volume and politeness, JavaScript-heavy sites, false positives, PDF heuristics, legal nuance about exemptions, slow government sales cycles, and a crowded vendor market (Siteimprove, Monsido/CivicPlus, AudioEye, Level Access, Deque) in which differentiation must come from openness, document triage and the resident loop rather than from scanning itself. No hardware, regulatory approval or logistics required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Violations per page over time for monitored entities; percentage of live forms that are tagged and labelled; median time from resident barrier report to fix; task completion rates for screen-reader users on each entity's top tasks; number of entities meeting Title II or EAA requirements by their deadline; reduction in formal complaints and lawsuits.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-analysis/main/reports/snapshot-primary.csv
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-analysis/main/reports/snapshot-all.csv
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-documentation/main/data/Site_Scanning_Data_Dictionary.csv
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-documentation/main/pages/scan_steps.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-documentation/main/README.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GSA/site-scanning-analysis/main/README.md
