## 40. Recruitment-fee debt bondage of low-wage migrant workers in Asia-Gulf, Asia-Malaysia/East Asia and Central America-US corridors

*Domain:* labor-migration · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Low-wage temporary labour migrants, overwhelmingly men aged 20-40 from Bangladesh, Nepal, India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Indonesia, Philippines, Myanmar, Vietnam, Ethiopia, Kenya and Uganda going to Saudi Arabia, Qatar, UAE, Kuwait, Oman, Malaysia, Taiwan, South Korea and Japan (construction, security, cleaning, plantations, electronics, gloves, fisheries); women domestic workers from Indonesia, Philippines, Ethiopia, Sri Lanka going to the Gulf, Hong Kong, Singapore, Lebanon; and Mexican/Guatemalan/Honduran H-2A/H-2B workers in US agriculture, landscaping and seafood. The debt is also carried by families at origin who mortgage land or borrow from moneylenders.

**Scale and severity.** VERIFICATION CAVEAT: in this session the web-search budget was exhausted and the environment's network policy blocked every fetch to ilo.org, worldbank.org, knomad.org and unhcr.org, so all figures below are from the researcher's prior knowledge of the named reports and must be re-checked. ILO/Walk Free/IOM Global Estimates of Modern Slavery (published 2022, reference year 2021): 27.6 million people in forced labour on any given day, about 86% in the private economy; adult migrant workers are more than three times as likely to be in forced labour as non-migrant adults; withholding of wages and debt are among the most common means of coercion. ILO 'Profits and Poverty: The Economics of Forced Labour' (2024): illegal profits from forced labour about US$236 billion per year (2021), roughly US$10,000 per victim, up about US$64 billion from the 2014 estimate. ILO Global Estimates on International Migrant Workers (4th edition, reference year 2022): about 167.7 million international migrant workers, roughly 4.7% of the global labour force; the Arab States have the highest migrant share of any regional workforce (about 40%). Recruitment-cost surveys run by KNOMAD and ILO for SDG indicator 10.7.1 (2015-2021 pilots) found Bangladeshi and Pakistani workers paid on the order of US$3,000-5,000 to reach Gulf jobs, equal to roughly 8-9+ months of destination earnings; Nepali and Indian workers paid roughly US$1,000-1,500 (3-4 months); Vietnamese workers to Japan's trainee programme several thousand dollars. Enforcement actions show the scale per employer: Top Glove (Malaysia) repaid about RM150 million (roughly US$36 million) in recruitment fees to around 13,000 workers in 2021 after a US CBP Withhold Release Order; Qatar's World Cup Supreme Committee reported more than US$28 million reimbursed or committed to roughly 49,000 workers by 2022. Severity: fees are typically illegal in the destination country and capped at origin, yet are paid to unlicensed sub-agents in cash with no receipt, leaving the worker unable to quit, complain or refuse unsafe work for the 1-2 years it takes to repay at 20-60% interest.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Employer Pays Principle commitments by multinational buyers and IOM's IRIS ethical-recruiter certification cover a tiny share of recruiters and never reach the village-level sub-agents who collect most of the money. (2) Origin-country fee caps (Bangladesh BMET corridor caps, Nepal's 'free visa, free ticket' policy, India eMigrate caps, Philippines' no-fee rules for domestic workers) are unenforced because the ministry has no record of what was actually paid. (3) Social audits in the destination rely on on-site worker interviews where fear of retaliation suppresses disclosure, and auditors have no independent payment evidence. (4) Information apps (e.g. Just Good Work) tell workers the rules but create no durable evidence; recruiter-rating sites (Contratados.org for US-Mexico) are single-corridor and rating-based, not payment-based. (5) Remediation happens only reactively after a CBP Withhold Release Order or a brand exposure, years later, and concentrated in a few Malaysian exporters; workers in construction, security and domestic work supplying no exporting brand get nothing. (6) Supply-chain due-diligence laws (EU CSDDD, German LkSG, UK Modern Slavery Act) require companies to find and remediate fees but companies have no worker-side data source to find them.

**What people do today.** Borrow from informal moneylenders (commonly 20-60% annual interest) or relatives, mortgage or sell farmland, pawn jewellery; rely on a trusted village sub-agent because a licensed agency is unreachable; vet agents through Facebook/WhatsApp groups of returnees; keep no receipt or a handwritten slip; after arrival, endure substituted contracts and lower pay because leaving means returning with unpayable debt; complain to the embassy labour wing or an NGO (Migrant Forum in Asia network, Pravasi Nepali Coordination Committee, OKUP in Bangladesh, Tenaganita in Malaysia) only in extreme cases; some obtain refunds only because a Western buyer's audit or a CBP order forces the employer to pay.

**Proposed intervention.** A worker-held recruitment-cost ledger and corridor fee-cap evidence system, built as a mobile-first web app plus backend. Each worker (or an NGO/union intake worker on their behalf) logs every payment in the recruitment chain: amount, date, payee (sub-agent, licensed agency, medical centre, training centre, visa trader), what it was for, and a photo of any receipt, with the entry hashed and timestamped so it cannot be backdated. The backend holds a curated rule base of legal fee caps and prohibitions per origin-destination corridor (origin caps, destination fee bans, employer-pays obligations in US H-2A/H-2B regulations, Qatar Law 21/2015, Malaysian Employment Act rules), computes the illegal excess per worker, and aggregates by named agency/sub-agent and by destination employer. Three outputs are novel relative to existing tools: (a) an automatically generated, jurisdiction-specific refund/remediation evidence packet (employer, buyer, origin ministry, CBP/NCP complaint formats) the worker or their NGO can file; (b) a sub-agent-level map of who is collecting illegal fees in which districts, exposed to origin-country licensing authorities and to buyers doing due diligence, with worker identities withheld unless the worker consents; (c) a corridor cost-ratio benchmark (the SDG 10.7.1 metric) updated continuously from real entries rather than from occasional surveys. No AI is required for the core; optional OCR/translation of receipts is a convenience. Everything can be entered pre-departure (when workers still have time and are not yet under employer control) and the record survives the worker's phone confiscation because it is server-side.

**Works across countries?.** The pattern (illegal fees collected at origin by informal sub-agents, prohibited in the destination, remediable through employer or buyer pressure) is the same across Asia-Gulf, Africa-Gulf, Asia-Malaysia, Vietnam/Indonesia-Taiwan/Japan/Korea, and Mexico/Central America-US H-2 corridors. What differs is the legal rule set and the remedy channel, so the system must be configured per corridor; a corridor rule base is a modest curation task and already exists in fragments (ILO NATLEX, KNOMAD surveys, US DOL H-2A rules). Works less well where recruitment is state-run with no private sub-agent layer (Korea's EPS) because fees are already low.

**Sustainable business model.** Workers never pay. Revenue from (1) buyers and tier-1 suppliers who must demonstrate due diligence and remediation under EU CSDDD, German LkSG, Norway Transparency Act and US CBP forced-labour enforcement, paying per supplier/corridor for anonymised prevalence data and verified remediation tracking; (2) licensed recruitment agencies paying for a verified-receipt certification that proves they charged within caps (a differentiator when buyers demand ethical recruitment); (3) grants/contracts from ILO Fair Recruitment, IOM, origin-country ministries that need SDG 10.7.1 data. Safeguards: worker data governed by a worker-organisation board, aggregate data sold only in anonymised form, worker consent required before any identifying disclosure, so the commercial side cannot erode the protective purpose.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable by a small team as a web app (Next.js/React front end, Postgres, auth with phone-OTP and NGO/organisation roles, S3-type storage for receipt images, hashed append-only ledger). Real public data usable now: US DOL OFLC H-2A/H-2B disclosure data (quarterly files listing certified employers, agents and worksites), DOL WHD H-2A debarment lists, Philippines DMW and Nepal DoFE licensed-agency lists (scrapable), ILO NATLEX law texts and KNOMAD recruitment-cost survey microdata for benchmarks. There is no clean API for origin-country fee caps; a hand-curated corridor rule base of ~15 corridors is required and is the main curation cost. Hard parts, honestly: reaching workers pre-departure (distribution via NGOs, pre-departure orientation centres and returnee networks, not app-store discovery); evidentiary weight of self-logged payments (mitigated by timestamps, receipt photos and corroboration across many workers naming the same sub-agent); retaliation risk and data protection under origin and destination law; low literacy and feature-phone users (needs a USSD/SMS or NGO-assisted intake path, which is still software). No custom hardware, no regulatory approval and no logistics network are needed; the tool is useful even with zero AI.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median recruitment cost ratio (months of destination earnings; SDG 10.7.1) for enrolled corridors over time; share of enrolled workers with illegal fees who receive a refund and total US$ refunded within 12 months (baseline today is near zero outside brand-exposed Malaysian exporters); number of sub-agents identified in two or more independent worker ledgers and number subsequently sanctioned or delisted by origin authorities; average debt outstanding at arrival among enrolled vs comparable non-enrolled workers; reduction in forced-labour indicators (passport retention, inability to quit) reported at 6-month follow-up.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/global-estimates-modern-slavery-forced-labour-and-forced-marriage
- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/profits-and-poverty-economics-forced-labour
- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/ilo-global-estimates-international-migrant-workers-results-and-methodology
- https://www.knomad.org/publication/migration-and-development-brief-41
- https://www.cbp.gov/trade/forced-labor/withhold-release-orders-and-findings
- https://iris.iom.int/
- https://contratados.org/
- https://www.dol.gov/agencies/eta/foreign-labor/performance

## 41. Wage theft against migrant and informal workers, and the collapse of wage claims once the worker leaves the job or the country

*Domain:* labor-migration · *Researcher confidence:* 0.55

**Who experiences it.** Migrant workers on employer-tied visas in Gulf states (construction, security, cleaning, domestic work), Malaysia, Singapore, Taiwan, Japan and Korea; undocumented and temporary workers in the US (construction, restaurants, agriculture, car washes, home care), Australia (hospitality, horticulture, international students), the UK (care sector sponsored visas), Italy and Spain (agriculture); and informal workers generally, including refugees working without permits in Türkiye, Lebanon, Jordan, Uganda, Colombia. Women domestic workers and workers paid in cash or by the piece are hit hardest because no record exists.

**Scale and severity.** VERIFICATION CAVEAT: figures are from prior knowledge of the named reports; the session's network policy blocked all source fetches and the search budget was exhausted, so re-verify before use. ILO/Walk Free/IOM Global Estimates (2022): systematic withholding of wages was the single most commonly reported means of coercion in forced labour (roughly a third of cases), i.e. wage theft is the mechanism by which much of the 27.6 million forced-labour total is maintained. United States: Economic Policy Institute (2017) estimated minimum-wage violations alone take about US$15 billion per year from workers nationally (US$8 billion in the ten most populous states), with earlier EPI work putting all forms of wage theft near US$50 billion per year; the US DOL Wage and Hour Division recovers only around US$270-280 million per year (FY2023-24) with fewer than 800 investigators for roughly 165 million workers. UCLA Labor Center/NELP 'Hollow Victories' (2013) found that of California workers who won wage judgments in 2008-2011, only about 17% ever collected any money (about US$42 million of US$282 million awarded). Australia: the Fair Work Ombudsman recovered on the order of A$470-510 million per year for workers in 2022-23 and 2023-24, repeatedly finding migrant workers over-represented; wage theft became a federal crime in January 2025. Gulf: Qatar's Workers' Support and Insurance Fund had paid out more than US$300 million to over 35,000 workers by late 2022 (ILO Qatar programme reporting), and wage complaints are the dominant category among the tens of thousands of cases filed annually at Qatar's labour dispute committees; Migrant Forum in Asia's Justice for Wage Theft campaign (2020-2023) documented thousands of returnees sent home during COVID-19 without months of wages or end-of-service benefits. Severity: stolen wages typically equal several months of income for people remitting most of their pay; for employer-tied visa holders the claim is forfeited at departure.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Burden of proof sits on the worker, who has no payslips, no contract copy, is paid in cash, and whose phone may be confiscated; (2) Gulf Wage Protection Systems verify that a bank transfer happened, not that the amount was right, and are evaded by cash top-ups, deductions and non-registration; (3) labour courts and agencies are slow (months to years) while the worker has weeks before their visa ends, so employers settle for pennies against an exit permit or simply wait the worker out; (4) once the worker has left the country there is no practical way to pursue, be heard, or collect, so most transnational claims die, which is the specific gap not addressed by any national enforcement body; (5) in the US and Australia enforcement capacity is tiny relative to the workforce, private lawyers reject small claims, and employers dissolve or rename entities so judgments go unpaid; (6) fear of immigration consequences deters undocumented workers even where the law protects them; (7) existing worker-facing apps are either information-only or single-jurisdiction hour trackers with no legal-rate computation, no claim generation and no cross-border persistence.

**What people do today.** Photographing timesheets, rosters and WhatsApp messages from supervisors; handwritten notebooks of hours; co-worker testimony; going to the embassy labour attache or a worker centre/NGO (Workers Defense Project, Migrant Forum in Asia members, Migrante, Nepali and Bangladeshi diaspora groups); publicising on social media; accepting partial settlements in exchange for an exit permit or release letter; filing with the state labour agency and then abandoning the claim after returning home; in the Gulf, signing wage waivers to obtain a no-objection certificate; in the US, small-claims court or state wage boards where the award is rarely collected.

**Proposed intervention.** A worker-side wage ledger and transnational claim-persistence platform. Core (no AI needed): a multilingual web/PWA where the worker or an NGO intake worker records contract terms, hours, pay received, deductions and in-kind charges against an embedded rule base of the legal minimum wage, overtime, rest-day and end-of-service rules for the jurisdiction (US federal and state, Australian awards via the FWO pay rates, UK, Gulf labour laws, Malaysia, Taiwan); the system continuously computes the amount owed and keeps the evidence hashed and server-side so it survives phone loss or confiscation. Differentiators: (a) jurisdiction-specific claim generation: it produces the demand letter and the agency/committee filing in the exact format and language of the destination body (DOL WHD, state labour commissioner, FWO, UK employment tribunal ET1, Qatar/UAE labour dispute procedures, Malaysian Labour Department); (b) claim hand-off across borders: a power-of-attorney and case-file transfer workflow so an NGO or lawyer in the destination can continue the case after the worker has returned home, with the worker tracking status from the origin country, a channel that today functionally does not exist; (c) employer pattern detection: multiple independent ledgers naming the same employer or labour supplier produce a repeat-offender signal for inspectorates and for buyers' due diligence, with workers' identities withheld. Collection support: links the judgment to public corporate registries and (in the US) to DOL enforcement data to find successor entities.

**Works across countries?.** Wage theft and the departure-forfeiture problem exist in every destination country; the rule base and filing formats are jurisdiction-specific but bounded (a few dozen jurisdictions cover most migrant workers). The cross-border hand-off needs an NGO/legal partner in each destination; this is a partnership task, not a technical one. Less effective where there is no functioning labour-dispute body at all, in which case the tool is mainly an evidence bank for later remediation via buyers or the origin-country embassy.

**Sustainable business model.** Workers never pay. (1) Subscriptions from unions, worker centres, legal-aid organisations and migrant-support NGOs that use it as case-management and intake software (these organisations already pay for case tools); (2) origin-country governments and overseas-worker welfare funds (Philippines OWWA, Sri Lanka SLBFE, Nepal Foreign Employment Board) funding claim pursuit for their nationals; (3) where legal ethics permit, partner law firms on contingency for aggregated claims against the same employer; (4) anonymised repeat-offender analytics licensed to buyers and inspectorates. Safeguards: contingency partners cannot cherry-pick because intake is open to all workers and small claims are bundled; data is never sold identified; governance includes worker organisations. Honest risk: unions and NGOs have thin budgets, so the sustainable core is likely origin-government welfare funds plus buyer due-diligence revenue.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app with backend, Postgres, auth (worker, NGO case-worker, lawyer roles), document storage and PDF generation. Real public data available now: US DOL enforcement data (enforcedata.dol.gov bulk WHD case data for repeat-offender lookup), US federal and state minimum wage and overtime rules (published tables), Australia's FWO pay rates (published award rates), UK National Minimum Wage rates and employment tribunal forms, ILO NATLEX texts for Gulf and Asian labour laws, OpenCorporates for entity lookup. Hard parts: no open API for Gulf dispute procedures (must be hand-encoded and kept current); evidentiary weight of self-recorded hours (improved by contemporaneous timestamps, geolocation at clock-in if the worker opts in, and corroboration across co-workers); power-of-attorney validity across borders; reaching workers before the dispute rather than after; retaliation. No custom hardware, regulatory approval or logistics required; the ledger plus rule-based calculation plus claim generator is useful with no AI at all. AI is optional for translation and receipt OCR.

**Measurable outcomes.** US$ in back wages and end-of-service benefits recovered per 1,000 enrolled workers; share of claims that continue to resolution after the worker has left the country (baseline near zero); median days from first missed payment to filed claim; share of filed claims supported by contemporaneous documentary evidence; collection rate on judgments won (vs the ~17% California baseline); number of employers flagged by two or more independent ledgers that are subsequently inspected or sanctioned.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/global-estimates-modern-slavery-forced-labour-and-forced-marriage
- https://www.epi.org/publication/employers-steal-billions-from-workers-paychecks-each-year/
- https://enforcedata.dol.gov/
- https://www.fairwork.gov.au/
- https://mfasia.org/
- https://www.business-humanrights.org/
- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/ilo-global-estimates-international-migrant-workers-results-and-methodology

## 42. Occupational heat stress injuries, deaths and chronic kidney disease among outdoor, piece-rate and migrant workers

*Domain:* labor-migration · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Construction, agriculture, delivery, sanitation, brick-kiln, fisheries and plantation workers, disproportionately internal and international migrants and informal workers: South Asian migrants on Gulf construction sites, farmworkers in the US, Mexico, Spain and Italy (largely migrants), sugarcane cutters in Central America and Sri Lanka (Mesoamerican nephropathy belt), brick-kiln and construction workers in India and Pakistan, delivery riders in South and Southeast Asia, and workers across Sub-Saharan Africa where more than 90% of the workforce is exposed to excessive heat. Piece-rate and daily-wage workers are worst affected because every rest break costs them pay.

**Scale and severity.** VERIFICATION CAVEAT: figures are from prior knowledge of the named ILO reports; fetches were blocked by the session's network policy and the search budget was exhausted, so re-verify before use. ILO 'Ensuring safety and health at work in a changing climate' (April 2024) and ILO 'Heat at work' (July 2024), reference year 2020: about 2.41 billion workers (over 70% of the global workforce) are exposed to excessive heat at some point each year; excessive heat causes an estimated 22.85 million occupational injuries, about 18,970 deaths and 2.09 million DALYs per year; an estimated 26.2 million people live with chronic kidney disease linked to workplace heat stress; exposure shares are highest in Africa (about 93%) and the Arab States (about 84%). The ILO reports also attribute about 18,960 deaths per year from non-melanoma skin cancer to occupational UV exposure and around 860,000 deaths among outdoor workers to air pollution. Official national counts are far lower than the ILO modelled figures (US BLS records only a few dozen heat deaths a year, while independent US estimates run into the hundreds to low thousands), which is itself evidence of undercounting. Severity is rising with climate change: the number of days above WBGT action thresholds in the Gulf, South Asia and the US Southwest is increasing, and heat stress also drives falls, equipment accidents and the irreversible kidney disease that has killed tens of thousands of young cane cutters in Central America.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Gulf and South Asian rules are calendar-and-clock bans (e.g. UAE and Saudi midday breaks from mid-June to mid-September; Qatar added a WBGT 32.1 C stop-work rule in 2021) that ignore actual conditions on dangerous days outside the window and are rarely enforced on small sites; (2) the US has no federal heat standard in force (OSHA's proposed rule published in 2024 has not been finalised), so protection depends on a handful of state rules (California Title 8 s3395, Washington, Oregon, Maryland) and most LMICs have none; (3) employers rarely know the applicable trigger thresholds for a specific site-hour, and keep no auditable record of rest, shade and water provision, so inspections cannot verify compliance; (4) piece-rate and daily-wage pay makes workers skip rest even where it is legally guaranteed; (5) existing tools (the OSHA-NIOSH Heat Safety Tool app) show a heat index but encode no jurisdiction rules, no worker entitlements, no acclimatisation schedules and no record; (6) proven protocols (rest-shade-hydration programmes such as La Isla Network's work with cane cutters showed reduced kidney injury) are not scaled because there is no low-cost system to schedule, deliver and prove them.

**What people do today.** Self-pacing, working at night or pre-dawn, soaking clothing, improvised shade, carrying water from home; foremen using phone weather apps and intuition; employers relying on the statutory midday ban only; workers hiding symptoms to avoid being sent home unpaid; migrant workers accepting heat illness as part of the job because complaining risks contract termination; NGOs and unions distributing paper heat-safety leaflets; some large contractors in Qatar and the US buying WBGT meters and running their own programmes, which small subcontractors cannot replicate.

**Proposed intervention.** A site-level heat-rule compliance and worker-entitlement engine, delivered as a web app with backend, that converts open weather data into legally specific, auditable actions. The backend pulls hourly temperature, humidity, solar radiation and wind from open APIs (Open-Meteo, NOAA NWS api.weather.gov, Copernicus ERA5-HEAT for historical calibration), estimates WBGT per site using the Liljegren method, and applies a curated rule base of jurisdiction thresholds and required responses (Qatar Ministerial Decision 17/2021, UAE/Saudi/Kuwait/Oman/Bahrain midday bans, California s3395 and the 2024 indoor rule s3396, Washington, Oregon, Maryland, Spain's 2023 heat-alert work ban, Italian regional ordinances, OSHA's proposed 80 F / 90 F triggers, and ISO 7243 / ACGIH TLV defaults where no law exists). Outputs that do not exist today in one tool: (a) an hour-by-hour site schedule stating exactly when work must stop, how many minutes of rest per hour are due, and acclimatisation ramps for new or returning workers; (b) a tamper-evident compliance log (rest breaks taken, water and shade checks) that an employer can show an inspector or insurer and a union can use in a complaint; (c) worker-facing multilingual text notifications (SMS/WhatsApp/PWA) stating in plain language the rest they are legally entitled to right now, plus a one-tap anonymous report when it is denied, aggregated by site and contractor for inspectorates; (d) piece-rate protection: a calculator showing the guaranteed minimum pay for mandated rest time where the law requires it (e.g. California's paid rest for piece-rate workers). No AI is required; the value is in rule encoding, open weather data and the record.

**Works across countries?.** Outdoor heat exposure is global and the weather inputs are globally available from open APIs. Legal thresholds differ, but the engine can ship with ISO 7243 / ACGIH defaults and overlay national or state rules where they exist; the Gulf, US states, Spain, Italy, Greece, Cyprus and Qatar already have codified triggers, and India's and Pakistan's city heat action plans provide thresholds that can be adopted voluntarily. Weakest where there is neither a rule nor an employer with any compliance incentive (purely informal work), in which case the worker-facing entitlement/alert side still functions as a safety and advocacy tool.

**Sustainable business model.** Worker-facing side always free. Revenue from (1) contractors and employers who face heat regulations or buyer audits (Gulf main contractors, US agricultural and construction employers in regulated states, Spanish and Italian agribusiness) paying per site for the schedule and auditable log, which reduces citation and liability risk; (2) workers' compensation insurers and construction insurers offering premium credits for logged compliance, a common pattern for safety telematics; (3) public agencies and labour inspectorates licensing the inspection view; (4) development and climate adaptation funders for LMIC deployments. Safeguards: worker reports are anonymous by default and the log records employer obligations, not worker productivity, so the commercial side cannot be turned into a surveillance tool.

**MVP feasibility.** High feasibility for a small team: Open-Meteo provides free hourly forecasts and archives (temperature, relative humidity, shortwave radiation, wind) worldwide without an API key; NOAA NWS provides US forecasts and heat products; WBGT estimation from these inputs is a published algorithm. Jurisdiction rules are public documents and need hand-encoding for perhaps 15-20 jurisdictions. The stack is a standard web app (map-based site setup, scheduling engine, notification service via SMS/WhatsApp Business API, Postgres, auth with employer/worker/inspector roles). Honest hard parts: gridded weather underestimates on-site WBGT on reflective or enclosed sites (optional, not required, on-site sensors can calibrate); employers may decline to log; workers cannot compel rest, so impact depends on pairing the tool with inspectorates, unions or buyer audits; indoor heat (kitchens, warehouses, garment factories) needs sensor input and is out of scope for a pure open-data MVP. No regulatory approval is needed to deploy; no custom hardware is required for the core.

**Measurable outcomes.** Heat-related illness incidents and workers' compensation claims per 1,000 workers on enrolled sites versus matched sites; share of hours above the applicable WBGT threshold during which mandated rest was actually logged; number of worker-reported rest denials and their resolution time; reduction in OSHA/state citations or Gulf inspectorate violations for enrolled contractors; for longer-term studies, incidence of acute kidney injury markers among enrolled piece-rate workers compared with the pre-enrolment baseline.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://www.ilo.org/publications/ensuring-safety-and-health-work-changing-climate
- https://open-meteo.com/en/docs
- https://www.osha.gov/heat-exposure/rulemaking
- https://www.dir.ca.gov/title8/3395.html
- https://api.weather.gov/
- https://www.ilo.org/publications/major-publications/ilo-global-estimates-international-migrant-workers-results-and-methodology

## 43. Eviction cases decided without the tenant: no lawyer, no answer filed, and a permanent public record

*Domain:* housing-justice · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Low-income renters in the United States facing a court eviction filing, concentrated among Black women with children: Black renters are 18.6% of US renters but account for over half of eviction filings (Graetz et al., PNAS 2023, cited by Eviction Lab); 58% of named defendants in 2024 were women vs 53% of renters (Eviction Lab 2024 report). Geographically concentrated in Sun Belt and Southern metros: in 2025 landlords filed one case for every four renter households in metro Atlanta, and Phoenix recorded a record 86,946 filings in 2024 (14.3% of renter households). Parallel populations exist wherever possession proceedings are formal and tenants are unrepresented: England (where the Renters' Rights Act, in force 1 May 2026, abolished Section 21 'no-fault' evictions so more cases will now be contested in court), Ontario's Landlord and Tenant Board, Australian state tribunals.

**Scale and severity.** Across the 48 sites the Eviction Lab tracks (home to roughly one-third of US renter households) landlords filed 1.23 million eviction cases in 2025 and 1.26 million in 2024; the average filing rate was 8.0%, i.e. one case per 13 renter households (Eviction Lab, 'Eviction Filing Patterns in 2025', April 2026). National estimates are roughly 3.6 million filings and about 1 million eviction judgments a year (Gromis et al., PNAS 2022, as cited by the Eviction Research Network); the Eviction Lab's own 2016 count was 2.35 million filings and 898,479 evictions with New York and California records incomplete. Representation: across ~40 local studies compiled by the National Coalition for a Civil Right to Counsel, about 4% of tenants have a lawyer versus 84% of landlords; in NYC before Right to Counsel it was 1% vs 99%. Non-appearance: across New York State courts 54% of nonpayment filings went unanswered by the tenant (2016 to mid-2022) and defaults account for 40% of eviction warrants issued (NYU Furman Center, 2023). Health: an eviction filing is associated with a 19% higher risk of death in following years and a judgment with 40% higher risk (Graetz et al., 2024, 38 million court records linked to Census and death records). Records: roughly 22% of eviction records carry ambiguous or false outcome information (Porton, Gromis and Desmond, 2021); the tenant-screening industry that scrapes them is valued at $3.77 billion; sealing laws now exist in over twenty states plus DC but only California and Colorado seal from the moment of filing and most others require a petition (Eviction Lab 'Scarlet E', July 2026). Context: LSC's 2022 Justice Gap report found low-income Americans got no or inadequate help for 92% of civil legal problems that substantially affected them, and LSC-funded organizations turn away about 49% of requests. Cross-country indicator: more than 70,000 homeless households in London were in temporary accommodation in May 2025 (BBC), and the APPG on temporary accommodation cited it as a possible factor in the deaths of 74 children, mostly under one, between April 2019 and March 2024. Note: these figures were read from the Eviction Lab's own website source repository and GitHub-hosted mirrors because the session's network blocked the original hosts.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Right to counsel works (84% of represented NYC tenants stayed housed in early years; an estimated $12 return per $1 in Philadelphia per Stout 2018) but exists in only about 5 states and ~2 dozen cities as of 2026, and even NYC's program cannot staff demand: the NYC Bar testified that 'there are not enough attorneys available to meet the need, with the result that thousands of tenants are going unrepresented' despite serving 43,000 households in FY2023. Legal aid is funded to serve a fraction of eligible people. Existing DIY tools are single-jurisdiction and passive: Suffolk LIT Lab's Massachusetts docassemble interviews (motion to stay, petition to seal), JustFix (NYC), Rentervention (Illinois), TexasLawHelp answer forms. None is triggered by the court docket itself, so the tenant who does not know a case was filed, or does not know the answer deadline (filing-to-judgment windows run 0 to 23 days by state), never reaches the form. Record sealing requires a petition most tenants never learn about, and screening companies scrape dockets before sealing happens, so the remedy arrives too late. Court e-filing systems (Tyler Odyssey) are closed, and unauthorized-practice-of-law rules keep most tools at the level of static information.

**What people do today.** Most tenants simply do not respond and move out before the hearing or lockout (the Eviction Research Network notes most leave well before lockout); many negotiate alone with a landlord's attorney in the courthouse hallway; some borrow from family or payday lenders to cure arrears (90% of evicted tenants owe at least one month, median about $1,252 at move-out, NBER 2024); others call 211 or legal aid hotlines and are turned away; informal, forced moves without any court case are estimated at 5.5 for every formal eviction (American Housing Survey 2017). After the case, tenants with a record double up with relatives, apply under a relative's name, or accept substandard housing from landlords who do not screen.

**Proposed intervention.** A docket-triggered eviction defense and record-relief engine. (1) Ingest public eviction dockets for a jurisdiction (the same court feeds the Eviction Lab already tracks in 48 sites; open data in NYC, Philadelphia, Harris County, Maricopa) and, for a tenant who registers or is matched by an advocate, detect the filing and compute the jurisdiction-specific answer deadline and hearing date, then push deadline reminders by SMS/email. (2) Run a structured defense-discovery interview mapped to that jurisdiction's statutory defenses, with automated evidence checks against public datasets: CARES Act 30-day-notice coverage of the building via HUD multifamily, LIHTC, FHA and GSE property databases; open code-violation and inspection records from city open data; ownership and rent-registration records; and the landlord's filing concentration (the top 100 buildings account for up to 58% of filings in some cities). (3) Generate a court-ready answer with affirmative defenses, exhibit packet, and fee-waiver form, in the court's own format. (4) After judgment or dismissal, auto-generate the state-specific sealing or expungement petition and FCRA dispute letters to the screening companies, keyed to a maintained rules table of each state's eligibility conditions. (5) Offer an intake API that hands legal aid and right-to-counsel providers a pre-built case file to cut attorney intake time. What is novel is the timing (triggered by the docket, before the deadline) and the cross-jurisdiction rules graph coupled to public-data evidence checks; it is a rules-and-data product that works without AI, with optional language-model help only for drafting narrative statements.

**Works across countries?.** The core pattern (unrepresented tenant, short deadlines, default judgments, durable records) recurs in England and Wales (possession claims, defence form N11, with contested hearings set to rise after Section 21's abolition on 1 May 2026), Ontario, Australia, and much of Europe. The product design transfers, but the content does not: each jurisdiction needs its own deadline rules, defense catalog, forms, and docket access, and public docket availability varies from excellent (US open courts) to poor (many European courts). Realistically a US-first product with a jurisdiction-plugin architecture.

**Sustainable business model.** Never landlord-funded. Revenue from institutions that already pay to reduce defaults and attorney time: court self-help centers and eviction-diversion programs (per-case or annual license; many are ARPA- or state-funded), right-to-counsel and legal aid providers (intake-triage SaaS, fundable through LSC Technology Initiative Grants), emergency rental assistance administrators, and municipal housing departments. Individual tenants always free; an optional paid record-sealing and screening-dispute service for tenants who can afford it (Upsolve-style freemium) is defensible as long as the free path is complete. Honest assessment: moderate sustainability; revenue depends on public and philanthropic budgets, but the institutional buyers are real and the cost-benefit evidence (Stout) is already accepted by them.

**MVP feasibility.** Buildable as a web app (backend, database, auth for tenants and advocates) by a small team: start with two or three jurisdictions whose dockets are public and machine-readable (e.g., Harris County JP courts, Philadelphia Municipal Court, NYC via OCA data), a rules database of answer deadlines and defenses, a docassemble-style interview, PDF generation in court formats, and SMS/email deadline reminders. Real public data exists: court dockets, HUD/LIHTC/FHA/GSE property databases for CARES Act coverage, city code-violation open data, state sealing statutes. Hard parts: docket formats differ and some courts publish only scanned images; matching a registered tenant to a filing (name and address variants); e-filing is closed so output is print-and-file; unauthorized-practice rules require the product to stay at 'legal information plus forms' or operate under a legal aid partner; maintaining 50-state rules is ongoing editorial work. No hardware, no regulatory approval before use.

**Measurable outcomes.** Share of filings with an answer or appearance filed among enrolled tenants versus the jurisdiction baseline (e.g., 54% unanswered in New York); default-judgment rate; share of cases dismissed, settled with time, or won; days from filing to first contact; sealing petitions filed and granted; FCRA disputes resolved; attorney intake minutes per case for partner legal aid offices; six-month housing retention of enrolled tenants versus matched non-users.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/EvictionLab/eviction-lab-website/blob/HEAD/content/updates/research/ets-report-2025/index.md

## 44. Losing Medicaid and SNAP over paperwork rather than eligibility: procedural terminations, denials and churn

*Domain:* housing-justice · *Researcher confidence:* 0.8

**Who experiences it.** Low-income households enrolled in or applying for means-tested benefits, especially people with unstable addresses (renters who move, people doubled up or homeless), non-English speakers, people with disabilities, and parents of children on Medicaid/CHIP. In the US this is tens of millions of people per renewal cycle; the same mechanism operates in India's Public Distribution System (Aadhaar e-KYC and biometric authentication failures for ration cards) and in conditional benefit systems such as UK Universal Credit. Homeless adults are disproportionately screened out by SNAP work-reporting rules (NBER w28877).

**Scale and severity.** United States: during the Medicaid 'unwinding' that began April 2023, about 25 million people lost coverage, and roughly 69% of those disenrollments were for procedural reasons (missing paperwork, outdated address, unreturned form), not because the person was found ineligible; about 56 million renewals retained coverage, and state disenrollment rates ranged from about 12% to 57% (KFF Medicaid Enrollment and Unwinding Tracker, 2024, read via a mirror). SNAP: a field experiment with about 65,000 Los Angeles applicants identified the intake interview as a key barrier; applicant-initiated flexible interviews raised approvals by six percentage points, doubled early approvals, and raised long-term participation by over two points (Giannella, Homonoff, Rino, Somerville, NBER w31239, 2023). Recertification: in administrative data roughly half of cases failed recertification, many appeared income-eligible, and about half of failures re-entered soon after, i.e. administrative churn (NBER Reporter, 2024). SNAP work requirements increased exits by 23 percentage points after 18 months with no employment effect (NBER w28877). 2025 shocks compounded this: 42 million people went weeks without SNAP during the government shutdown and the 2025 budget law permanently removed benefits for about 2.5 million (reported by Eviction Lab's 2025 report citing Politico and CBPP). India (secondary source, lower confidence): about 4.39 crore ration cards were deleted 2013 to 2021, with a J-PAL study finding 88% belonged to genuine beneficiaries; 11 lakh cards were cancelled in Jharkhand in 2017; the Right to Food Campaign documents dozens of starvation deaths linked to Aadhaar-related denials; biometric authentication failure rates of 6 to 12% are reported for manual workers. Severity is loss of health coverage or food for eligible people, with measurable downstream rent and utility hardship after benefit exits.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Federal rules already give relief that is underused: 42 CFR 435.919(d) requires states to reconsider eligibility without a new application if the person submits the missing information within 90 days of a procedural termination, but few people know this window exists or how to invoke it. Ex parte (automatic) renewal rates vary widely by state and the USDS Medicaid renewals playbook shows fixes are state-by-state engineering and policy projects measured in years. Notices are mailed to stale addresses and written in dense English; call-center waits are long. Nonprofit navigation has fragile funding: Benefits Data Trust, the field's best-known data-driven outreach nonprofit, filed a WARN notice for 273 workers effective 24 August 2024 and shut down. Code for America's state-hosted application tools (GetCalFresh, MNbenefits/Shiba) focus on the front door (applying), not on surviving renewals and reversing terminations. Appeals and fair hearings are rarely requested because deadlines and procedures are opaque. In India, exclusion is built into the authentication design itself and recourse is informal.

**What people do today.** Reapplying from scratch after termination (which is what produces churn and coverage gaps of weeks to months); going uninsured and using emergency departments; skipping medications; relying on food pantries; repeatedly calling county offices and managed-care plan hotlines; asking clinic front-desk staff, community health workers, or church volunteers to decipher notices; in India, repeated trips to ration shops and enrollment centers, or relying on neighbors' cards. Managed-care organizations and hospitals already run ad hoc outreach to keep members enrolled because they lose revenue when eligible people are dropped.

**Proposed intervention.** A renewal-and-reinstatement engine for navigators, clinics and managed-care plans, with a free individual path. (1) Notice intake: photograph or upload any state notice; parse program, case number, action, and due dates into a tracked timeline (OCR plus templates per state; no conversational AI needed). (2) Jurisdiction rules: a maintained database of each state's renewal windows, verification document lists, interview rules, and the federal 90-day reconsideration right, used to generate a complete renewal packet and document checklist. (3) Proof-of-submission ledger: timestamped records, fax and portal receipts, and mailing confirmations so a 'we never received it' termination can be contested with evidence. (4) Reinstatement automation: on a procedural termination, generate the 90-day reconsideration request, fair-hearing request, and continued-benefits-pending-appeal request with correct deadlines and rule citations, addressed to the correct office. (5) Population view for organizations: which members have renewals due, which were terminated procedurally, aggregated failure reasons benchmarked against the CMS unwinding dataset (data.medicaid.gov datastore API) by state. Novelty is the post-termination focus (reconsideration and hearings as a first-class workflow) and evidentiary submission records; existing tools stop at application. Works without AI.

**Works across countries?.** The failure mode (eligible people losing benefits to process) is global and growing with digital eligibility systems; India's PDS e-KYC, UK Universal Credit journaling and sanctions, and EU social assistance all have deadline, document and appeal structures. The rules content must be rebuilt per country and per state, and some systems lack a formal reconsideration right, so the product travels as a framework with country modules rather than as a single ruleset.

**Sustainable business model.** Buyers whose incentives align with keeping eligible people enrolled: Medicaid managed-care organizations (which lose capitation payments when members are dropped and already fund retention outreach), federally qualified health centers and hospitals (uncompensated care), state and county agencies (reduced churn workload), and community navigator networks. Per-member or per-seat SaaS to organizations; always free to individuals and to small nonprofits. Risk: an MCO could want to prioritize high-revenue members; mitigate by keeping the individual path free and the product eligibility-agnostic. Honest assessment: strong alignment on Medicaid, weaker on SNAP (no comparable paying intermediary), and sales cycles to public agencies are slow.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web application with backend, database, and role-based auth (individual, navigator, organization admin): notice parser with state templates, rules tables for the largest 5 to 10 states, packet and letter generation (PDF), deadline reminders, and a submission ledger. Real public data: the CMS 'Medicaid and CHIP CAA Reporting Metrics' unwinding dataset via the data.medicaid.gov datastore API, published Medicaid and CHIP eligibility levels, USDA SNAP state options reports, state policy manuals and hearing forms, 42 CFR text. Hard parts: no state APIs expose individual case status, so status comes from the person's notices; submissions to state portals remain manual; health data needs HIPAA-grade handling and business associate agreements with MCOs; rules drift constantly and need editorial maintenance. No hardware or pre-use regulatory approval.

**Measurable outcomes.** Procedural termination rate among enrolled members versus county or state baseline from the CMS dataset; share of procedural terminations reversed within the 90-day window; median days of coverage or benefit gap; renewal completion rate by due date; fair-hearing requests filed and outcome rates; for organizations, outreach staff hours per retained member; downstream, self-reported missed medications or food insecurity at 90 days.

**Sources consulted:**

## 45. The identity-document catch-22: people without a fixed address cannot obtain the birth certificate and ID that housing, benefits and work require

*Domain:* housing-justice · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** People experiencing homelessness (745,652 people on a single night in January 2025 in the US, including 266,320 unsheltered and 155,750 chronically homeless individuals, the highest chronic count recorded; HUD 2025 AHAR Part 1 as summarized by the Congressional Research Service, IF12985), people leaving jails and prisons, survivors fleeing domestic violence, disaster-displaced people, and people born out of state or abroad. Globally, an estimated 850 million people lack any official proof of identity (World Bank ID4D Global Dataset, 2023), disproportionately women, rural residents and refugees; national ID registers skew male and urban even where enrollment is large (Nigeria's NIN register was 56% male at October 2025 with 142 million enrolled by September 2026 against a 180 million target).

**Scale and severity.** United States (2024 surveys by the University of Maryland Center for Democracy and Civic Engagement with VoteRiders and Brennan Center, read via a mirror): about 2.6 million voting-age citizens have no government photo ID at all, about 21 million lack a current driver's license, 28.6 million hold an ID without current name or address, and 21.3 million (9.1%) lack ready access to proof of citizenship. A Franklin County, Ohio process audit (August 2026) documents the mechanics for a person with no address, no photo ID and no birth certificate: four separate in-person trips plus a one-to-three-week mail wait in the best typical case; certified birth certificates cost $17 to $28 per copy in Ohio and out-of-state records add two to eight weeks and require a mailing address that will still be valid; the Ohio BMV's accepted address-proof list contains no document a shelter can provide, while nursing-home residents have an accommodation written into the form; and whether a vital-records office will issue a birth certificate to someone with no photo ID at all is unresolved in practice, which is the closed circle. Without ID, people are denied jobs, housing applications, bank accounts, benefits, and in some cases emergency shelter (National Law Center on Homelessness and Poverty, 'Photo Identification Barriers Faced by Homeless Persons'; the only dedicated prevalence survey is from 2004, so current national prevalence among homeless people is not well quantified, which lowers confidence). Globally, lack of ID blocks SIM cards, bank accounts, land registration and benefits for hundreds of millions. Note: figures were read from GitHub-hosted mirrors because the session's network blocked the original hosts.

**Why existing solutions fail.** Fee-waiver laws (e.g., Ohio HB 472, California AB 1733) address the fee, which the Franklin County audit found is not the binding constraint; the address-proof and ID-to-get-ID circles remain. Vital-records offices and DMV branches apply uniform rules non-uniformly, so the 'workable route' (SSA correspondence, benefit statements, probation orders, agency verification letters) exists only if someone reads the acceptable-document list line by line; most people bring a shelter letter, are turned away, and lose the trip. Commercial services such as VitalChek require an existing ID and a card. Nonprofit ID clinics (Homeless ID Project in Phoenix, ID Ministry, county guides) are local, manual, dependent on a caseworker's personal relationships with specific offices, and do not scale across the 50-plus state vital-records regimes a person born elsewhere must navigate. World Bank ID4D programs build national registries but struggle to reach the last mile (registers skewed male and urban; social registers largely unlinked to the ID spine).

**What people do today.** Outreach workers who personally know a clerk; repeated bus trips and days lost to waiting; using a shelter or drop-in center as a mailing address and hoping the envelope arrives before the bed does not; asking relatives in the birth state to request records; carrying photocopies that are then lost or destroyed in sweeps; hospitals holding patients they cannot discharge to housing for lack of documents; in the worst case, buying fraudulent IDs or giving up on benefits and formal work entirely.

**Proposed intervention.** A document-path navigator and request engine built on a maintained, machine-readable rules graph of how to obtain each foundational document in each jurisdiction. (1) Rules graph: for every US state and DC (and local registrars where they set rules), the vital-records request requirements, accepted alternative identity proofs and affidavits, fee-waiver eligibility and forms, homeless-verification letter templates, and DMV acceptable-document lists including the hidden alternative address-proof paths; encoded as data with verification dates and office-level variance notes. (2) Path planner: from the person's birthplace, current documents and situation, compute the shortest feasible sequence (which document first, which office, which proofs), flag closed circles honestly and route them to a caseworker. (3) Packet generation: pre-filled request forms, sworn statements, agency verification letters, fee-waiver applications, money-order instructions, and a designated mailing address at a partner organization, with tracking of each outbound request and expected turnaround. (4) Organization workspace for outreach workers, shelters, hospitals and reentry programs: caseload, status, and a feedback loop that records what each office actually accepted or refused, which keeps the rules graph honest. (5) Secure digital custody of document images for the individual. Novelty is the explicit encoding of the alternative-proof paths and office-level practice, plus a verification loop; it is a data-maintenance product that is useful without AI, and language models are at most an aid to drafting cover letters.

**Works across countries?.** The structure (birth registration, foundational ID, proof of address, and the circular dependencies among them) exists in every country, and the rules-graph approach transfers; the content is entirely local. In the UK the problem is proof-of-address rather than a national ID; in India it is Aadhaar enrollment for people without an introducer and state certificate workflows; for refugees it is UNHCR and host-state documentation. Each is a separate module, and the US module alone is large.

**Sustainable business model.** Organizational SaaS for the institutions that already bear the cost of missing documents: Continuums of Care and shelters (HUD CoC and ESG funds can cover case-management tools), hospital discharge planning and health systems, state corrections and reentry programs, Medicaid managed-care plans, county human services, and legal aid; per-seat or per-case pricing with the individual path free. Possible add-on revenue from partner organizations acting as mailing-address designees. Honest assessment: buyers are public and nonprofit with slow procurement; margins depend on keeping rules maintenance cheap through the office-feedback loop; it will not be venture-scale, but a modest, durable business is plausible.

**MVP feasibility.** Feasible as a web application with backend, database and auth (individual, caseworker, organization): a rules database seeded from public vital-records and DMV pages for all states, a path planner, PDF form generation, request tracking with reminders, and an organization caseload view. Real public data: state vital-records requirements and fee schedules, DMV acceptable-document lists, USPS address validation API, HUD CoC directories, and state fee-waiver statutes. Hard parts: the rules are published but inconsistently applied, so the product needs continuous office-level verification (the Franklin County guide explicitly refuses to promise the no-ID path works until a counter confirms it); in-person requirements and mail cannot be removed by software; document custody carries privacy and identity-theft risk; no hardware or regulatory approval, but adoption depends on frontline organizations entering feedback.

**Measurable outcomes.** Days from intake to state ID in hand; number of in-person trips per completed case; share of cases completed without caseworker escalation; share of office visits that fail on document grounds (should fall as the rules graph improves); out-of-state birth certificate turnaround; downstream, benefits enrollments, housing applications submitted, and employment starts that were previously blocked for lack of ID; for organizations, caseworker hours per completed ID.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/fm58cn6g4d-stack/project-2050/HEAD/library/text/crs-if12985-homelessness-2026.txt
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/UditDewan/Franklin-County-ID-Access-Guide/HEAD/research/BARRIER-MEMO.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/robennals/steelview/HEAD/content/drafts/us-voter-id/candidate-data.md
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/goinvo/civic-engine/HEAD/civic-engine-app/data/methodologies/sources.ts
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bendyline/knowledge/HEAD/catalogs/wikipedia/on-this-day-en/content/articles/19394651.md
