## 46. Drug and medical-device safety recalls never reach the people actually using the product

*Domain:* overlooked / medication and medical device recalls (cross-cutting: health, consumer safety, caregiving, open data) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.55

**Who experiences it.** (1) Patients taking recalled prescription drugs: e.g., the 2018-2019 nitrosamine (NDMA/NDEA) recalls of valsartan/losartan/irbesartan touched tens of millions of blood-pressure prescriptions in the US and EU; most patients learned from news coverage or not at all. (2) Users of home-use medical devices: the June 2021 Philips Respironics CPAP/BiPAP/ventilator recall (foam degradation) covered on the order of 5.5 million US and roughly 15 million worldwide devices; users had to find out and register themselves. (3) Implant recipients (ICDs/pacemaker leads, hip/knee implants, breast implants, insulin pumps, spinal cord stimulators) whose device identity lives on an implant card and in a hospital record, not in any system that is checked against new recalls. (4) Consumers of OTC products (2023 artificial-tears contamination that caused infections, blindness and deaths). (5) Family caregivers managing medication for elderly or disabled relatives, and small independent pharmacies, home-health agencies, DME suppliers and clinics that have no recall-management staff or paid alert subscriptions. Geographically this is worst where no public recall feed exists at all (most low- and middle-income countries) but it is unsolved even in the US, UK, EU, Canada and Australia, which do publish recalls.

**Scale and severity.** RESEARCH CONSTRAINT: web search was unavailable to this agent (shared per-turn budget exhausted) and fda.gov, oig.hhs.gov, cdc.gov and journal hosts are outside this session's allowed network, so the figures below are split into VERIFIED (read in primary source code/docs) and UNVERIFIED (recalled from named reports; must be re-checked). VERIFIED from the FDA's own openFDA codebase: the FDA Recall Enterprise System (RES) publishes structured enforcement records for every drug, device, food and biologic recall since 20 June 2012 via weekly enforcement reports, exposed at api.fda.gov/drug/enforcement and /device/enforcement; a separate /device/recall endpoint carries CDRH device-recall records; /device/udi carries the GUDID device registry; /drug/shortages.json carries the drug-shortage list. Each enforcement record carries classification (Class I/II/III), product_quantity, distribution_pattern, reason_for_recall, code_info (lot/serial text) and a field literally named 'Initial Firm Notification of Consignee or Public' - the notification target is the consignee (distributor, pharmacy, hospital), not the end user. UNVERIFIED (canonical sources named for the verification step): FDA's January 2024 Philips update reported >116,000 medical device reports including 561 reported deaths associated with the recalled devices (Apr 2021-Dec 2023) [fda.gov safety communication on Philips Respironics]; HHS OIG audit A-01-15-00504 (2017) found Medicare paid about $1.5 billion over 2005-2014 for services tied to seven recalled or prematurely failed cardiac devices and recommended device identifiers on claims [oig.hhs.gov]; CDC's 2023 artificial-tears outbreak investigation reported 81 infections, 4 deaths and 14 cases of vision loss [cdc.gov]; FDA posts on the order of several thousand recall records per year across product types, with device recall events in the high hundreds per year and dozens of Class I (most serious) events annually [fda.gov recall database]. Severity: Class I recalls are by definition products with a reasonable probability of serious injury or death; the harm from non-notification is continued exposure, delayed replacement of failing implants and missed monitoring.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) The recall system is legally and operationally built around consignees. VERIFIED: the FDA enforcement schema records how the firm notified 'Consignee or Public' (letter, press release, e-mail, phone); there is no field or process for the end user, and effectiveness checks run down the distribution chain, not to patients. (2) Manufacturers usually do not know who the end user is (drugs dispensed by pharmacies, devices sold via DME suppliers and hospitals), and pharmacies record NDC but rarely lot numbers on the patient's profile, so lot-specific recalls cannot be matched to patients automatically. (3) VERIFIED: FDA device-recall records carry product codes, 510(k)/PMA numbers, brand text and free-text code_info, but no UDI device identifier field, while GUDID records carry identifiers, brand_name, version_or_model_number and product_codes - so matching an implant card to a recall requires a crosswalk that no consumer-facing tool builds. (4) VERIFIED: openFDA's own device-recall pipeline had to fall back to scraping FDA's HTML pages with self-imposed rate limiting because the structured CSV feed stopped being supplied - the public data path itself is fragile and unofficial. (5) Public notification is broadcast (press releases, MedWatch e-mail list, recalls.gov for consumer products, news), not targeted; the signal-to-noise ratio for an individual is near zero. (6) Institutional recall-management services (ECRI Alerts Tracker, RASMAS and similar) are priced and designed for hospital systems, not for a 2-pharmacist store, a home-health agency or a family. (7) Medication-tracking apps focus on adherence; they are not lot-aware and do not ingest device recalls. (8) Outside the US, feeds are national and heterogeneous (UK MHRA alerts on gov.uk, Health Canada recall API, Australia TGA, EU national competent authorities with EUDAMED vigilance delayed), so even multinational manufacturers cannot rely on a single patient channel [EU/UK/CA status unverified in this session].

**What people do today.** Patients hear about recalls from TV or social media and then phone the pharmacy; pharmacists pull shelf stock and, for Class I drug recalls, manually scan dispensing logs for the NDC (not the lot) and call patients; cardiology and orthopedic practices with their own implant logs run ad-hoc chart searches; manufacturers stand up one-off registration websites after a major recall (Philips) and rely on users to find them; caregivers keep paper or spreadsheet lists of medications and device serial numbers; hospitals pay for institutional alert services; many patients simply keep using the recalled product until a clinician happens to mention it.

**Proposed intervention.** A personal and small-provider recall-matching service built on the public recall feeds. Users (patients, family caregivers, independent pharmacies, home-health agencies, clinics, DME suppliers) record what they use: drugs by name (resolved to RxNorm/NDC via the NLM RxNorm API) and devices by brand/model/catalog number or by scanning the UDI on the implant card or device label (resolved via GUDID), optionally with lot/serial numbers. A backend continuously ingests api.fda.gov /drug/enforcement, /device/enforcement, /device/recall, /device/udi and /drug/shortages.json (all verified to exist in FDA's openFDA codebase), normalizes them, builds the two crosswalks the FDA does not publish - (a) NDC/UPC/RxCUI to drug-recall records using the openfda harmonization fields on each enforcement record, and (b) device-recall product_code + brand/model text to GUDID device identifiers - and parses free-text code_info lot/serial ranges into structured matchers. When a new record matches, the user gets a targeted e-mail/SMS/push alert with the FDA classification, the recalling firm's stated action, a plain-language 'what to do', and a one-click path to report a problem to MedWatch. The B2B view inverts it: a pharmacy or agency uploads a dispensing or inventory export and receives the list of affected patients per recall, with an audit log. What is novel: it matches at the end-user and lot level across drugs and devices from one place, whereas the existing system stops at the consignee; it builds and maintains the recall-to-UDI crosswalk that is missing from the public data; and it treats the drug-shortage feed as the same problem (supply information not reaching the person taking the drug). It is fully useful without AI - matching is deterministic; a language model is optional only for parsing messy lot-range text and drafting plain-language summaries.

**Works across countries?.** The core design (user-owned product list matched against a public recall feed, with device identifiers converging on the IMDRF UDI model) transfers to any country that publishes recalls: UK (MHRA alerts and field-safety notices), Canada (Health Canada recall database/API), Australia (TGA), Singapore (HSA), EU member states, Japan, and increasingly India (CDSCO alerts). Honest limits: outside the US the feeds are less structured and often lack machine-readable lot data; the EU has no single API; drug coding differs by country (NDC vs national codes, with ATC/RxNorm as bridges); many LMICs publish nothing, so the product cannot help there until a feed exists. Device UDI is the most portable key globally; drug lot matching is the least.

**Sustainable business model.** Free, complete service for individuals and family caregivers (the public-safety layer must not be gated). Paid per-site subscriptions for independent and small-chain pharmacies, home-health and hospice agencies, long-term-care facilities, DME suppliers, ambulatory surgery centers and clinics that need bulk matching, patient lists per recall, audit logs and documentation for accreditation and state board inspections; these organizations already pay for recall handling in staff time. Possible later buyers: Medicaid managed-care plans and PBMs (member safety outreach) and manufacturers (verified reach for recall effectiveness checks) - the latter only under terms that never let a manufacturer suppress or delay an alert. No sale of user data; health data encrypted; B2B tier under HIPAA business-associate terms. Public-health grants are plausible for the free tier.

**MVP feasibility.** HIGH for a small team as a web application. Stack: web app with auth; Postgres; scheduled ingestion workers hitting api.fda.gov (public, no key needed at low volume, higher limits with a free key) and NLM's RxNorm and AccessGUDID services; nightly diff of new/changed recall records; matching engine; e-mail/SMS notifications. Verified public data: the openFDA endpoints and their field lists (drug/device enforcement, device recall, device UDI, drug shortages). Hard parts, stated honestly: (1) lot/serial ranges live in free text (code_info) with inconsistent formats, so lot-level matching will be partial and needs human review and a confidence score; (2) device recalls carry no UDI, so the recall-to-GUDID crosswalk via product code and brand/model text will have false positives and misses; (3) a direct-to-consumer tool is not a regulated medical device if it only relays regulator information, but B2B use with patient lists is protected health information and needs HIPAA-grade handling; (4) adoption depends on channel partners (pharmacies, patient groups) rather than app-store discovery; (5) openFDA's own pipeline shows FDA source pages change without notice. Note: api.fda.gov and download.open.fda.gov were unreachable from this sandbox's network policy, so endpoint liveness was verified from FDA's published source code, not by querying; confirm from the build environment. No custom hardware, no regulatory approval and no physical logistics are required.

**Measurable outcomes.** Median hours from FDA posting of a Class I/II record to delivery of a matched user alert (target under 24 h); share of Class I drug and device recalls for which at least one lot-level or UDI-level match was produced; number and share of alerted users who confirm they were affected and report a completed action (stopped use, obtained replacement, contacted prescriber) within 14 days; MedWatch reports submitted through the tool; for B2B sites, patients identified per recall and staff hours per recall event before vs after adoption, and findings in state board or accreditation inspections; downstream, matched-cohort comparison of continued dispensing of recalled lots among enrolled vs non-enrolled pharmacies.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/FDA/openfda
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/FDA/openfda/master/README.md
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/tree/master/openfda
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/openfda/device_recall/pipeline.py
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/openfda/res/pipeline.py
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/openfda/res/annotate.py
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/api/faers/api.js
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/schemas/res_mapping.json
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/schemas/devicerecall_schema.json
- https://github.com/FDA/openfda/blob/master/schemas/deviceudi_schema.json

## 47. Public contracting data is published but no one with standing is watching it continuously

*Domain:* overlooked / public procurement corruption and open data gaps (cross-cutting: governance, small business survival, civic participation) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.6

**Who experiences it.** Taxpayers and users of public services wherever contracts are steered or overpriced - most acutely in Latin America, Eastern Europe, sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia, but also inside the EU where competition for public contracts has been falling for a decade. Small and medium enterprises that are shut out by short submission windows, tailored specifications and pre-arranged awards, and which have no market intelligence on which buyers are genuinely competitive. Local journalists, civil-society monitors and supreme audit institutions with one or two analysts and no engineering capacity to run red-flag analytics on millions of records. Procurement regulators who receive data but cannot triage it. Honest buyers who are indistinguishable from bad ones because no public comparison exists.

**Scale and severity.** RESEARCH CONSTRAINT: statistical primary hosts (open-contracting.org, eca.europa.eu, oecd.org, unodc.org) were outside this session's allowed network and web search was unavailable; figures are labelled VERIFIED (read in primary docs) or UNVERIFIED (recalled, to be re-checked). VERIFIED (Open Contracting Data Standard documentation, maintained by the Open Contracting Partnership): governments spend an estimated US$13 trillion per year through contracts, 'yet contracting information is often unavailable for public scrutiny'; the OCDS is implemented by more than 30 governments and more than 50 countries and cities are pursuing open contracting reforms. VERIFIED (OCP's Kingfisher Collect repository): 155 live scraper definitions exist for OCDS publishers across roughly 70 jurisdictions (e.g., Mexico 19, Honduras 8, Peru 7, Tanzania 6, Nigeria 6, Chile 4, Paraguay 4, Georgia 4, Uruguay 4, plus EU/TED via Digiwhist and dozens of single-publisher countries from Albania to Zambia) - i.e., the data exists at scale. VERIFIED (OCP's Cardinal tool): the standard red-flag indicator set is small and well defined (short submission period, single bid received, price close to winning bid, excessive unsuccessful bids, identical bid prices, late bid won, all-but-winner disqualified, lowest bid disqualified, excessive disqualified bids, heterogeneous supplier, heavily discounted bid) and each is grounded in EU anti-fraud, World Bank Integrity Vice Presidency and academic corruption-risk literature. UNVERIFIED: European Court of Auditors Special Report 28/2023 found single-bidding in EU public procurement rose from about 24% of procedures in 2011 to about 42% in 2021 [eca.europa.eu]; OECD Government at a Glance puts public procurement at roughly 12-13% of GDP across OECD members [oecd.org]; OECD and UNODC guidance commonly cite 10-25% of procurement value lost to corruption, which on US$13 trillion implies losses on the order of US$1-3 trillion per year [oecd.org, unodc.org]. Severity: losses translate directly into fewer hospitals, worse roads and failed services, and into business failure for excluded SMEs.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Publication was treated as the goal. OCDS has made data available in dozens of countries, but the standard's own guidance acknowledges that impact only follows 'when it is linked to systemic changes that allow people to use the information'. (2) VERIFIED: the best generic analytic tool, Cardinal, is a command-line program that requires OCDS compiled releases in line-delimited JSON, a settings file and the ability to interpret interquartile fences - an analyst's tool, not something a journalist, auditor or bidder can use. (3) Monitoring platforms that do work (Ukraine's DoZorro, Paraguay's and Colombia's portals, Opentender for Europe) are single-country builds or static research dashboards; nothing continuous and cross-country exists for a user to subscribe to a specific buyer, sector or supplier. (4) Red-flag outputs are long lists without prioritization, comparators or an evidence pack; users cannot act on 'buyer X flagged 400 times'. (5) There is no feedback loop: flags are not tied to inquiries, audits, bid protests or outcomes, so nobody learns which flags matter. (6) Data quality: many publishers omit bid-level data, which disables the strongest indicators; entity resolution across organization identifiers is poor. (7) Government-run monitoring is conflicted; civil-society monitoring is grant-dependent and intermittent.

**What people do today.** Freedom-of-information requests and manual browsing of tender portals; journalists building one-off spreadsheets for an investigation; cross-border reporting consortia pooling analysts; whistleblowers; losing bidders filing protests with review bodies without statistical context; OCP and donor-funded technical assistance that produces a dashboard which decays after the grant; auditors sampling a few hundred contracts a year by hand; SMEs paying for commercial tender-alert services that list opportunities but say nothing about whether competition is real.

**Proposed intervention.** A continuous procurement-watch service on top of open contracting data. The backend ingests OCDS feeds (reusing OCP's Kingfisher scrapers and merge libraries), resolves buyers and suppliers to stable identifiers, computes the standard red-flag indicators (Cardinal's set, callable through its Python package) plus buyer- and supplier-level time series that nobody publishes - single-bid rate, direct-award share, average submission window, contract-amendment growth, supplier concentration, win rate per supplier per buyer - and benchmarks each buyer against peers in the same country and sector. Users subscribe to a buyer, sector, region or supplier and receive 'evidence packs': the specific records, the flag's rationale in plain language, the peer percentile, document links and the procedural steps available (protest, audit request, FOI). Every buyer gets a public report-card page. An outcome layer records what happened after a flag (inquiry opened, award annulled, story published, nothing), so the ranking learns which flags lead anywhere. The second face is bidder market intelligence: which buyers actually run competitive procedures, realistic win rates, and upcoming tenders in the bidder's category. What is novel: subscription-based, cross-country, buyer-level accountability with evidence packs and an outcome loop, rather than one-off national dashboards or analyst CLIs; and pairing the public-integrity use with the SME market-access use that OCP's own user-needs guidance identifies but that no single product serves. It works without AI; a language model is optional for summarizing tender documents.

**Works across countries?.** Strong by construction: OCDS is a cross-country standard and live scrapers exist for roughly 70 jurisdictions (verified), EU TED data is available in OCDS form via Digiwhist, and non-OCDS sources (US USAspending/SAM.gov, UK Contracts Finder, India GeM) can be mapped. Honest limits: indicator quality depends on each publisher's fields (bid-level data is missing in many), legal exposure differs by country (defamation risk means outputs must be framed as risk indicators, never accusations), and in some states public flagging of buyers carries political and personal risk for local users.

**Sustainable business model.** Free public buyer report cards and a free tier for journalists and civil-society monitors (the accountability layer must stay public and un-gated). Revenue from SME bidders paying for market intelligence and competition-aware tender alerts; from supreme audit institutions, procurement regulators and multilateral lenders paying for internal monitoring dashboards and API access; and from philanthropic and multilateral grants (OCP, World Bank, EU, national anti-corruption programs) that already fund one-off versions of this. Guardrail: government customers never get the ability to suppress public flags; contracts are structured so the public layer is independent of any single buyer's revenue.

**MVP feasibility.** HIGH for one to three countries with good feeds. Stack: web app with auth; Postgres with JSONB for OCDS releases; ingestion workers running Kingfisher spiders (verified open source) for, e.g., Paraguay DNCP, Chile ChileCompra, Mexico, Colombia, Honduras, Georgia, Ukraine Prozorro or EU/TED via Digiwhist; OCDS merge to compiled releases; indicator jobs calling the ocdscardinal Python package (verified in the Cardinal repo) plus custom buyer time series; subscription and e-mail delivery; public buyer pages. Hard parts: data volume (millions of releases for large publishers - start with one country), organization entity resolution, heterogeneous field coverage that silently disables indicators, defamation-safe presentation, and sustaining scrapers as portals change. No regulatory approval, no hardware, no logistics. The openFDA and OCP data sources were read in this session from source code and documentation; live feeds were not queried because of the sandbox's network policy and should be exercised from the build environment.

**Measurable outcomes.** Number of flagged processes that result in a formal inquiry, audit, bid protest or annulled award (tracked through the outcome layer); published investigations citing the evidence packs; change in single-bid rate, direct-award share and median submission window for watched buyers versus unwatched peers over 12-24 months; number of distinct SMEs bidding on watched buyers' tenders; median days from publication of a procedure to first flag delivery; precision of flags as judged by auditor feedback.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/open-contracting/standard/blob/1.1/docs/index.md
- https://github.com/open-contracting/standard/blob/1.1/docs/primer/what.md
- https://github.com/open-contracting/standard/blob/1.1/docs/guidance/design/user_needs.md
- https://github.com/open-contracting/standard/blob/1.1/docs/guidance/design.md
- https://github.com/open-contracting/cardinal-rs
- https://github.com/open-contracting/cardinal-rs/tree/main/docs/cli/indicators/R
- https://github.com/open-contracting/kingfisher-collect/tree/main/kingfisher_scrapy/spiders

## 48. People who depend on electricity to stay alive get no warning or help when the power fails

*Domain:* overlooked / energy outages (cross-cutting: disability, caregiving for disabled children and elders, climate and disaster health) · *Researcher confidence:* 0.45

**Who experiences it.** People who rely on powered medical equipment at home - ventilators, oxygen concentrators, CPAP/BiPAP, home dialysis, enteral feeding and infusion pumps, suction, powered wheelchairs and beds, refrigerated insulin and other medicines - and the family caregivers who manage them, including parents of children with complex medical needs discharged home on technology. In the US this population is disproportionately older, lower-income, rural or in disaster-exposed states (Gulf Coast, Puerto Rico, Texas, California wildfire zones, the heat-exposed Southwest). Globally it includes households and small clinics in countries with chronic unscheduled outages (Nigeria, Pakistan, Lebanon, Iraq, Venezuela, parts of India and South Africa) and the very large population served by health facilities without reliable electricity in sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia.

**Scale and severity.** RESEARCH CONSTRAINT: every statistical primary host for this problem (empowerprogram.hhs.gov, eia.gov, who.int, worldbank.org, trackingsdg7.esmap.org) was outside this session's allowed network and web search was unavailable; the only data repository I could check (OWID's dataset repo on GitHub) holds no outage or reliability series. All figures below are therefore UNVERIFIED recollections of named reports and must be re-checked; confidence is lowered accordingly. HHS's emPOWER program states that more than 4.5 million Medicare beneficiaries rely on electricity-dependent durable medical equipment and home health services, and publishes county-level counts on the public emPOWER Map [empowerprogram.hhs.gov]; this excludes Medicaid-only children and adults and the privately insured, so the true US population is larger. Average annual outage hours per US customer have roughly doubled over the past decade, driven by major weather events [EIA, Form 861 reliability data]. Event mortality: Texas officially attributed 246 deaths to Winter Storm Uri (February 2021) [Texas DSHS]; the GWU study estimated about 2,975 excess deaths after Hurricane Maria (2017), with loss of power for oxygen, dialysis and refrigeration repeatedly cited as a mechanism; peer-reviewed modelling (Stone et al., Environmental Science & Technology, 2023) projected that a multi-day blackout during a heat wave in Phoenix would send roughly half the population to emergency departments and cause thousands of deaths. Globally, a 2023 WHO/World Bank/IRENA/SEforALL report estimated close to 1 billion people in low- and middle-income countries are served by health facilities with no or unreliable electricity; Nigeria's national grid suffered about a dozen total collapses in 2024; South Africa had scheduled load-shedding on most days of 2023. Severity: for ventilator and oxygen users an unplanned outage longer than battery runtime is immediately life-threatening; for the wider group it means ER visits, missed dialysis, spoiled insulin and 911 overload.

**Why existing solutions fail.** (1) Utility 'medical baseline' or critical-care registries are opt-in, poorly publicized, differ per utility, mostly affect billing or restoration priority, and give no personal warning or help; enrollment is a small fraction of the eligible population. (2) emPOWER data goes to public-health and emergency-management officials as aggregates; it cannot reach the individual, and officials cannot see who has what battery runtime. (3) Outage maps are per-utility web pages; alerts, where they exist, go to the account holder, not the caregiver, carry no medical context and do not estimate when a specific person's equipment will stop. (4) Emergency plans required of DME suppliers and home-health agencies by CMS are paper exercises with no live outage trigger. (5) 911 and utility phone lines saturate during the events that matter. (6) Backup power is a private purchase that low-income households cannot afford; generator programs are small. (7) In LMICs, outages are unscheduled so information products cannot exist without crowdsourcing - the one counter-example, South Africa's EskomSePush app, succeeded precisely because Eskom published schedules, showing that outage information products are adopted when data exists. (8) Commercial outage aggregators (PowerOutage.us) are B2B data products, not person-level alerting.

**What people do today.** Caregivers buy batteries, inverters or generators out of pocket; call the utility repeatedly; drive to a relative's house, a hospital lobby or the ER to plug in; hospitals and fire stations become informal charging refuges; home-health nurses phone through their patient lists by hand; parents of medically complex children maintain WhatsApp or Facebook groups to share who has power; in LMICs households run small generators or go without and clinics ration fuel; some people simply ride it out and present to hospitals in crisis.

**Proposed intervention.** An electricity-dependence outage companion built around the person, not the meter. A caregiver or patient registers the devices they rely on and each device's battery runtime, their address and utility, and a response circle (family, neighbor, home-health agency, local emergency manager, with explicit consent per contact). The backend continuously monitors the public signals that exist today - utility outage feeds and outage-map endpoints, Public Safety Power Shutoff notices, National Weather Service alerts for heat, cold and storms (api.weather.gov), and FEMA/Red Cross open shelter and cooling-center data - and, when the registrant's area loses power or a shutoff is announced, sends a targeted alert with a runtime countdown ('oxygen concentrator battery ends in 2h40m'), the nearest open shelter or charging location with power, a one-tap 'I need power or transport' message to the circle, and pre-authorized escalation to the local emergency manager or agency if nobody responds. Home-health agencies, DME suppliers and county emergency managers get an aggregated, consent-based view of registrants at risk by runtime, which turns the CMS-mandated emergency plan into a live operation. What is novel: device-runtime-aware, cross-utility, caregiver-centric alerting with a consented path to responders, versus per-utility account alerts and aggregate emPOWER maps; a design that works without AI (rules and feeds) and degrades to SMS. An LMIC variant replaces utility feeds with crowdsourced outage reports by feeder area plus SMS, and a facility variant monitors clinic power via existing smart-plug telemetry (optional hardware, later).

**Works across countries?.** The dependence is universal; the data is not. US, Canada, Australia, much of the EU, the UK and South Africa publish outage or shutoff information that can be ingested; many LMIC utilities publish nothing, so there the product must start from crowdsourced reporting and SMS, which is slower to bootstrap. Shelter and weather-alert data are strong in the US and patchier elsewhere. Privacy law (HIPAA, GDPR) shapes the responder-sharing layer but does not block a consent-based design.

**Sustainable business model.** Free for individuals and family caregivers. Paid for the organizations that already carry the obligation and the cost: home-health, hospice and DME suppliers (CMS emergency-preparedness requirements, per-patient-month pricing), hospital discharge and pediatric complex-care programs, Medicaid managed-care plans and Medicare Advantage plans (avoided ER visits and admissions), utilities (fulfilling medical-registry and PSPS notification duties at lower cost) and county or state emergency-management agencies (consented registrant views). Guardrails: no data sale, consent per recipient, the free individual layer never gated, and no dependency on a single utility customer.

**MVP feasibility.** MEDIUM. The web application itself (auth, registry, device catalog with runtimes, alerting, shelter lookup, responder views) is straightforward with a backend, database and e-mail/SMS provider. The hard part is outage-signal coverage: only some utilities expose stable JSON outage endpoints, many outage maps are vendor widgets that must be scraped and break, and the best aggregator API is commercial; NWS alerts and OpenFEMA shelter data are open and reliable; PSPS notices are available for California utilities. Other honest difficulties: false or late alerts carry liability in a life-safety context (the product must be framed as supplemental and tested with agencies), reaching the population requires channel partners (home-health agencies, children's hospitals, utilities) rather than consumer marketing, and the individual-level emPOWER data is not public (only county aggregates), so the registry must be built opt-in. No regulatory approval and no custom hardware are required for the core; facility power telemetry would need hardware later. Scale figures could not be verified from this sandbox and should be confirmed before ranking.

**Measurable outcomes.** Alert lead time relative to each registrant's battery exhaustion (share of outage events where the alert arrived with more than one hour of runtime remaining); share of registrants with a completed plan and a responding circle member; number of assisted charging, transport or shelter placements per event; outage-related ER visits, 911 calls and unplanned admissions among registrants versus matched non-registrants (using emPOWER county baselines and partner agency records); utility medical-registry enrollment lift where the product is used as the enrollment channel; agency time per event to account for all at-risk patients.

**Sources consulted:**

- https://github.com/owid/owid-datasets
